Part 1 · Platform and Deployment

Architecture, Management and Licensing

Everything later in this guide is configured on top of a platform that is already reachable, licensed and time synchronised. Start here.

Mode

Read the topic, recall what you can, then reveal. Mark what's solid to track what still needs work. Controls act across every page.

Single pass: the packet is inspected once. App-ID, User-ID, Content-ID and threat prevention all operate against that one pass rather than each engine rescanning the traffic.
Parallel processing: networking and security functions run on dedicated hardware at the same time rather than in a chain.
The two planesA firewall splits into a management plane and a data plane.
Management plane: web interface, CLI, logging, configuration, routing protocol daemons, User-ID agent processes.
Data plane: forwarding and inspection. On larger platforms it subdivides into signature match, security processing and network processing.
Why the split matters operationallyA management plane problem (slow GUI, log ingestion lag, commit taking forever) is not the same as a data plane problem (throughput, session capacity, packet loss), and they are diagnosed separately:
show system resources: management plane CPU and memory
show running resource-monitor: data plane CPU per core
show system state filter sw.mprelay.*: plane to plane communication
In practiceA hung or sluggish web interface with traffic still flowing perfectly is the classic management plane symptom. Don't reboot the firewall for it, check management plane CPU first, because a rebooting firewall interrupts traffic that was fine.

The two planes, and which symptom belongs to which the most useful first question in any performance complaint

Defaults: management address 192.168.1.1/24, credentials admin/admin with a forced password change on first login, console 9600 8N1.
The MGT interface is out of band and has its own default gateway, configured at Device › Setup › Management. It does not use the data plane routing table.
Initial CLI configurationconfigure
set deviceconfig system ip-address 10.0.0.5 netmask 255.255.255.0 default-gateway 10.0.0.1
set deviceconfig system dns-setting servers primary 10.0.0.53
set deviceconfig system hostname fw-edge-01 timezone Europe/London
set deviceconfig system ntp-servers primary-ntp-server ntp-server-address 10.0.0.123
commit
Service routes at Device › Setup › Services send specific services (updates, DNS, syslog, LDAP, WildFire, Palo Alto Networks Services) out a data interface instead of MGT.
Get NTP and DNS right at this stage. Certificate validation and log correlation both depend on accurate time, and a firewall with bad time produces decryption failures that look like configuration errors.
In practice"The firewall routes production traffic fine but can't download content updates" is MGT's separate gateway. Fix it with a service route, not a static route.
The base platform gives App-ID, User-ID, the policy engine and networking. Everything else is a subscription:
Threat Prevention → antivirus, antispyware, vulnerability protection
Advanced Threat Prevention → adds inline ML based detection
URL Filtering (PAN-DB) → web categories
WildFire / Advanced WildFire → sandbox analysis and rapid signatures
DNS Security → cloud DNS lookups and sinkhole intelligence
GlobalProtect → needed for HIP checks and clientless VPN, not for a plain VPN
SD-WAN, IoT Security, Enterprise DLP, AIOps as further options
Support is separate from subscriptions. Without a valid support licence you cannot download PAN-OS software, even while content updates keep arriving.
request license info · request license fetch · show system info
In practiceThe diagnostic tell: content updates still arriving but a PAN-OS upgrade refusing to download means support has lapsed, not the subscriptions. Two different renewals, two different failure modes.

✓ Check yourself architecture and management

  1. The GUI is crawling but traffic is unaffected. Which plane, and which command?
    Management plane. show system resources. Don't reboot, you'd interrupt traffic that was fine.
  2. Which command shows data plane CPU per core?
    show running resource-monitor.
  3. Why can a firewall route perfectly and still fail to reach the update server?
    The MGT interface has its own default gateway and does not use the data plane routing table. Fix with a service route.
  4. Which two settings must be right before deploying certificates, and why?
    NTP and DNS. Certificate validation and log correlation depend on accurate time; bad time looks like a config error.
  5. Content updates arrive but PAN-OS won't download. What has expired?
    Support, not the subscriptions. No support licence, no software download.
  6. Does a basic site to site style remote access VPN need the GlobalProtect subscription?
    No: the subscription is needed for HIP checks and clientless VPN, not plain connectivity.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Defaults: 192.168.1.1/24, admin/admin, console 9600 8N1.
  • MGT has its own gateway, separate from the data plane.
  • Management plane = GUI, CLI, logging, routing daemons, User-ID.
  • Data plane = forwarding, signature match, security and network processing.
  • No support licence, no software download.
  • GlobalProtect subscription is for HIP and clientless, not basic VPN.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Rebooting for a slow GUI and interrupting healthy traffic.
  • Assuming MGT uses the data plane routing table.
  • Deploying certificates before NTP is correct.
  • Confusing support expiry with subscription expiry.
  • Buying GlobalProtect licences for a deployment that doesn't need them.
SymptomPlaneCommandDo not
Slow web UI, slow commitManagementshow system resourcesReboot reflexively
Log ingestion lagManagementshow log-collector-es-cluster healthBlame throughput
Throughput below line rateDatashow running resource-monitorCheck the GUI first
Session table fullDatashow session infoRaise timeouts blindly

Part 1 · Platform and Deployment

The Deployment Options

The foundation for everything else in this part, every orchestration decision that follows operates on a form factor you have chosen.

Mode
The physical appliance, with dedicated hardware for each plane and the highest throughput per unit. Ranges run from small branch models through mid range campus units to chassis based data centre platforms.
What differs by model: throughput (App-ID, Threat Prevention and IPsec figures are quoted separately, and the Threat Prevention number is the realistic one), maximum sessions, new sessions per second, maximum security rules, maximum VSYS, and whether the platform supports multi VSYS at all.
Smaller models have a single combined plane, so a management plane spike affects traffic in a way it would not on a larger platform. That is worth knowing before sizing a branch.
Hardware features on larger units: redundant power supplies, hot swappable fans, dedicated HA ports, and a log card slot on some chassis.
show system info for model and serial, show system environmentals for power, fans and temperature.
In practiceSize on the Threat Prevention throughput figure, not the App-ID one. App-ID numbers assume no threat inspection, which is not how anyone runs a firewall in production.
The virtual machine form factor, running the same PAN-OS on a hypervisor or in a public cloud.
Private cloud: VMware ESXi and NSX-T, KVM, Hyper-V, Nutanix, OpenStack.
Public cloud: AWS, Azure, GCP, Oracle Cloud, Alibaba Cloud.
Licensing is either by fixed model (VM-50 through VM-700, sized by vCPU and memory) or by flexible / credit based licensing, where you allocate credits to instances and choose vCPU count and which subscriptions to enable. Flexible licensing is the current model and suits autoscaling.
Bootstrapping lets an instance configure itself at first boot from init-cfg.txt and optionally bootstrap.xml, so a new firewall comes up already licensed and connected to Panorama with no manual step. Covered in full in the cloud and orchestration part.
Performance depends on the hypervisor: SR-IOV or DPDK give far better throughput than a standard virtual NIC, and CPU pinning matters.
In practiceVM-Series in a cloud is not the same as Cloud NGFW. With VM-Series you own the instance, patch PAN-OS, and size the vCPUs. That distinction answers most "which form factor" questions.

The five deployment options all run the same PAN-OS and policy model: what differs is who owns the instance

CN-Series · the containerised firewallRuns inside Kubernetes as two pod types:
CN-MGMT: the management plane, deployed as a StatefulSet, holding configuration and logs.
CN-NGFW: the data plane, deployed as a DaemonSet so there is one per node, inspecting pod traffic.
Its purpose is east west traffic between containers, which a perimeter firewall never sees. Policy can use Kubernetes metadata (namespaces, labels, service names) rather than pod IPs, which matter because pod IPs are ephemeral. It is managed from Panorama with the Kubernetes plugin.
Cloud NGFW · the managed servicecloud provider managed NGFW in AWS and Azure. You subscribe through the provider's marketplace, attach it to a VPC or VNet, and manage only policy: there is no instance to size, patch or scale.
Policy is managed either through the cloud provider's console or through Panorama using Cloud Device Groups. Availability and scaling are the provider's responsibility.
In practiceCN-Series is the answer when the requirement names pod to pod or container traffic. Cloud NGFW is the answer when the requirement stresses no operational overhead or no instances to manage.
Choosing the right deployment option comes down to matching a requirement to a form factor.
Physical site, high throughput, hardware control → PA-Series.
Virtualised data centre, or IaaS where you want full control → VM-Series.
Traffic between containers in a cluster → CN-Series.
Cloud native with minimum operational overhead → Cloud NGFW.
AI applications, models, data and agents → AI Runtime Security.
Mixed estates are normal, and all of them are manageable from Panorama, which is why the automation domain pairs deployment with centralised management.
Sizing considerations that cut across all of them: throughput with threat inspection enabled, session count, whether you need multi VSYS, and whether the deployment must autoscale, which pushes you toward VM-Series with flexible licensing or Cloud NGFW.
In practiceRead the requirement for the operational ownership signal. "We have no platform team" points at Cloud NGFW. "We need SR-IOV performance and custom sizing" points at VM-Series. The throughput number alone rarely decides it.

✓ Check yourself deployment options

  1. Which throughput figure should you size on, and why?
    Threat Prevention throughput. The App-ID figure assumes no threat inspection, which is not how anyone runs production.
  2. Name the two CN-Series pod types and their Kubernetes workload types.
    CN-MGMT as a StatefulSet (management plane), CN-NGFW as a DaemonSet, one per node (data plane).
  3. Why does CN-Series policy use Kubernetes metadata rather than IPs?
    Pod IPs are ephemeral. Namespaces, labels and service names are stable identifiers.
  4. VM-Series in AWS versus Cloud NGFW in AWS: what actually differs?
    Who owns the instance. VM-Series: you size, patch and scale it. Cloud NGFW: you manage policy only.
  5. The requirement stresses no platform team and no instances to patch. Which option?
    Cloud NGFW. The operational ownership signal decides it, not throughput.
  6. Which two form factors suit autoscaling, and what licensing supports it?
    VM-Series with flexible / credit based licensing, and Cloud NGFW, which scales by itself.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Five options: PA-Series, VM-Series, CN-Series, Cloud NGFW, AI Runtime Security.
  • CN-Series = CN-MGMT StatefulSet + CN-NGFW DaemonSet.
  • CN-Series policy uses Kubernetes metadata, not pod IPs.
  • Cloud NGFW is provider managed, policy via console or Cloud Device Groups in Panorama.
  • VM-Series licensing: fixed model or flexible credits.
  • SR-IOV / DPDK and CPU pinning drive VM-Series performance.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Sizing on App-ID throughput instead of Threat Prevention.
  • Treating VM-Series in cloud as equivalent to Cloud NGFW.
  • Expecting a perimeter firewall to see pod to pod traffic.
  • Forgetting small PA models may share a single combined plane.
  • Writing container policy against pod IPs.
Requirement signalOptionYou manageManaged from Panorama?
Physical site, max throughputPA-SeriesEverythingYes
Hypervisor or IaaS, full controlVM-SeriesPAN-OS and the instanceYes
Pod to pod, east west in a clusterCN-SeriesPods and PAN-OSYes, with the K8s plugin
No operational overheadCloud NGFWPolicy onlyYes, Cloud Device Groups
AI apps, models, data, agentsAI Runtime SecurityPolicyVia the AIRS platform

Part 1 · Platform and Deployment

PAN-OS 12.1 Orion · Quantum Readiness

Orion's headline theme is quantum safety, which is a strong signal of how recent this material is. Three topics below slow down for the threat, the standards, and the legal mandate behind them, before the tooling.

Mode
Palo Alto Networks' name for this whole capability area is Quantum Resistant Encryption (QRE): it's the umbrella term covering the threat itself, the algorithms and standards answering it, and Orion's specific tooling to deploy it. This topic and the next cover why QRE exists and what Orion does about it; the two after go deeper into the threat and the standards behind it.
The driver is harvest now, decrypt later. Traffic captured today can be stored and decrypted once the cryptography protecting it becomes breakable. So a VPN tunnel carrying data with a long confidentiality life is exposed by a future capability, not a present one.
That is why standards bodies moved before the capability existed. NIST standardised the replacement algorithms, and the ones to know by name are ML-KEM for key encapsulation, plus ML-DSA and SLH-DSA for signatures.
Two migration problems Orion addressesYou do not know what you are using. Cryptographic usage across an estate is largely invisible, so you cannot plan a migration.
Some things cannot be upgraded. Legacy applications may never support quantum safe ciphers natively.
Orion's answers are a Quantum Readiness assessment and dashboard for the first problem and cipher translation for the second.
This is recognition and configuration awareness, not algorithm internals. Know why you would enable it, and where the setting sits relative to conventional IPsec parameters.
In practiceThe question to be able to answer is "which tunnels should be quantum safe first", and the answer is those carrying data that must stay confidential for years, not the ones carrying the most traffic.

The Orion quantum toolkit assess what you have, upgrade what you can, translate what you cannot

Quantum Readiness Dashboard gives a real time inventory of cryptographic assets, validates them for compliance against post quantum standards, and offers inline remediation, so the assessment and the fix are in the same place. This, plus cipher translation below, is Orion's actual delivery of QRE.
Its value is planning. A PQC migration is a multi year exercise, and you cannot sequence it without knowing which tunnels, certificates and cipher suites are in use and which are already compliant.
Cipher translation handles the applications that cannot be upgraded. The firewall terminates and reestablishes with quantum safe cryptography, so a legacy application becomes quantum safe without any change to the application itself.
Alongside the software, Orion introduced quantum optimised hardware: 14 new fifth generation NGFW models engineered for high performance processing of post quantum cryptography, including a PA-5500 Series for data centres and a ruggedised PA-455R-5G with native 5G for industrial environments.
In practiceThe pairing is the point. The dashboard tells you what to migrate and in what order; cipher translation covers the things that will never migrate themselves. Neither alone solves the problem.
Topic 8 said this is recognition and configuration awareness, not algorithm internals. This topic and the next one are the internals, taken slowly enough to actually stick, so the dashboard and cipher translation stop being a black box.
What a quantum computer actually isQuantum computers (QCs) represent a new supercomputing platform based on the laws of quantum mechanics, not a faster version of a normal computer:
  • Quantum algorithms: solve problems that classical computers structurally cannot handle.
  • Qubits: unlike a classical bit, always either 0 or 1, a qubit can exist in simultaneous states.
  • Entanglement: qubits can be linked to other qubits, correlating their states.
  • The result: a QC can sift through enormous amounts of data and spot subtle patterns that classical algorithms simply miss.
Why migration is genuinely hard, not just theoretically necessaryFour separate obstacles stack up together:
  • Time consuming: the transitions themselves take real time.
  • Expensive: the work carries a real cost.
  • Workforce gap, not enough people who actually understand post quantum cryptography.
  • Early adoption reduces risk: waiting doesn't make any of the other three easier.
The clock, stated as four numbersNIST frames the migration timeline directly:
  • Prep and planning: about a year.
  • Migration itself: a completion goal of two to five years.
  • Time data needs to stay secure once migrated: roughly eight years.
  • CRQC: a Cryptographically Relevant Quantum Computer, one actually capable of breaking today's encryption, currently estimated five to fifteen years away.
Run those four numbers forward from today, and an organisation that waits for more certainty on the fifteen year end of that range has already missed the one year prep window and most of the migration window too.
Harvest now, decrypt lateris what actually collapses "years away" into "start now." Harvesting attacks are already happening, traffic is being captured and stored today, encrypted, waiting for the capability to decrypt it to arrive. Anything with a long confidentiality lifetime that's been captured since roughly 2023 is already sitting in that exposure window, whether or not the CRQC that eventually breaks it exists yet.
Two issues, and only one is covered herePalo Alto Networks frames the immediate remediation problem as two separate issues:
  • Data harvesting attacks: the harvest now decrypt later problem above.
  • Unsanctioned post quantum ciphers: a bad actor already using an available, unapproved PQC to hide traffic on your network today. This one, and the standards that answer both issues, is the next topic.
In practiceIf asked why this is urgent rather than a five to fifteen year problem, the answer is harvest now, decrypt later: the attack already happened for anything captured today, the decryption is just deferred. The prep and planning year should already be running.
What PQC actually isPost quantum cryptography (PQC) is the essential advancement in encryption built specifically to resist quantum attacks. Together, these are the guidelines for implementing quantum resistant algorithms so data stays secure through the migration described in the previous topic:
  • FIPS 203, FIPS 204, FIPS 205: three NIST approved encryption standards.
  • RFC 8784, RFC 9242, RFC 9370: address the same threat inside existing protocols like IKEv2.
Shor's algorithm, breaks asymmetric encryption outrightShor's algorithm is a method of factoring complex numbers, including finding the large prime numbers that asymmetric encryption, and by extension PKI, is built on. Shor's doesn't need to brute force a private key: it derives the key directly, based on the large primes used to decrypt messages. That's why asymmetric encryption needs entirely new maths, not a longer key, a longer key built on the same factoring problem is still solved by the same algorithm.
Grover's algorithm, weakens symmetric encryption, doesn't break itGrover's algorithm targets symmetric encryption instead, and does something structurally different from Shor's: it gives a quadratic speed up on brute force key search, which halves the effective strength of a symmetric key rather than defeating it outright. A 256 bit key halved by Grover's still leaves 128 bits of real security; a 128 bit key halved does not. That single fact is why AES-256 is the going forward recommendation over AES-128: doubling the key length is a sufficient defence against Grover's in a way it can never be against Shor's.
FIPS 203, general encryptionFIPS 203 is the primary standard for general encryption:
  • Small encryption keys: comparatively small, so two parties can exchange them easily.
  • Speed of operation.
  • Based on CRYSTALS-Kyber, renamed ML-KEM: Module-Lattice-Based Key Encapsulation Mechanism.
FIPS 204, the primary signature standardFIPS 204 is the primary standard for protecting digital signatures:
  • Based on CRYSTALS-Dilithium, renamed ML-DSA: Module-Lattice-Based Digital Signature Algorithm.
FIPS 205, the deliberate backup, on different mathsFIPS 205 is also for digital signatures:
  • Based on Sphincs+, renamed SLH-DSA: Stateless Hash Based Digital Signature Algorithm.
  • Built on a different mathematical approach entirely from ML-DSA.
  • Exists specifically as a backup in case ML-DSA is later found to be vulnerable: a deliberate hedge, not duplication.
The RFCs, carrying all of this over IKEv2
  • RFC 8784: combines a post quantum pre shared key (PPK) with IKEv2, protecting specifically against Shor's algorithm.
  • RFC 9242: defines the IKE_INTERMEDIATE exchange.
  • RFC 9370: multiple key exchanges (hybrid keys); its extra post quantum key material actually needs 9242's exchange to have somewhere to go.
Together, all three RFCs mark significant progress in strengthening the security of digital communication and data protection.
Catching the other issue from the previous topic: unsanctioned PQCUnsanctioned PQCs means there are open source and other PQC implementations freely available, and a bad actor using one can potentially hide on a network, obscuring a secure shell (SSH) session inside traffic that looks compliant, for example. Palo Alto Networks NGFWs can recognise the behaviour of a PQC even when it's an unapproved or open source implementation, which is exactly what makes it possible to find and stop. It's imperative to be able to identify whether someone is using an unauthorised PQC inside your network at all.
Enhanced Decryption Policies and Threat Signaturesare the mechanism: they prevent unknown threats hidden by post quantum cryptography in SSL. The three things this buys, named directly:
  • Traceability: you can see it happening.
  • Control: you can act on it.
  • Protection: the session doesn't get a free pass just because it's encrypted with something new.
In practiceKeep the two algorithms straight in one line: Shor's derives the asymmetric key outright; Grover's only halves symmetric strength. That's why PKI needs three entirely new FIPS standards while AES just needs a longer key, and why FIPS 205 exists on purpose, as insurance against FIPS 204 rather than a second copy of it.
Everything so far has been the technical answer. This topic is the regulatory and organisational pressure sitting behind it, why a migration plan isn't optional for a lot of organisations, and what actually doing one looks like.
Y2Q, the governance clock started on a specific dateY2Q (Years to Quantum) is framed as having started ticking on 13 August 2024. It deliberately echoes Y2K: a fixed, dated point after which the remaining runway to migrate is shrinking on a known schedule, not an open ended someday.
The National Quantum Initiativeis the federal source and gateway to quantum R&D across the US government, the umbrella programme that the legal requirements below sit under.
The actual legal mandateThe Quantum Computing Cybersecurity Preparedness Act, passed July 2022, is where "someday" became a dated requirement:
  • Within a year of NIST announcing its new PQC standards, the Director of OMB must issue guidance.
  • That guidance requires each executive agency to develop a migration plan, including interim benchmarks, to move the agency's IT to post quantum cryptography.
This is what turns Part 1's QRE material from "a good idea" into "a plan with benchmarks a federal agency is legally required to produce."
Mapping to DoD Zero TrustPalo Alto positions QRE directly against the DoD's own Zero Trust principles, three pillars at a time:
  • Consolidate: use existing NGFW platform capability, centrally managed. No new box to deploy for this.
  • Integrate: open standards, an integrated platform, modular PQC deployment rather than a rip and replace.
  • Accelerate: leverage the investment already made; simple to deploy, change and evolve; a phased approach rather than one cutover.
How an organisation actually becomes quantum ready, five stepsThis is a genuine operating cycle, not a one time project, sourced from A guide to a quantum safe organization (QED-C, December 2021):
  • 1 · Assign resources and build awareness: designate a team to manage the transition, build an internal budget, and engage vendors. Reading this guide is literally step 1 in motion.
  • 2 · Define responsibilities: break responsibilities out by use case. This is named directly as a huge undertaking: an organisation cannot become quantum safe in one swoop.
  • 3 · Develop an inventory and priority list: review every device, system, platform and vendor for three things: how long it needs to stay secure, the lifetime of the asset (will it even still be around in five years?), and its susceptibility to attack.
  • 4 · Evaluate solutions, experiment, and test: evaluate how PQC, QKD and other quantum technologies will secure the organisation's assets. This can include layering PQC on existing RSA architecture, the same additive pattern as RFC 8784 and RFC 9370 on the VPN page, while preparing for future implementations.
  • 5 · Continue, monitor progress: review, monitor and refine policies, and develop relationships with vendors and experts to track advancements in quantum safe solutions and quantum computing itself. The cycle feeds back into step 1 rather than ending.
In practiceThe five step cycle and the Consolidate/Integrate/Accelerate framing describe the same migration from two angles: one is the organisational process, the other is how Palo Alto's own platform maps onto it. Step 3's inventory question, how long does this asset need to stay secure, is exactly the question the Quantum Readiness Dashboard from the earlier topics answers.

✓ Check yourself the quantum threat, PQC and governance

  1. What does QRE stand for, and what does it cover?
    Quantum Resistant Encryption: Palo Alto Networks' umbrella term for the threat, the standards answering it, and Orion's tooling to deploy it.
  2. What does QC stand for, and what makes a qubit different from a classical bit?
    Quantum Computer. A qubit can exist in simultaneous states and link to other qubits through entanglement: a classical bit is always just 0 or 1.
  3. Explain harvest now, decrypt later in one sentence.
    Traffic captured today is stored and decrypted once the cryptography becomes breakable, so long lived confidentiality is the exposure, not current traffic volume.
  4. Name the four numbers in NIST's migration timeline.
    ~1 year prep and planning, 2 to 5 years migration, ~8 years data needs to stay secure, inside an overall 5 to 15 year threat window.
  5. What does CRQC stand for?
    Cryptographically Relevant Quantum Computer: one actually capable of breaking today's encryption, currently estimated 5 to 15 years away.
  6. What does PQC stand for, and what is it for?
    Post Quantum Cryptography: encryption built specifically to resist quantum attacks.
  7. What's the difference between what Shor's algorithm does and what Grover's algorithm does?
    Shor's factors large primes and derives an asymmetric private key directly. Grover's only halves the effective strength of a symmetric key. It weakens, it doesn't break outright.
  8. Name the algorithm and full spelled out name behind FIPS 203, 204 and 205.
    203: CRYSTALS-Kyber → ML-KEM (Module-Lattice-Based Key Encapsulation Mechanism). 204: CRYSTALS-Dilithium → ML-DSA (Module-Lattice-Based Digital Signature Algorithm). 205: Sphincs+ → SLH-DSA (Stateless Hash Based Digital Signature Algorithm).
  9. Which FIPS standard exists purely as a backup, and why?
    FIPS 205: built on different maths from FIPS 204, specifically in case ML-DSA is later found vulnerable.
  10. What does RFC 8784 specifically defend against, and what does RFC 9242 actually enable?
    RFC 8784's post quantum PSK defends specifically against Shor's algorithm. RFC 9242 defines the IKE_INTERMEDIATE exchange that RFC 9370's multiple key exchanges need to carry larger PQ key material.
  11. How does a firewall catch someone using an unsanctioned PQC to hide a session?
    It recognises the behaviour of PQC in the traffic itself, via Enhanced Decryption Policies and Threat Signatures, even from an unapproved implementation.
  12. What does the Quantum Readiness Dashboard give you, and why does it matter?
    A real time inventory of cryptographic assets, compliance validation and inline remediation. You cannot sequence a multi year migration without it.
  13. What date is Y2Q framed as having started, and what does the Quantum Computing Cybersecurity Preparedness Act actually require?
    13 August 2024. Within a year of NIST's PQC standards, OMB must issue guidance requiring every executive agency to produce a migration plan with interim benchmarks.
  14. Name the three DoD Zero Trust pillars QRE is mapped against.
    Consolidate, Integrate, Accelerate.
  15. What are the five steps to becoming quantum ready, in order?
    Assign resources and build awareness → define responsibilities → develop an inventory and priority list → evaluate, experiment and test → continue and monitor progress.

✓ Anchor facts

  • QRE (Quantum Resistant Encryption) is Palo Alto's umbrella term for the threat, the standards, and Orion's tooling together.
  • Quantum computers (QCs) use qubits (simultaneous states) and entanglement, not just faster classical bits.
  • Migration clock: ~1yr prep, 2 to 5yr migrate, ~8yr data stays secure, inside a 5 to 15yr threat window.
  • CRQC = Cryptographically Relevant Quantum Computer, one actually capable of breaking today's encryption.
  • The driver is harvest now, decrypt later: already happening, not a future risk.
  • PQC = Post Quantum Cryptography, resisting quantum attacks specifically.
  • Shor's derives the asymmetric key outright; Grover's only halves symmetric key strength.
  • FIPS 203 = ML-KEM/Kyber (encryption). FIPS 204 = ML-DSA/Dilithium (signatures). FIPS 205 = SLH-DSA/Sphincs+ (backup signatures, different maths).
  • RFC 8784 defends against Shor's. RFC 9242 carries what RFC 9370 needs to send.
  • Quantum Readiness Dashboard = inventory, compliance, inline remediation. Cipher translation upgrades apps that cannot upgrade themselves.
  • Y2Q started 13 August 2024. The Preparedness Act requires OMB guidance and agency migration plans within a year of NIST's PQC standards.
  • DoD Zero Trust mapping: Consolidate, Integrate, Accelerate.
  • Five step readiness cycle: assign → define → inventory → evaluate → monitor, then repeat.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Studying the algorithm internals as the main goal. Recognition and configuration awareness is the level Orion's tooling operates at, the maths is background, not the test.
  • Prioritising quantum safe migration by traffic volume rather than confidentiality lifetime.
  • Treating "PQC" and "quantum safe" as vague synonyms rather than PQC being the specific, named standard set (FIPS 203/204/205 plus the RFCs).
  • Assuming FIPS 205 is redundant with FIPS 204. It's a deliberate hedge on different maths, not duplication.
  • Forgetting RFC 9242 exists at all. It's easy to only remember 8784 and 9370, but 9370 depends on 9242's exchange to work.
  • Thinking cipher translation changes the application. It does not.
  • Treating passwordless as a replacement for auth profiles and MFA rather than an addition.

FIPS 205 (SLH-DSA)Backup digital signaturesSphincs+, different maths from 204

Part 1 · Platform and Deployment

PAN-OS 12.1 Orion · Passwordless Authentication

A password is a single, reusable, stealable piece of information. Passwordless removes it from the equation entirely rather than trying to protect it better, the threat, the architecture, and how to actually build and troubleshoot it.

Mode
Why this exists at allCredential compromise is the number one attack vector in public breaches, and passwords account for 49 percent of publicised cyber attacks in recent years. Strong authentication and MFA are still recommended, but the course is direct about their limit: these measures are no longer sufficient on their own.
The reason a password is uniquely weak: it's a single, reusable, often weak piece of information, not something tied to a physical device. Once stolen, it hands an attacker the literal keys to an account, and it's easy to steal and exploit at scale in a way a device bound credential isn't.
Four ways attackers bypass strong authentication anyway
  • Man in the Middle (MiTM): intercepting the authentication exchange itself.
  • SIM swapping: hijacking the phone number an SMS or call based factor relies on.
  • MFA fatigue: bombarding a user with approval prompts until one is accepted by exhaustion or accident.
  • Stolen session cookies: taking the already authenticated session token, sidestepping the login step entirely.
Three tangible outcomes, stated directlyevery improvement in authentication reduces the available attack surface, because authentication is the primary gatekeeper for every protected resource behind it:
  • Improve security posture: prevents commonly exploited identity based attacks: credential compromise, MFA bypass, MFA fatigue, SIM swapping.
  • Simplify the end user's experience: one biometric scan (fingerprint, face, or retinal), with no MFA prompt needed on top.
  • Reduce the password management burden: removes constant password resets and refreshes, which cost IT organisations millions of dollars a year in OPEX.
The standards it's actually built ona passwordless future has to rely on rigorous, open industry standards, so devices, browsers and platforms all authenticate securely and consistently:
  • FIDO2 (Fast Identity Online 2), the modern open authentication standard, described directly as secure, easy to use, and phishing resistant. This is the standard the whole architecture, covered on the next topic, is primarily built on.
  • Passkey, CTAP, and WebAuthn: the other named open standard components working alongside FIDO2.
Licensingis acquired and enabled the same way as other PAN-OS NGFW options. Worth knowing as a matter of principle: the standards based components of passwordless authentication remain open and accessible to the public: the license covers Palo Alto's own implementation, not the underlying open standards themselves.
In practiceIf asked why passwordless is worth the migration effort, the cleanest answer is the one sentence version: a password is information that can be stolen at scale, and every one of the four bypass methods above exploits that fact in a different way, passwordless removes the shared secret entirely rather than trying to protect it better.
The architecture, primarily based on FIDO2, is built to replace a vulnerable shared password with secure public key cryptography. The core move is decentralising authentication: the secret, the private key: moves from a server's database to the user's own device, which is what makes the whole model immune to server side data breaches and phishing: there's no central password database to steal, and nothing to phish because there's nothing the user types.
Three layers, one workflow
  • User device layer: where the private key actually lives, and never leaves.
  • Authentication server layer: the firewall itself, holding Kerberos Constrained Delegation (KCD).
  • Service application layer: the app the user is actually trying to reach.
The five step flow, worth having in order1 the user is authenticated to the firewall. 2 the user requests a service from the target app. 3 the firewall impersonates the user to retrieve a ticket for that app, against the Kerberos server, using KCD. 4 the firewall inserts a SPNEGO token into the HTTP header and sends the request on to the app. 5 the user gets the service back from the app, with no password ever entered for that application.
Three supported use casesPAN-OS now provides comprehensive passwordless support across genuinely different scenarios:
  • IdP managed applications: a central IdP (Okta, Microsoft Entra ID, Ping Identity) becomes the single trusted authority, verifying identity with a passkey or other passwordless method once, then granting access to every federated app behind it without reauthentication.
  • Kerberos applications: the mechanism the Orion release actually introduces: users reach Kerberos protected network resources with no password entered at all, which is exactly what makes it highly resistant to phishing and credential theft. There's no password to steal in the first place.
  • Legacy and custom applications: the use case worth remembering specifically for apps that cannot be modified: implementing a modern identity solution as a secure gateway in front of the app gives it a robust security shield without touching existing code at all. The legacy app itself never needs its own password or SSO capability, PAN-OS proxies access on its behalf.
In practiceThe legacy app use case is the one worth having ready for a design conversation: it's the answer whenever a scenario describes an application that's too old or too fragile to modify but still needs modern, phishing resistant authentication in front of it.
Configuring passwordless means two things together: enabling modern authentication standards inside the organisation's IdP, and guiding users through a one time registration of their secure authenticator. The goal throughout is shifting identity verification from something the user knows (a password) to something they have or are (biometrics, a physical key).
Step one: the Kerberos Server Profilethis gives the NGFW the IP address of the Kerberos server, and the firewall's policy uses it when requesting constrained delegation on the user's behalf: the exact mechanism from the previous topic's five step flow. The profile holds a name, an Administrator Use Only option, and a servers table (name, Kerberos server address, port, port 88 is the Kerberos standard).
CLI troubleshooting starts with authda core OS service that acts as the central broker for every user authentication request and exposes runtime counters from the moment it starts. Troubleshooting begins by retrieving its statistics.
MP commandsthe management plane is responsible for learning user identities after they authenticate passwordlessly, then distributing that information to the data plane. The commands worth knowing by name: show session ID, check the connection to identity sources, and show authentication delegation.
DP commandsrun on the data plane to verify the firewall actually learned the user's identity and correctly mapped it to their IP address:
  • User to IP address mappings: show user ip-user-mapping all, displaying the table mapping users to their IP addresses.
  • View live traffic sessions.
  • Check user group mappings.
  • Debugging on the data plane.
Why logs matter more here than elsewherepasswordless logs are described as indispensable: they give a definitive, step by step record of authentication events, clearly identify the point of failure, and precisely state the reason, turning an ambiguous problem into a specific, solvable one, rather than leaving the administrator guessing which of the five workflow steps actually failed.
Three log sources, each answering a different question
  • System logs: the definitive chronological record of every event on the box; crucial for troubleshooting failures, investigating security incidents, and auditing activity generally.
  • Authd.log, even when tickets are cached: authd logs every local authorisation and session event, not only network authentication, so it still shows output when a Kerberos ticket is simply being served from cache rather than freshly requested.
  • Authd.log generally: its main value is a low level, OS focused perspective, revealing hardware interactions and local policy failures that are invisible to browser or server side logs entirely.
In practiceWhen a passwordless failure doesn't show up anywhere a browser or the target application logs, that's exactly when authd.log is worth checking. It sees local, OS level events the rest of the stack simply has no visibility into.

✓ Check yourself passwordless authentication

  1. What percentage of publicised cyber attacks do passwords account for?
    49 percent, with credential compromise the number one attack vector.
  2. Name all four ways attackers bypass strong authentication, covered here.
    Man in the Middle, SIM swapping, MFA fatigue, and stolen session cookies.
  3. Which open standard is the architecture primarily built on?
    FIDO2: secure, easy to use, and phishing resistant.
  4. Where does the private key live, and why does that make the model phishing resistant?
    On the user's own device, and it never leaves: there's nothing typed, so nothing to phish.
  5. Walk through the five step flow for a Kerberos protected app.
    User authenticates to the firewall → requests the app → firewall impersonates the user via KCD to get a ticket → firewall inserts a SPNEGO token into the HTTP header → user gets the response.
  6. Which use case is the answer for an application that cannot be modified?
    Legacy and custom applications: PAN-OS proxies access as a secure gateway in front of it, no code changes needed.
  7. What does the Kerberos Server Profile actually give the firewall?
    The IP address of the Kerberos server, used when requesting constrained delegation on the user's behalf.
  8. Which command shows the user to IP address mapping table?
    show user ip-user-mapping all, run on the data plane.
  9. Why does authd.log still show output even when a Kerberos ticket is served from cache?
    Authd logs every local authorisation and session event, not only network authentication.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Passwords: 49% of publicised attacks; credential compromise is attack vector #1.
  • Four bypass methods: MiTM, SIM swapping, MFA fatigue, stolen session cookies.
  • Architecture is built on FIDO2; the private key never leaves the user's device.
  • Five step flow ends with the firewall inserting a SPNEGO token via KCD.
  • Three use cases: IdP managed, Kerberos apps, legacy/custom apps (no code changes needed).
  • Kerberos Server Profile carries the server IP; standard port is 88.
  • show user ip-user-mapping all is the DP command to know.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Assuming MFA alone is sufficient, the course is explicit that it no longer is on its own.
  • Thinking a legacy app needs code changes to gain passwordless protection. It doesn't.
  • Confusing s4u2self (ticket for the KCD agent itself) with s4u2proxy (ticket for the target app).
  • Overlooking authd.log in favour of only checking browser or server side logs.
  • Forgetting that authd.log logs local events too, not just network authentication.
TermDefinition
Data plane (DP)High speed engine that processes all network traffic through the firewall
Management plane (MP)The interface administrators use to configure, monitor and manage the device
Kerberos Constrained Delegation (KCD)Lets a service impersonate a user to access specific authorised backend services on their behalf, limiting which services an app can reach while acting as the user
KCDReqA request to authd to create a SPNEGO token to access a Kerberos protected application
s4u2selfKerberos protocol to retrieve the ticket for the KCD agent itself, on behalf of the user
s4u2proxyKerberos protocol to retrieve the ticket for the target Kerberos application, on behalf of the user
pan_comThe PAN-OS communication infrastructure the MP uses to send data to the DP
SPNEGO tokenA message packet carrying authentication data, letting client and server securely negotiate the auth protocol with no manual credential entry

Part 1 · Platform and Deployment

PAN-OS 12.1 Orion · Scale, Fabrics and Lifecycle

The rest of Orion: what got bigger, the two fabrics, and the lifecycle changes that matter most if you run firewalls at scale.

Mode
Orion is positioned as optimised for cloud, scaling policy and identity enforcement for large, dynamic deployments.
Up to 10 million user contexts, described as a 20x increase over previous generations. That is the headline identity scale figure, and it exists so User-ID and user based policy remain usable in the largest environments.
Enhanced session handling: session scale improvements for better performance and resilience under heavy load.
High speed payload analysis pipelines between the data planes and cloud analysis engines, using a real time payload exchange. Beyond raw speed, this enables cross session correlation: detecting activity that hides across multiple sessions, which is how advanced persistent threats and staged data exfiltration evade per session inspection.
Also in the release: decryption enhancements for performance and coverage of encrypted traffic inspection, and expanded Device-ID capabilities.
In practiceCross session correlation is the conceptually interesting one. Everything earlier in this guide inspects a session; this looks across sessions, which is a different detection model and the answer to threats deliberately split up to stay under per session thresholds.
Hyperscale Security Fabric (HSF) brings clustering for VM-Series, so multiple software firewalls act as one logical high capacity system rather than as individually sized instances. This is the answer to throughput requirements that exceed a single VM.
Multicloud Security Fabric addresses the operational problem of security spread across several clouds. Software Firewall, Cloud Firewall and Prisma AIRS instances are deployed automatically, cloud infrastructure is configured to steer traffic, and a secure multi cloud networking mesh is established. Scaling and load balancing are native, removing the need for separate load balancers.
Alongside it, Cloud and AI Risk Assessment continuously evaluates every cloud and AI asset for risks from weak or missing protection, highlighting where controls are needed.
Both fabrics reappear in the cloud and orchestration part, because that is where you deploy them. Here the point is what problem each solves: HSF is about capacity, Multicloud Security Fabric is about consistency and reach across clouds.
In practiceKeep the two apart in your head. If the requirement is "more throughput than one VM-Series can deliver", that is HSF. If it is "consistent policy and traffic steering across AWS, Azure and GCP without hand built plumbing", that is the Multicloud Security Fabric.

Orion at a glance five themes, and which part of this guide each connects to

Orion's operational theme is removing the manual work of running a large estate, and it lands mostly through Panorama.
PAN-OS patch deploymentInstead of a full image upgrade for every fix, you can deploy lightweight hot, warm or cold patches. These target critical vulnerabilities with minimal disruption, which changes the calculus of responding to a CVE, you no longer need a full upgrade window for a single fix.
HA upgrade orchestrationHistorically upgrading an HA pair meant a manual sequence: upgrade the passive unit, fail over, watch for stability, then upgrade the former active. Panorama now automates that orchestration from the UI.
Also: intelligent health checks before and after deployment, plugin dependency identification for upgrades, and optimised UI performance for large scale deployments.
The three pillars stated for the release are frictionless upgrades, operational simplification and optimised user experience.
In practiceIf you run a large estate, patch deployment and HA orchestration are the features that change your week. The HA upgrade sequence is still worth knowing manually, Part 16 covers it, because you need to understand what the orchestration is doing before you trust it.
This is the architecture underneath everything else Orion adds: engineered for greater agility and scale, accommodating both traditional on box management and modern API driven automation, so operational consistency holds whether you're managing one appliance or a global deployment.
Why it matters for Strata Cloud Manager specificallythe modernised architecture is foundational to deep SCM integration: it streams richer, near real time telemetry for AIOps, and provides the hooks for faster, more granular policy pushes from the cloud. Together, that's what enables genuine zero touch provisioning and consistent, AI driven policy management across the entire enterprise security fabric.
The commit process, rearchitectedthe change is a genuine structural one, worth holding as a before/after:
  • Legacy commit: the entire configuration was parsed, validated, and applied in one single sequential process. The administrator waited for all of it to finish, and a failure at any stage could mean a full rollback.
  • Modernised commit: validation is separated from application. The configuration is validated first, for syntactical and logical correctness; only then are the changes applied to the management plane and dataplane processes, often in parallel, which is what actually cuts the total time down.
  • The operational benefit, stated directly: commit duration drops from minutes to seconds for many common changes. That lets administrators push security policy with real agility and confidence, even during production hours, shrinking how much change work has to be crammed into a dedicated maintenance window.
New visibility down to the individual processeffective troubleshooting needs deep visibility into what's actually running, and the modernised architecture adds a genuine new suite of diagnostic tooling for exactly that: enhanced CLI commands and API endpoints exposing far more granular data points than before, giving both administrators and support engineers real insight into system health and traffic processing logic rather than a black box.
Per process telemetry, specificallyevery major software process inside the modernised PAN-OS now exposes its own detailed telemetry, CPU and memory consumption, thread counts, and other key performance indicators, reachable through the same new CLI commands and API calls. The practical payoff: a process experiencing genuine resource contention can be identified precisely, rather than inferred from the whole system's aggregate load.
In practiceThe decoupled commit process is the detail worth having ready if asked what changed architecturally in Orion: validation happening before, and separately from, application to the management and data planes is exactly what turns a multi minute commit into a multi second one.
Orion is positioned as a platform shift rather than a point release, with the stated theme of securing the quantum, multicloud and AI era, which maps neatly onto the three things this guide covers that older material does not: post quantum crypto, the cloud fabrics, and AI security.
For study purposes, the useful framing is which Orion feature connects to which part of the syllabus:
Quantum → tunnels and IPsec configuration.
Passwordless and 10M user contexts → authentication and identity.
Decryption enhancements → certificates and decryption.
HSF and Multicloud Security Fabric → cloud and orchestration.
Patches and HA orchestration → Panorama and software updates.
Worth remembering that Orion appears twice in the Software Firewalls course, once as the AI Hyperscale Security Fabric and once as the Multi Cloud Security Fabric, so the fabrics get their configuration detail there rather than here.
In practiceTreat Orion as a lens rather than a chapter. Almost every feature is an enhancement to something covered elsewhere in this guide, so learning it as "what changed in the thing I already know" sticks better than learning it as a feature list.

✓ Check yourself Orion scale, fabrics and lifecycle

  1. What is the headline identity scale figure, and why does it exist?
    10 million user contexts, a 20x increase, so User-ID and user based policy stay usable in the largest environments.
  2. What does cross session correlation detect that per session inspection cannot?
    Activity deliberately split across sessions: APTs and staged data exfiltration hiding under per session thresholds.
  3. Distinguish the two Orion fabrics in one line each.
    HSF = capacity, clustering VM-Series into one logical system. Multicloud Security Fabric = consistency and traffic steering across clouds.
  4. What changes about responding to a critical CVE?
    Hot, warm or cold patches instead of a full image upgrade, minimal disruption, no full upgrade window.
  5. What did HA upgrade orchestration replace?
    The manual sequence: upgrade passive, fail over, monitor, upgrade the former active. Panorama now automates it.
  6. Which three eras does Orion position itself against?
    Quantum, multicloud and AI, which maps to tunnels, the cloud fabrics, and Prisma AIRS.

✓ Anchor facts

  • 10 million user contexts, a 20x increase.
  • Cross session correlation catches threats split across sessions.
  • HSF = VM-Series clustering for capacity.
  • Multicloud Security Fabric = auto deploy, traffic steering, native scaling and LB.
  • Patches come as hot, warm or cold.
  • Panorama automates HA pair upgrade orchestration.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Confusing the two fabrics, one is capacity, one is reach.
  • Assuming every fix now needs a full image upgrade.
  • Trusting HA orchestration without knowing the manual sequence.
  • Treating Orion as a separate subject rather than enhancements to existing features.

Part 2 · Prisma AIRS

The Platform and the Two Intercepts

AI Runtime Security is a fifth deployment option alongside the four covered so far, and Prisma AIRS is the platform it belongs to. The shape to learn: what it protects, and the two ways it intercepts.

Mode
Prisma AIRS, AI Runtime Security: is a purpose built, centralised AI security platform covering the entire AI ecosystem. The four things it protects are worth memorising as a set, because the whole platform is organised around them:
AI applications · AI models · AI data · AI agents
It is described as securing the entire AI attack lifecycle, across three layers: in the code, at runtime, and in the cloud. That framing explains why the platform has both a development time component and an inline one.
The five capabilitiesAI Runtime Firewall: inline network protection for AI traffic (Network Intercept).
AI Runtime API: API level protection embedded in application code (API Intercept).
AI Model Security: protects model artifacts and training pipelines.
AI Red Teaming: automated adversarial testing.
Posture Management: continuous compliance and risk monitoring.
It also secures the surrounding workloads: AI and traditional applications on VMs, containers or cloud native platforms, Kubernetes clusters, and all network traffic in the cloud environment, inbound, outbound and east west.
In practiceHold onto the four protected things and the five capabilities. Almost every question about AIRS resolves to "which of those four is at risk, and which of the five capabilities addresses it".

The two intercepts the single most important distinction on this page

Prisma AIRS AI Runtime: Network Intercept is an inline security intercept providing real time, AI powered network protection. It monitors your cloud network architecture for AI specific and conventional network attacks, so it covers both the new threat class and the ordinary one.
Prisma AIRS AI Runtime: API Intercept secures your AI model, applications, data and agents by embedding Security as Code directly into your source code. The APIs scan prompts and model responses to identify threats programmatically and return actionable recommendations. There is an official Python SDK for integration.
Choosing between themNetwork Intercept when you cannot modify the application, a third party AI tool, or traffic you only see on the wire.
API Intercept when you own the code and need per prompt, per response decisions inside the application flow.
They are complementary, not alternatives.
The same architecture point as the firewall itself applies: Network Intercept is inline and transparent to the app; API Intercept requires a code change but gets far richer context about what the application is actually doing.
In practiceThis is the AIRS equivalent of the tap versus inline decision. Network Intercept needs no application change and sees traffic; API Intercept needs a code change and sees intent.
The named threat classes are the most examinable detail, because they define what the platform actually stops:
Prompt injection: malicious prompt manipulation that subverts model intent or leaks information. The classic example is a jailbreak instructing the model to adopt an unrestricted persona.
Malicious code: code generated or carried through the model.
Malicious URL: links in prompts or responses.
Sensitive data loss, with a mask sensitive data option as an alternative to blocking.
Database security attack: the model being used to reach a backing store.
Toxic content: filtered across several categories.
Resource overload: the AI equivalent of a denial of service.
Hallucination attacks and contextual grounding: checking a response is grounded in its source material.
AI agent threats: covered on the next page.
Custom topic guardrails: your own subject boundaries.
Secure MCP: protection for Model Context Protocol exposure.
In practiceGroup them to remember them: input threats (prompt injection, malicious URL), output threats (sensitive data loss, toxic content, hallucination), availability (resource overload), and agent and tooling threats (agent threats, MCP).

✓ Check yourself the AIRS platform

  1. What does AIRS stand for?
    AI Runtime Security.
  2. Name the four things Prisma AIRS protects.
    AI applications, AI models, AI data, AI agents. The whole platform is organised around this set.
  3. Name the five capabilities.
    AI Runtime Firewall, AI Runtime API, AI Model Security, AI Red Teaming, Posture Management.
  4. Distinguish the two intercepts in one line.
    Network Intercept sits in the traffic path; API Intercept sits in the application code.
  5. You cannot modify a third party AI tool. Which intercept?
    Network Intercept: inline, no application change needed.
  6. What does contextual grounding check?
    That a response is grounded in its source material: the defence against hallucination attacks.
  7. Which three layers does AIRS secure across?
    In the code, at runtime, and in the cloud.

✓ Anchor facts

  • AIRS = AI Runtime Security.
  • Protects AI applications, models, data and agents.
  • Five capabilities: Runtime Firewall, Runtime API, Model Security, Red Teaming, Posture Management.
  • Network Intercept = inline. API Intercept = Security as Code in your source.
  • API Intercept scans prompts and responses and returns recommendations. Python SDK available.
  • Covers AI specific and conventional network attacks.
  • Three layers: code, runtime, cloud.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Treating the two intercepts as alternatives. They are complementary.
  • Assuming AIRS only handles AI specific threats. It covers conventional ones too.
  • Forgetting agents are a protected category in their own right.
  • Thinking API Intercept is a proxy. It is embedded in your code.

7

Part 2 · Prisma AIRS

Model Security, Agents, Red Teaming and Posture

The remaining capabilities, plus the Discover, Protect, Defend arc the course is built around. Agent security is the newest material and the part most worth attention.

Mode
Protects models throughout their lifecycle, from development to deployment, so this is the "in the code" layer rather than the runtime one.
What it does: comprehensive model scanning, detecting vulnerabilities, preventing model poisoning, and ensuring integrity from training through deployment. It also covers policy enforcement and compliance for models.
Ongoing controls include continuous monitoring of model behaviour, automated vulnerability scanning, and policy based access controls over model assets.
The on premises scanning optionModels can be scanned entirely on premises, so sensitive weights and training data never leave your control. That matters for intellectual property and for regulated environments where model artifacts cannot be sent to a cloud service.
The threat this addresses is distinct from runtime: a poisoned or tampered model is compromised before it ever serves a prompt, so no amount of prompt inspection would catch it.
In practiceThe distinction to hold: Model Security protects the artifact; Runtime Security protects the conversation. A poisoned model passes every runtime check because the malicious behaviour is the model, not the traffic.
Agents differ from chat applications because they take actions and use tools, which gives them a genuinely new attack surface. AIRS secures agents including those built on no code and low code platforms, where the builder may not be a developer at all.
The three named agentic threatsIdentity impersonation: an agent acting as someone it is not.
Memory manipulation: tampering with the agent's retained context so it behaves differently later.
Tool misuse: the agent's legitimate tools being turned to unintended ends.
Learn those three as a set. They map to the three things an agent has that a chatbot does not: an identity, persistent memory, and tools.
The governance controls follow from that: agent discovery across cloud, SaaS and endpoints, agent identities with role based access control and audit trails, and secure MCP for the Model Context Protocol through which agents reach tools.
The scale argument for central governance: hundreds of agents deployed without it becomes ungovernable, because nobody knows what exists or what it can reach.
In practiceMemory manipulation is the one that is easiest to underestimate. It is a persistence attack, poison the memory once and the agent misbehaves on future interactions, with nothing malicious in the later traffic to detect.

Discover, Protect, Defend the arc the course follows, and where each capability lands

Automated adversarial testing of your AI applications and models, the platform runs red team simulations against them rather than waiting for a real attacker.
Positioned as a comprehensive security assessment, it belongs in the Protect phase: you test before and during deployment, so weaknesses are found by you rather than in production.
The value is coverage and repeatability. Manual prompt injection testing finds a handful of techniques; automated red teaming exercises a large library of them consistently, and can be rerun after every model or prompt change.
This is the AI equivalent of vulnerability scanning, and it pairs with Posture Management the same way scanning pairs with configuration compliance, one tests behaviour, the other assesses configuration and risk.
In practiceThe operational argument is regression. Every change to a system prompt, a model version or a tool definition can reintroduce a weakness, so testing has to be automated and repeatable rather than a one off exercise before launch.
AI Posture Management continuously evaluates security posture, vulnerabilities and compliance across all AI assets, with automated risk scoring. It is the ongoing assessment layer rather than an inline control.
Discovery is its necessary partner: complete visibility into every AI application, model and agent across the organisation, whether sanctioned or shadow AI. You cannot assess or protect assets you do not know exist, and shadow AI is the realistic case, teams adopt AI tools without telling security.
Discovery spans cloud, SaaS and endpoints, which is what makes it capable of finding unsanctioned usage rather than only the deployments you built deliberately.
How the pieces fitDiscovery finds the assets → Posture Management scores their risk and compliance → Model Security and Red Teaming harden them → Runtime Security defends them in production. Each stage depends on the one before it.
Deployment is primarily SaaS via the Palo Alto Cloud Platform, and it integrates with the wider Prisma platform including CNAPP.
In practiceShadow AI is the phrase that makes discovery meaningful. The assets most likely to be unprotected are precisely the ones security does not know about, which is why discovery comes first in the arc rather than being an afterthought.

✓ Check yourself model security, agents, posture

  1. Why would runtime inspection not catch a poisoned model?
    The malicious behaviour is the model, not the traffic. Model Security protects the artifact; Runtime protects the conversation.
  2. Why can models be scanned on premises?
    So sensitive weights and training data never leave your control: IP protection and regulated environments.
  3. Name the three agentic threats and what they map to.
    Identity impersonation, memory manipulation, tool misuse: mapping to an agent's identity, memory and tools.
  4. Why is memory manipulation easy to underestimate?
    It is a persistence attack, poison once, and later interactions misbehave with nothing malicious in that traffic.
  5. What makes red teaming an ongoing need rather than a launch task?
    Regression: every change to a prompt, model version or tool can reintroduce a weakness.
  6. Why does discovery come first in the arc?
    You cannot assess or protect what you don't know exists, and shadow AI is exactly what security hasn't been told about.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Model Security = lifecycle, scanning, poisoning prevention, integrity. On prem scanning available.
  • Agentic threats: identity impersonation, memory manipulation, tool misuse.
  • Agent governance: discovery, agent identities with RBAC and audit, secure MCP.
  • Red Teaming = automated adversarial simulation, in the Protect phase.
  • Posture Management = continuous risk, vulnerability and compliance scoring.
  • Discovery covers sanctioned and shadow AI across cloud, SaaS and endpoints.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Expecting runtime controls to catch a poisoned model.
  • Treating agents as chat applications. They have identity, memory and tools.
  • Running red teaming once at launch instead of after every change.
  • Assuming discovery only finds deployments you built deliberately.
  • Confusing Posture Management (assessment) with Runtime Security (inline enforcement).

Part 3 · Interfaces and Zones

Interface Modes

Interfaces and zones are the foundation, and every device setting is configured on top of an interface that already works. Learn what each mode can and cannot do before anything else.

Mode

Read the topic, recall what you can, then reveal. Mark what's solid to track what still needs work. Controls act across every page.

Tap: receives a copy of traffic from a SPAN or mirror port. Full App-ID, User-ID and threat visibility, but cannot block anything because it is not in the path. Used for evaluation and for baselining before going inline.
Virtual Wire: two interfaces bound together so traffic passes between them transparently. No IP, no MAC, no routing, no ARP participation. Invisible to the surrounding network, so you can insert the firewall without readdressing anything. It can enforce policy, NAT and inspection.
Layer 2: interfaces are assigned to a VLAN object and the firewall switches between them while inspecting. Add a VLAN interface to provide routing into that broadcast domain.
Layer 3: interfaces have IP addresses and the firewall routes. The most common production mode. Requires assignment to a virtual router or logical router.
Tunnel: a logical interface with no physical port, used as the endpoint for IPSec and GRE. Assigned to a zone and a router like any other interface.
HA: dedicated interfaces for HA1 control and HA2 state sync.
Log Card and Decrypt Mirror: specialised roles on supporting platforms.
Interfaces are configured at Network › Interfaces › Ethernet. Verify with show interface all, show interface ethernet1/1, and show interface hardware for physical state.
In practiceChanging an interface mode clears its configuration, so decide the mode before building on it. If a requirement says "insert a firewall without changing the IP design", the answer is virtual wire. If it says "inspect without any risk of blocking", it's tap.

Choosing a mode how much of the network design may change, and do you need to block?

In Layer 2 mode the firewall behaves like a switch that inspects. The VLAN object at Network › VLANs is the container: it lists the Layer 2 interfaces in that broadcast domain and optionally references a VLAN interface.
The VLAN interface is a logical Layer 3 interface (named vlan.1 and so on) that gives the VLAN an IP address so the firewall can route into and out of it. It is assigned to a zone and a router like any other Layer 3 interface.
The firewall maintains a MAC address table and an ARP table in this mode. Verify with show mac all, show arp all, and show vlan all.
Traffic within the VLAN is switched, and because those interfaces are usually in the same zone it hits the intrazone-default allow rule. Traffic leaving the VLAN goes via the VLAN interface and is evaluated as normal interzone traffic.
In practiceLayer 2 mode without a VLAN interface gives you inspection but no gateway, hosts still point at their existing router. Adding the VLAN interface is what lets the firewall become the gateway.
A Layer 3 interface takes an IP address statically, by DHCP client, or by PPPoE, and supports multiple addresses on one interface. IPv6 is configured alongside IPv4 on the same interface.
Required assignments: a security zone, a virtual or logical router, and optionally a Virtual System if multi-vsys is enabled.
Under the Advanced tab: link speed, duplex and link state (auto or forced), MTU, adjust TCP MSS with an IPv4 and IPv6 adjustment value, untagged subinterface, an Interface Management Profile, and ARP entries or ND entries.
Adjust TCP MSS matters whenever traffic crosses a tunnel. It clamps the MSS so packets do not need fragmenting later. LLDP is configured per interface via an LLDP profile.
Verify with show interface ethernet1/1, show arp all, and show lldp neighbors all.
In practiceForcing speed and duplex on one side while the other autonegotiates causes duplex mismatch, which shows as throughput far below the link rate with rising errors, not as a down interface.

✓ Check yourself interface modes

  1. Which interface mode gives full visibility but cannot block traffic?
    Tap. It receives a mirrored copy and is not in the forwarding path.
  2. Name the four things virtual wire does not have.
    No IP address, MAC address, routing participation or ARP. It is transparent to the surrounding network.
  3. What two objects does Layer 2 mode need before it can route out of the VLAN?
    A VLAN object to hold the interfaces, and a VLAN interface (vlan.1) to give it an IP, zone and router.
  4. What happens to an interface's configuration when you change its mode?
    It is cleared: zone and router assignment are lost. Decide the mode before building on the interface.
  5. Which setting clamps TCP segment size so packets do not need fragmenting across a tunnel?
    Adjust TCP MSS, on the interface's Advanced tab, with separate IPv4 and IPv6 adjustment values.
  6. Which two commands would you use to confirm physical versus logical interface state?
    show interface hardware for physical state, show interface all or show interface ethernet1/1 for logical.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Tap sees but cannot block. Virtual wire is inline and invisible.
  • Virtual wire has no IP, no MAC, no routing, no ARP.
  • Layer 2 needs a VLAN object; add a VLAN interface to route.
  • Layer 3 and tunnel interfaces need a virtual or logical router.
  • Every interface needs a zone before it passes traffic.
  • Layer 3 addressing: static, DHCP client or PPPoE, multiple addresses allowed.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Expecting a tap interface to block. It is out of path.
  • Building on an interface then changing its mode, config is wiped.
  • Layer 2 without a VLAN interface: inspection but no gateway.
  • Forcing speed/duplex on one side only → mismatch, not a down link.
  • Forgetting MSS adjustment on interfaces feeding tunnels.
ModeIP address?Routes?Can block?Needs
TapNoNoNoZone; a mirror source
Virtual WireNoNoYesZone; a virtual wire object
Layer 2Via VLAN interfaceVia VLAN interfaceYesZone; VLAN object
Layer 3YesYesYesZone; virtual/logical router

Part 3 · Interfaces and Zones

Aggregate Ethernet, Subinterfaces, Loopbacks and Management

Aggregate Ethernet is frequently paired with virtual wire in scenarios about inserting a firewall without redesigning the network.

Mode
An Aggregate Ethernet (AE) group bundles physical interfaces into one logical interface named ae1, giving both more bandwidth and link redundancy.
Order of configurationYou must create the AE interface first, set its interface type (Layer 3, Layer 2, virtual wire) and its zone and router, and only then assign physical interfaces as members. PAN-OS will not let you point a member at an AE group that does not exist.
The zone and IP address go on the AE interface, never on the physical members. Members contribute only capacity.
Members must matchInterface speed and duplex, and they should be the same media type. Mismatched members will not join the bundle.
LACPOptional, without it the bundle is static. When enabled you set:
Mode: active (initiates) or passive (responds only).
Transmission rate: fast (1 second) or slow (30 seconds).
Max ports and system priority (lower wins the election).
Fast failover for subsecond recovery.
LACP in an HA pairPre negotiation lets the passive firewall negotiate LACP in advance so the bundle is already up at the moment of failover, cutting recovery time significantly. Available in active/passive HA.
Verify with show lacp aggregate-ethernet all, show interface ae1, and show lacp counters.
In practiceTwo rules save most AE faults: build the AE interface before the members, and put the zone on the bundle. If one side runs LACP passive and the other is static "on", the bundle never forms, at least one side must actively negotiate.

Aggregate Ethernet: what goes where and why pre negotiation matters in an HA pair

Subinterfaces carry 802.1Q tags and are named ethernet1/1.100 or ae1.100. The suffix is an arbitrary identifier and the VLAN tag is configured separately: they do not have to match, though keeping them identical is good discipline.
Available on Layer 3, Layer 2 and virtual wire interfaces, and on AE groups. Each subinterface takes its own zone, router and IP, which is how one physical link serves several segments with different policy.
An untagged subinterface option lets the parent handle untagged frames while subinterfaces handle tagged ones.
Loopback interfaces are logical and always up. Used for the GlobalProtect gateway address, router IDs, DNS proxy, and as a stable management target.
Tunnel interfaces are the same idea for VPN endpoints, no physical port, assigned to a zone and router.
In practiceSubinterfaces on a virtual wire are how you apply different policy to different VLANs crossing the same cable, which is a common transparent insertion requirement.
The MGT interface is out of band. It has its own IP, netmask and default gateway configured at Device › Setup › Management, and it does not use the data plane routing table. Permitted IP addresses and services are set there too.
Service routes at Device › Setup › Services › Service Route Configuration let you send specific services (updates, DNS, syslog, LDAP, WildFire) out a data interface instead of MGT. This is how a firewall with no MGT internet path still reaches the update server.
By default a data plane interface answers nothing, no ping, no SSH, no HTTPS. An Interface Management Profile at Network › Network Profiles › Interface Mgmt selectively enables Ping, Telnet, SSH, HTTP, HTTPS, SNMP, Response Pages, User-ID, User-ID Syslog Listener, and is attached on the interface's Advanced tab.
The profile also takes a permitted IP list, which is the recommended control rather than relying on a security rule.
In practiceTwo classic faults. "I can't ping the inside interface". There is no management profile attached, not a routing problem. And "the firewall routes fine but can't download updates", that is MGT's separate gateway, fixed with a service route. Never enable HTTP or Telnet, and never attach a management profile to an untrusted facing interface.

✓ Check yourself aggregate, subinterfaces, management

  1. In what order must you configure an AE group, and where does the zone go?
    Create ae1 and set its type first, assign zone, IP and router to ae1, then add members. Members carry no zone or IP.
  2. Two firewalls: one LACP passive, one static "on". Does the bundle form?
    No. At least one side must actively negotiate. Passive responds but never initiates.
  3. What does LACP pre negotiation do, and in which HA mode?
    In active/passive, the passive firewall negotiates LACP in advance so the bundle is already up at failover, cutting recovery time.
  4. Is the subinterface number the same as its VLAN tag?
    No: they are configured separately. Keeping them identical is discipline, not a requirement.
  5. The firewall routes traffic fine but cannot reach the update server. Most likely cause?
    MGT has its own default gateway and no path out. Fix with a service route sending updates via a data interface.
  6. Why does a ping to the firewall's inside interface fail even with a permit rule?
    No Interface Management Profile is attached. Data interfaces answer nothing by default.

✓ Anchor facts

  • AE interface first, members second. Zone and IP on the bundle.
  • AE members must match speed and duplex.
  • LACP: active initiates, passive responds; rate fast 1s or slow 30s.
  • Pre negotiation speeds HA failover on LACP bundles.
  • Subinterface suffix ≠ VLAN tag; each takes its own zone, router and IP.
  • MGT has its own gateway. Service routes move services onto data interfaces.
  • Data interfaces answer nothing without an Interface Management Profile.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Assigning a zone to AE members instead of the AE interface.
  • Trying to add members before the AE interface exists.
  • Passive + passive, or passive + static, and expecting a bundle.
  • Assuming MGT uses the data plane routing table.
  • Enabling HTTP or Telnet in a management profile.

Aggregate Ethernetae1Yes, on the bundleBandwidth and link redundancy
