Part 3 · Interfaces and Zones

Virtual Wire, Zones and Zone Protection

Virtual wire earns its own page because its behaviour is least like a conventional interface. Zones then close the foundation, since policy is written between zones, never between interfaces.

Mode
A virtual wire object at Network › Virtual Wires binds exactly two interfaces. Traffic arriving on one is inspected and passed out the other. The firewall does not participate in ARP, does not appear as a hop, and needs no IP addressing.
Tag AllowedControls which 802.1Q VLAN tags may pass. 0 means untagged. Left at the default it passes untagged traffic only, which is the most common reason tagged traffic is silently dropped on a new virtual wire.
Multicast FirewallingOff by default, meaning multicast passes without security policy evaluation. Enable it to apply policy to multicast.
Link State Pass ThroughWhen enabled, losing link on one side brings down the partner interface too. This matters because the devices either side rely on link state to detect failure, without it they keep sending into a black hole and upstream failover never triggers.
Virtual wire subinterfacesSubdivide a wire by VLAN tag, or by VLAN tag plus IP classifier, so one physical pair carries several logical wires in different zones with different policy.
Supported: security policy, NAT, App-ID, User-ID, Content-ID, decryption, QoS.
Not supported: routing, VPN termination, DHCP, GlobalProtect, and it cannot host management services.
Verify with show interface all and show session all filter ... to confirm traffic is traversing the wire.
In practiceTwo settings account for most virtual wire faults: Tag Allowed not listing the VLANs in use, and link state pass through left disabled so neighbour failover never fires. Check both before looking at policy.

Zones, and the two default rules you never see in the rulebase policy is between zones, and an interface belongs to exactly one

A zone is a logical grouping of interfaces of the same type, and security policy is written between zones, never between raw interfaces.
Rules: an interface must be in a zone to process traffic; an interface can be in only one zone; a zone may hold many interfaces; the zone type must match the interface type: Layer 3, Layer 2, Virtual Wire, Tap, Tunnel, or External for inter VSYS traffic.
Default behaviour comes from two implicit rules: intrazone-default allows within a zone, interzone-default denies between zones. Neither logs until you override them, which is why "the deny isn't in the log" is so common.
Attached at zone level: a Zone Protection profile, Packet Buffer Protection, User-ID enable and exclude lists, and log settings.
Configured at Network › Zones. Verify with show zone all.
In practiceOverride both implicit rules on day one to enable logging. Without it you have no record of what the firewall drops, which turns every investigation into guesswork.
All three defend against volumetric and malformed traffic, and none is a security profile: they are separate objects evaluated before a session is created, which is why they can stop floods that would otherwise exhaust the session table.
Zone Protection attaches to a zone and acts on the aggregate of traffic entering it:
Flood protection: SYN with random early drop or SYN cookies, ICMP, ICMPv6, UDP, Other IP, each with alert, activate and maximum rates.
Reconnaissance protection: TCP and UDP port scan, host sweep, with an action and block duration.
Packet based attack protection: spoofed IP, fragmented traffic, malformed options, specific TCP flag combinations.
Protocol protection: non IP protocol allow or block lists.
DoS Protection is applied through a DoS policy rule and is far more granular, targeting specific source and destination pairs. Thresholds are aggregate (shared by everything matching) or classified (per source IP, destination IP, or both).
Packet Buffer Protection protects the firewall itself from sessions consuming excessive buffer, enabled globally at Device › Setup › Session and toggled per zone.
In practiceThe ordering point matters: zone protection acts earliest, before a session exists; security profiles act last, on the content of an allowed session. If a flood is exhausting the session table, no security profile will help you.

✓ Check yourself virtual wire and zones

  1. Tagged traffic is being dropped on a new virtual wire. First thing to check?
    Tag Allowed on the virtual wire object. Default passes untagged only, 0 means untagged.
  2. What does link state pass through prevent?
    Neighbours sending into a black hole. Losing link one side brings down the partner, so upstream failover triggers.
  3. Name three things virtual wire cannot do.
    Routing, VPN termination, DHCP, also GlobalProtect and hosting management services.
  4. Same zone traffic: allowed or denied by default, and is it logged?
    Allowed by intrazone-default, and not logged until you override the rule.
  5. How many zones can one interface belong to?
    Exactly one. A zone can hold many interfaces, but not the reverse.
  6. A SYN flood is exhausting the session table. Which control helps, and why not a security profile?
    Zone Protection flood protection: it runs before session creation. Security profiles inspect content only after a session is allowed.

✓ Anchor facts

  • A virtual wire binds exactly two interfaces.
  • Tag Allowed 0 = untagged. Tagged traffic needs its tags listed.
  • Link state pass through is what lets neighbours detect failure.
  • Virtual wire subinterfaces split by VLAN tag or tag plus IP classifier.
  • intrazone-default allows, interzone-default denies, and neither logs by default.
  • Zone Protection = per zone, aggregate. DoS Protection = per rule, classified or aggregate.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Tagged traffic dropped because Tag Allowed was never set.
  • Expecting a virtual wire to terminate a VPN or serve DHCP.
  • Putting a Layer 3 interface in a virtual wire zone, types must match.
  • Wondering why denies aren't logged: override the implicit rules.
  • Calling Zone Protection a security profile. It is not, and it runs far earlier.
Zone ProtectionDoS ProtectionPacket Buffer Protection
Attached toA zoneA DoS policy ruleGlobal, toggled per zone
ScopeAggregate entering the zoneSpecific source/destination pairsThe firewall's own buffers
ThresholdsAggregate onlyAggregate or classifiedLatency and buffer utilisation

Part 4 · Routing

Routing Foundations

PAN-OS has moved its routing implementation forward, so know the current engine rather than legacy virtual-router behaviour. This is where study material written a few years ago will catch you out.

Mode
PAN-OS now offers two routing modes, and which one is active changes the entire configuration layout.
Legacy mode · virtual routersThe traditional model. A virtual router (VR) holds interfaces, static routes and routing protocol configuration, each with its own RIB and FIB. Protocol configuration lives inside the virtual router object, and redistribution uses PAN-OS specific redistribution profiles.
Advanced Routing Engine · logical routersThe current implementation, built on a standards based routing stack. The container is now a logical router, and protocol configuration is separated into routing profiles that are created once and attached where needed, BGP profiles, OSPF profiles, filters, redistribution profiles, BFD profiles.
The benefits are a more standard configuration model, richer route filtering with route maps, prefix lists, access lists and AS path access lists, and closer parity with how routing is configured on dedicated routers.
Enabling it is a device wide change under Device › Setup › Management › Advanced Routing, and it requires a reboot. Existing virtual routers are not automatically converted. You rebuild the configuration as logical routers.
In practiceCheck which mode a firewall is in before you start looking for configuration, because the menu tree differs. If a scenario mentions route maps or prefix lists, that is the Advanced Routing Engine; if it mentions redistribution profiles inside a virtual router, that is legacy mode.

How PAN-OS picks a route and where Policy Based Forwarding sits relative to the routing table

A static route needs a destination prefix, a next hop (IP address, the next VR or logical router, an FQDN, or Discard to blackhole), and an interface. It also takes an administrative distance (default 10) and a metric (default 10).
Route selection order: longest prefix match first, then lowest administrative distance, then lowest metric.
Path monitoring on a static route sends ICMP probes to one or more destinations. If they fail, the route is withdrawn from the RIB so a backup route with a higher distance takes over. Configurable are the ping interval, ping count, failure condition (any or all), and a preemptive hold time before the route is reinstated.
Verify with show routing route, show routing fib, and for the Advanced Routing Engine show advanced-routing route.
In practiceA floating static backup route only works if the primary is actually removed from the table. Without path monitoring, a static route stays up as long as the interface is up, so a failure further along the path never triggers failover.

✓ Check yourself routing foundations

  1. What is the container for routing in legacy mode, and what replaces it under the Advanced Routing Engine?
    Virtual router in legacy mode; logical router under the ARE, with protocol config moved into reusable routing profiles.
  2. Does enabling the Advanced Routing Engine convert your existing configuration?
    No. It is a device wide toggle that requires a reboot, and you rebuild the configuration as logical routers.
  3. State the route selection order.
    Longest prefix match → lowest administrative distance → lowest metric. PBF is evaluated before any of it.
  4. Default administrative distance for a static route, OSPF internal, and eBGP?
    Static 10, OSPF internal 30, eBGP 20. OSPF external is 110 and iBGP is 200.
  5. Why does a floating static backup route sometimes never take over?
    No path monitoring. Without it the route stays up as long as the interface is up, so a failure further along the path never removes it.
  6. Which commands show the routing table and the forwarding table?
    show routing route and show routing fib; under the ARE, show advanced-routing route.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Virtual router = legacy. Logical router = Advanced Routing Engine.
  • Enabling ARE is device wide and requires a reboot; configuration is not auto converted.
  • ARE brings route maps, prefix lists, access lists, AS path lists.
  • Selection: longest prefix → lowest AD → lowest metric.
  • Static AD default 10; OSPF internal 30, external 110; eBGP 20, iBGP 200.
  • PBF runs before the route lookup and only on transit traffic.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Studying virtual routers when the estate runs the Advanced Routing Engine.
  • Expecting a floating static to fail over without path monitoring.
  • Forgetting PBF does not apply to firewall originated traffic.
  • Deploying PBF with no monitoring, creating a silent blackhole.
  • Assuming eBGP outranks OSPF everywhere, compare the actual distances.
Legacy virtual routerAdvanced Routing Engine
ContainerVirtual routerLogical router
Protocol configInside the VR objectReusable routing profiles
FilteringRedistribution profilesRoute maps, prefix lists, access lists, AS path lists
EnablementDefaultDevice wide toggle, reboot required
Migration, Manual rebuild, not automatic

Part 4 · Routing

Dynamic Routing

The objectives name dynamic routing protocols, redistribution and policies, and route monitoring. You need protocol competence plus how PAN-OS specifically exposes it.

Mode
Configured per virtual router or logical router. You set a Router ID, enable the protocol, define areas, and add interfaces to those areas.
Area types: Normal, Stub (blocks type 5, optionally with no summary for totally stubby), and NSSA (blocks type 5 but permits type 7, with a translation option on the ABR). Area 0 is the backbone and all other areas must connect to it, directly or through a virtual link.
Interface settings: link type (broadcast, p2p, p2mp), priority for DR/BDR election, cost, hello and dead intervals, and passive to advertise a network without forming adjacencies on it.
Authentication profiles support MD5 or simple password.
OSPFv3 is configured separately for IPv6 and uses IPsec for authentication rather than built in auth.
Verify with show routing protocol ospf neighbor, ... interface, ... area.
In practiceAdjacency failures on PAN-OS are the same as anywhere: mismatched area ID, hello/dead timers, MTU, network type, or authentication. Stuck in ExStart almost always means MTU.
Enable BGP on the router, set the Router ID and local AS, then create peer groups and peers within them. Peer group type is EBGP, IBGP, EBGP Confed or IBGP Confed.
Per peer: peer AS, local and peer addresses, multi hop TTL, keepalive and hold timers, MD5 authentication, and address family.
Import and export rules are where PAN-OS does route filtering and manipulation in legacy mode, match on prefix, AS path, community or next hop, then set local preference, MED, AS path prepend, weight, community or next hop. Under the Advanced Routing Engine this moves to route maps with prefix lists and AS path access lists.
Other settings: graceful restart, route reflector client, confederations, aggregation, and ECMP at router level.
Verify with show routing protocol bgp summary, ... peer, ... loc-rib, ... rib-out.
In practiceRemember the firewall is a full BGP speaker, so the best path rules apply as normal: weight, then local preference, then locally originated, then shortest AS path. The PAN-OS specific part is knowing that import/export rules are where you set those attributes.

Redistribution and route filtering legacy profiles on the left, the Advanced Routing Engine's toolkit on the right

Legacy mode: you build a redistribution profile with a priority from 1 to 255 (lower evaluated first), filter by source type, interface, destination or next hop, set the action to redist or no-redist, then reference that profile from the receiving protocol.
Advanced Routing Engine: standard constructs instead, prefix lists, access lists, AS path access lists, community lists, combined in a route map that matches and then sets attributes.
Typical redistributions: connected and static into OSPF or BGP, OSPF into BGP, BGP into OSPF, and the default route.
Always consider loop prevention when redistributing both directions between two protocols, and filter or tag routes so they do not come back in.
In practiceTwo way redistribution without filtering is how you create a routing loop on any platform. On PAN-OS the tool differs by mode, but the discipline is identical: tag on the way out, filter on the way back in.
BFD gives subsecond failure detection independently of protocol timers. A BFD profile sets the desired minimum transmit interval, required minimum receive interval and a detection multiplier, and is attached to an OSPF or BGP neighbour or to a static route.
ECMP is enabled per router, supporting up to four equal cost paths. Load balancing methods are IP modulo, IP hash, weighted round robin and balanced round robin. Enabling or disabling ECMP restarts the routing process, so it is disruptive.
Route monitoring is the umbrella term for path monitoring on static routes, PBF monitoring, and tunnel monitoring, each withdraws or bypasses something when a probe fails.
In practiceUse weighted round robin when the links are not equal capacity, and remember that changing ECMP settings is a restart, not a hitless change, schedule it.

✓ Check yourself dynamic routing

  1. An OSPF adjacency is stuck in ExStart. Most likely cause?
    MTU mismatch. Area ID, timers, network type and authentication mismatches prevent adjacency earlier than ExStart.
  2. How does OSPFv3 authenticate?
    With IPsec: it has no built in authentication, unlike OSPFv2 which supports MD5 or simple password.
  3. Where do you set local preference or AS path prepend in legacy mode, and under the ARE?
    Legacy: BGP import and export rules. ARE: route maps with prefix lists and AS path access lists.
  4. What range does a redistribution profile priority use, and which is evaluated first?
    1 to 255, and lower is evaluated first.
  5. How many equal cost paths does ECMP support, and what is the catch when changing it?
    Up to four. Enabling or disabling ECMP restarts the routing process, so it is disruptive, schedule it.
  6. Requirement is subsecond failure detection on a BGP peering. What do you add?
    A BFD profile attached to the neighbour. Protocol hold timers alone can take tens of seconds.

✓ Anchor facts

  • OSPF areas: Normal, Stub, NSSA; area 0 is the backbone; virtual links bridge the gap.
  • OSPFv3 authenticates with IPsec, not built in auth.
  • BGP peer group types: EBGP, IBGP, EBGP Confed, IBGP Confed.
  • Legacy filtering = import/export rules and redistribution profiles; ARE = route maps.
  • Redistribution profile priority is 1 to 255, lower first.
  • ECMP: up to 4 paths, and toggling it restarts routing.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Two way redistribution with no filtering → routing loop.
  • Forgetting passive interfaces advertise the network without forming adjacency.
  • Expecting ECMP changes to be hitless. They restart the routing process.
  • Relying on protocol hold timers when the requirement is subsecond, use BFD.
  • Looking for route maps on a firewall still in legacy routing mode.
TaskLegacy modeAdvanced Routing Engine
Filter redistributed routesRedistribution profileRoute map + prefix list
Manipulate BGP attributesImport / export rulesRoute map set clauses
Match AS pathRegex in an import ruleAS path access list
Match communitiesImport ruleCommunity list
ContainerVirtual routerLogical router

Part 5 · NAT and Traffic Steering

NAT

You cannot operate a firewall without it, and scenario questions assume it. The golden rule carries most of the page.

Mode
Dynamic IP and Port (DIPP): many to one using ports. The translated address is the interface address or a translated address pool. Standard outbound internet breakout. Oversubscription is configurable (1x, 2x, 4x, 8x) and determines how many concurrent sessions one IP supports.
Dynamic IP: one to one from a pool, no port translation. When the pool is exhausted new sessions fail, unless Advanced (Dynamic IP/Port Fallback) is configured so it falls back to DIPP.
Static IP: fixed one to one, port preserved. Can be bidirectional, which automatically creates the implicit reverse destination NAT: a rule you did not write.
Configured at Policies › NAT. Verify with:
show running nat-policy
show running ippool: DIPP utilisation per rule
show session all filter source 10.1.1.5
In practiceDIPP pool exhaustion presents as new sessions failing while existing ones keep working. Check show running ippool before assuming a policy problem, the symptom looks identical to a broken rule.
Destination NAT translates the destination address of inbound traffic to an internal server, optionally translating the port as well, which is port forwarding.
PAN-OS also supports dynamic destination NAT to a translated address pool, giving basic session distribution across several servers, round robin, not health aware.
The NAT rule's destination zone is the zone the pre NAT destination routes to, which is usually the untrust zone. That means the firewall needs a route for the public address so it can resolve the correct egress zone.
Where the public address is in the same subnet as the interface, PAN-OS answers ARP for it automatically. An address outside that subnet needs the upstream device to route it to the firewall.
In practiceThe most common inbound failure is the NAT rule's destination zone set to the server's zone. It must be the zone the firewall routes the public address to, not where the server lives.

The golden rule, and the U turn case that breaks people pre NAT address, post NAT zone

The scenario: an internal user reaches an internal server using the server's public IP, usually because internal DNS returns the public record.
Destination NAT alone is not enough. The firewall translates the destination, the server receives the packet, but the source is still the user's internal address, so the server replies directly to the user, bypassing the firewall. The user discards that reply because it comes from an address it never contacted.
The fix is a single NAT rule doing both translations: destination NAT to the server's private address and source NAT to the firewall's internal interface. The server replies to the firewall, which translates back correctly.
On that rule, source zone and destination zone are both the internal zone, because the traffic never leaves the firewall's inside.
The cleaner alternative is split DNS, so internal clients resolve the private address and U turn NAT is never needed.
In practice"The website works from outside but not from the office" is almost always this. Fix it with split DNS where you control the zone, U turn NAT where you don't.
The golden rule: security policy is evaluated using the pre NAT address but the post NAT zone.
For an inbound DNAT that means the security rule's destination address is the public IP (policy is evaluated before translation) while the destination zone is the internal zone (the firewall has already done its route lookup to determine egress).
NAT rules are matched top down, first match, evaluated on the original packet, and applied at egress.
Verification, in this order:
test nat-policy-match from TRUST to UNTRUST source 10.1.1.5 destination 8.8.8.8 protocol 6
test security-policy-match from ... to ... source ... destination ... application ...
then show session id <id>: the c2s and s2c flows show the actual post NAT values per direction.
In practice"NAT works but traffic is blocked" is this rule applied half right. Run both test commands: if NAT matches and security doesn't, check the zone; if security matches and traffic still fails, check the route.

✓ Check yourself NAT

  1. State the golden rule for writing a security rule alongside an inbound DNAT.
    Pre NAT address, post NAT zone. Destination is the public IP; destination zone is the internal zone.
  2. New outbound sessions are failing but established ones work. First check?
    show running ippool: DIPP pool or port exhaustion. Looks identical to a broken rule.
  3. What does bidirectional static NAT create that you did not write?
    The implicit reverse destination NAT rule. Worth remembering when auditing a rulebase.
  4. Why does U turn NAT need source translation as well as destination?
    Without it the server replies directly to the internal user, bypassing the firewall, and the user drops the reply.
  5. Which zone goes on a NAT rule's destination field for inbound DNAT?
    The zone the firewall routes the public address to, usually untrust, not the server's zone.
  6. Which two test commands do you run before changing configuration?
    test nat-policy-match and test security-policy-match.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Pre NAT address, post NAT zone.
  • NAT rules: top down, first match, on the original packet, applied at egress.
  • DIPP many to one with ports · Dynamic IP one to one no ports · Static fixed.
  • Bidirectional static NAT auto creates the reverse rule.
  • U turn needs destination AND source translation in one rule.
  • DIPP oversubscription: 1x, 2x, 4x, 8x.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Writing the security rule with the private destination IP.
  • NAT destination zone set to the server's zone rather than the routed pre NAT zone.
  • Solving U turn with destination NAT only.
  • Missing DIPP exhaustion: new sessions fail, old ones work.
  • Forgetting the implicit rule bidirectional static NAT created.
NAT typeMappingPorts translated?Exhaustion behaviour
Dynamic IP and PortMany to oneYesNew sessions fail once ports run out
Dynamic IPOne to one from a poolNoFails unless DIPP fallback configured
Static IPFixed one to oneNoN/A
Destination NATPublic to privateOptional (port forwarding)N/A
U turnDestination and sourceSource uses DIPPAs DIPP

14

Part 5 · NAT and Traffic Steering

Policy Based Forwarding, DHCP, IPv6, Sessions and QoS

Policy Based Forwarding overrides the routing table, and the service features (DHCP, IPv6, sessions and QoS) are the everyday configuration that surrounds it.

Mode
PBF overrides the routing table for traffic matching a PBF rule, letting you forward by policy rather than by destination alone. Configured at Policies › Policy Based Forwarding.
Match criteria, richer than routingSource zone or interface, source address and user, destination address, application, and service. Being able to match on application and user is what routing cannot do.
ActionsForward to a specified egress interface and next hop · Forward to VSYS · Discard · No PBF to exempt traffic and return it to normal routing.
Monitoring makes it safeWith monitoring enabled and Disable this rule if nexthop is unreachable set, a failed next hop causes the rule to be bypassed and the packet follows the routing table instead. Without it, a dead next hop is a silent blackhole.
Symmetric returnUses the ARP cache to send return traffic back out the interface it arrived on, preventing asymmetric routing when PBF steers flows down a non default path.
Two scope limits: PBF is evaluated before the route lookup, and it applies only to traffic through the firewall, not to traffic the firewall originates itself. For firewall sourced traffic use service routes instead.
Verify with show pbf rule all and test pbf-policy-match.
In practicePBF is the usual answer to "send guest traffic out the broadband link and corporate over MPLS". Always pair it with monitoring. And remember application based PBF has a chicken and egg problem: the firewall needs several packets to identify the application, so the first packets of a session may take the routed path before PBF can act.

Where PBF sits, and what it cannot reach before the route lookup, and transit traffic only

DHCP server on a Layer 3 interface: an address pool, reserved addresses, lease type (unlimited or timeout), and options including gateway, subnet mask, DNS, WINS, NTP and domain. Configured at Network › DHCP.
DHCP relay forwards client requests to up to eight external servers, with IPv4 and IPv6 relay configured separately. An interface is either a server or a relay, never both.
DHCP client on an interface obtains an address and can optionally accept the default route from the server, common on branch internet interfaces.
IPv6 is configured alongside IPv4 on the same interface. You enable IPv6 on the interface, add addresses, and choose whether the firewall sends Router Advertisements for SLAAC. Neighbour Discovery replaces ARP, with a DAD (duplicate address detection) setting and an ND cache. Security policy and NAT support IPv6, and NAT66, NAT64 and NPTv6 exist for translation.
Verify with show dhcp server lease all, show dhcp client state all, show ipv6 neighbor.
In practiceAn interface cannot be a DHCP server and relay simultaneously, the GUI will let you start configuring both and then complain on commit. And with IPv6 enabled, remember policy must be written for both families or you get silent single stack failures.
Session settings at Device › Setup › Session: global timeouts (TCP, UDP, ICMP, default, discard, plus TCP handshake and TIME_WAIT), TCP settings including reject non SYN TCP and asymmetric path handling, and session rematch.
Session rematch is important: with it enabled, a policy change is applied to existing sessions on the next packet. With it disabled, existing sessions keep their original treatment until they close. That is why tightening a rule sometimes appears to do nothing.
Reject non SYN TCP and asymmetric path are the two settings to relax only deliberately. They exist to reject out of state packets, which is exactly what asymmetric routing produces.
QoS on PAN-OS: a QoS profile defines eight classes with guaranteed and maximum bandwidth and priority (real time, high, medium, low); a QoS policy classifies traffic into a class by zone, address, user, application or service; and the profile is applied to a physical egress interface under Network › QoS.
QoS is egress only, and class 4 is the default for unmatched traffic. Verify with show qos interface ethernet1/1.
In practiceSession rematch explains the classic "I tightened the rule and traffic still flows". Either enable rematch or clear the sessions with clear session id <id>. And QoS only shapes what leaves the interface. You cannot shape inbound traffic on the link you are receiving it on.

✓ Check yourself steering and services

  1. PBF is configured and the next hop dies. What happens, and what makes that safe?
    Without monitoring the traffic blackholes. With monitoring plus "disable this rule if nexthop is unreachable", it falls back to the routing table.
  2. Why can PBF not steer the firewall's own update traffic?
    PBF applies only to transit traffic. Firewall originated traffic is steered with service routes.
  3. What is the catch with application based PBF?
    App-ID needs several packets, but PBF decides on the first one, so early packets may take the routed path.
  4. Can one interface be both a DHCP server and a DHCP relay?
    No: one or the other. Relay forwards to up to eight servers.
  5. You tighten a security rule but traffic keeps flowing. Why, and two fixes?
    Session rematch is disabled, so existing sessions keep their original treatment. Enable rematch, or clear session id.
  6. Which direction does PAN-OS QoS work in, and which class catches unmatched traffic?
    Egress only, applied to a physical interface. Class 4 is the default.

✓ Anchor facts

  • PBF runs before the route lookup and only on transit traffic.
  • PBF matches on application and user, which routing cannot.
  • No PBF action exempts traffic back to normal routing.
  • DHCP relay: up to eight servers; server and relay are mutually exclusive per interface.
  • Session rematch decides whether policy changes hit existing sessions.
  • QoS: 8 classes, egress only, class 4 default.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Deploying PBF with no monitoring → silent blackhole.
  • Expecting PBF to steer firewall sourced traffic.
  • Relaxing reject non SYN TCP casually to "fix" asymmetric routing.
  • Forgetting IPv6 needs its own policy, or you get single stack failures.
  • Trying to shape inbound traffic with QoS on the receiving interface.
Steering mechanismApplies toDecides onFailure behaviour
Routing tableAll trafficDestination prefixNext best route
PBFTransit onlyZone, src, dst, app, user, serviceBlackhole unless monitored
Service routesFirewall originated onlyThe service typeService fails
QoSEgress per interfaceClass from QoS policyFalls to class 4

Part 6 · Policy and Objects

Rule Evaluation

How a rulebase is read, and the two rules at the bottom that you never wrote. Get this and most policy behaviour becomes predictable rather than surprising.

Mode
Rules are read top down, and the first match wins and evaluation stops. Nothing below a matching rule is consulted. That means rule order is policy, not presentation.
The two implicit rulesAt the bottom of every rulebase sit two rules you cannot delete:
intrazone-default: traffic within the same zone is allowed.
interzone-default: traffic between different zones is denied.
Neither logs by default. You can override them to enable logging and to attach a log forwarding profile, which is the single most valuable day one change.
What a match is evaluated againstZone, address, user, application, service and URL category all have to match. A rule with any in a field matches everything in that field. Remember from the NAT page that policy uses the pre NAT address but the post NAT zone.
ActionsAllow · Deny (uses the application's default deny action) · Drop (silent) · Reset client · Reset server · Reset both. Drop presents to the user as a hang; reset presents as connection refused.
Verify with show running security-policy and predict a match with test security-policy-match from TRUST to UNTRUST source 10.1.1.5 destination 8.8.8.8 application ssl protocol 6 destination-port 443.
In practiceIf denied traffic never appears in the log, it hit an implicit rule that is not logging. Override both on day one. And match the user's described symptom to the action, "it hangs" means drop, "connection refused" means reset.

Reading a rulebase first match stops evaluation, and the bottom two are yours to override

Every rule has a Rule Type, and it changes which traffic the rule can match regardless of the zones you list.
universal (the default): matches traffic both within and between the listed zones. So a rule with source TRUST and destination TRUST-DMZ also matches TRUST-to-TRUST traffic.
intrazone: matches only traffic whose source and destination zone are the same. The destination zone field is greyed out because it is implied.
interzone: matches only traffic crossing from one zone to a different one.
The consequence: a universal rule is broader than it looks. If you intend a rule to apply only to traffic leaving a zone, set it to interzone rather than relying on the zone fields.
In practiceThis silently widens rules. A universal allow with the same zone in both fields permits intrazone traffic you may not have considered, and since intrazone-default already allows it, the effect is easy to miss until you tighten the default.
PAN-OS has several separate rulebases under Policies, each evaluated at a different point:
NAT: evaluated on the original packet, applied at egress.
Policy Based Forwarding: before the route lookup.
Decryption: before App-ID can see inside TLS.
Authentication: triggers a challenge for unidentified users.
Security: the allow or deny decision.
Application Override: bypasses App-ID for matched traffic.
QoS: classifies for egress shaping.
DoS Protection: threshold enforcement.
Tunnel Inspection: inspects inside GRE and non encrypted tunnels.
Each rulebase is independently top down, first match. A packet may match one rule in several rulebases. They are not alternatives.
In practiceThe ordering that catches people: decryption happens before security policy can use App-ID meaningfully on TLS, and application override happens before App-ID, which is why an override turns a rule into a port based one and disables threat inspection for that traffic.

✓ Check yourself rule evaluation

  1. Denied traffic is not appearing in the Traffic log. Why, and what is the fix?
    It hit an implicit rule, and neither logs by default. Override intrazone-default and interzone-default to enable logging.
  2. Same zone in both fields, rule type left at default. What traffic does it match?
    Universal matches both intrazone and interzone, so it is broader than it looks. Use interzone to restrict it.
  3. A user reports the application hangs. Which action is likely, and which would give "connection refused"?
    Drop gives a hang. Reset gives connection refused. Match the symptom to the log action.
  4. Why is rule 3 in a rulebase sometimes never reached?
    It is shadowed by a broader rule above it. First match wins and evaluation stops.
  5. Which two rulebases are evaluated before security policy can use App-ID properly?
    Decryption (so App-ID can see inside TLS) and Application Override (which bypasses App-ID entirely).
  6. Which command predicts the matching rule without generating traffic?
    test security-policy-match with from, to, source, destination, application and port.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Top down, first match, then stop. Rule order is policy.
  • intrazone-default allows, interzone-default denies, neither logs.
  • universal matches within and between zones, broader than it looks.
  • Drop = hang. Reset = connection refused.
  • Each rulebase is independent and separately first match.
  • Policy uses the pre NAT address, post NAT zone.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Leaving the implicit rules unlogged, then having no record of denies.
  • Relying on zone fields instead of setting interzone rule type.
  • Writing a rule below a broader one and wondering why it never hits.
  • Treating deny and drop as identical, the user experience differs.
  • Forgetting application override disables threat inspection for that traffic.
Rule typeMatchesDestination zone fieldUse when
universal (default)Within and between listed zonesEditableGeneral purpose, but check the breadth
intrazoneSame source and destination zone onlyGreyed outControlling traffic inside a zone
interzoneCrossing zones onlyEditableYou mean "leaving this zone"

16

Part 6 · Policy and Objects

Applications in Policy

The service field, dependencies and shadowing. application-default versus any is the single most consequential setting on a rule, and the one most often left wrong.

Mode
The Service column on a security rule decides which ports the named applications are permitted on. It has three meanings:
application-default: the application is allowed only on the ports Palo Alto defines as its standard ports. So web-browsing is permitted on 80, and an attempt to run web-browsing on 8080 is not matched by this rule.
any: the application is allowed on any port. Convenient, and the reason evasive applications succeed. You have said "this app, anywhere".
A specific service object: your own port list, which is what you use for non standard internal applications.
Why it mattersUsing any reopens the port hopping gap that App-ID exists to close. The recommendation is application-default for internet bound rules, and explicit service objects where an application genuinely runs on a non standard port.
The trade offapplication-default can break legitimate traffic that runs an app on an unusual port. The fix is a specific service object for that rule, not switching the whole rule to any.
In practiceThis is the most common real world misconfiguration. A rule reading "allow web-browsing, service any" looks tight because it names an application, but it permits that application on every port. Check the Service column whenever a rule looks safer than it behaves.

The Service column changes what a rule actually permits naming an application is not the same as constraining it

Dependencies: some App-IDs cannot be identified without their parent applications also being permitted. Allowing facebook-chat requires web-browsing and ssl as well, because the session starts as those before it can be refined.
PAN-OS shows dependencies in the application object, and Policy Optimizer will flag rules where a dependency is missing. The symptom is a rule that looks correct and an application that still fails.
Implicit dependency handling exists for some applications where the parent is allowed by the same rule, but do not rely on it, list the dependencies.
Application overrideA separate rulebase that bypasses App-ID for matched traffic, forcing it to a named application, usually a custom one, based on port and zone.
The cost is significant: overridden traffic skips App-ID and therefore skips threat inspection for that session, turning the rule into a port based one. Legitimate uses are a latency sensitive internal application, or one App-ID repeatedly misidentifies.
In practiceApplication override is a last resort, not a tuning tool. If an application is being misidentified, a custom App-ID is almost always the better answer because it preserves inspection.
Shadowing is a rule that can never match because a broader rule above it catches the same traffic first. PAN-OS warns about some shadowing on commit, but not all of it.
Rule usage tracking at Policies shows each rule's hit count, first hit and last hit, with a Rule Usage filter for unused rules. This is how you find rules safe to remove, though check the timeframe before concluding a rule is dead.
Policy OptimizerPurpose built for migrating port based rules to App-ID. Its views are:
No App Specified: rules still matching on service only.
Unused Apps: applications listed on a rule that never actually appear.
Rule Usage: hit counts over a period.
The workflow: find a port based rule, view the applications it actually carried, add those applications to the rule, set the service to application-default, then remove the port based service. Because it is built from observed traffic, the risk of breaking something is far lower than guessing.
Also useful: tags and rule groups for organising a large rulebase, audit comments on rule changes, and rule hit counters reset with clear rule-hit-count.
In practicePolicy Optimizer is the honest answer to "how do you move a legacy port based rulebase to App-ID without an outage". You migrate from what the rule actually carried, not from what you assume it carried.

✓ Check yourself applications in policy

  1. A rule allows web-browsing with service set to any. What is wrong with it?
    It permits that application on every port, reopening the port hopping gap. Use application-default.
  2. application-default is breaking a legitimate internal app on a non standard port. Correct fix?
    A specific service object for that rule, not switching the rule to any.
  3. You allow facebook-chat and it still fails. Why?
    Missing dependencies: it also needs web-browsing and ssl, because the session starts as those.
  4. What does application override cost you?
    Overridden traffic skips App-ID and threat inspection. A custom App-ID is usually better.
  5. Name the three Policy Optimizer views.
    No App Specified, Unused Apps, Rule Usage.
  6. Why is Policy Optimizer safer than manually rewriting port based rules?
    It shows the applications the rule actually carried, so you migrate from observed traffic rather than assumption.

✓ Anchor facts

  • application-default = standard ports only. any = every port.
  • Dependencies must be listed, facebook-chat needs web-browsing and ssl.
  • Application override bypasses App-ID, so it bypasses threat inspection.
  • Policy Optimizer views: No App Specified, Unused Apps, Rule Usage.
  • Rule usage shows hit count, first hit, last hit.
  • Custom App-ID beats override, because it keeps inspection.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Assuming a rule is tight because it names an application, check the Service column.
  • Switching to any to fix a broken app instead of adding a service object.
  • Omitting dependencies, then blaming App-ID.
  • Using application override as a routine tuning tool.
  • Deleting a "0 hits" rule without checking the counter timeframe.
Service settingPermits the application onRiskUse for
application-defaultIts standard ports onlyBreaks non standard usageInternet bound rules
anyEvery portPort hopping and evasionRarely justified
Service objectThe ports you listMaintenance overheadInternal apps on odd ports

17

Part 6 · Policy and Objects

Objects, EDLs and Dynamic Address Groups

Objects keep a rulebase maintainable, and the dynamic ones, EDLs and Dynamic Address Groups, let policy change without a commit, which is how modern estates stay current.

Mode
Address objects take an IP netmask, an IP range, an IP wildcard mask, or an FQDN. FQDN objects are resolved by the firewall on a refresh interval, which lets policy follow a hostname whose address changes.
Address groups are static (a fixed member list) or dynamic (membership from a tag match expression).
Service objects define protocol and port, with optional session timeout overrides per service, useful for an application needing a longer idle timeout than the global default.
Application groups are static collections; application filters are dynamic, selecting by category, subcategory, technology, risk and characteristics. A filter automatically picks up new applications matching its criteria on each content update, which is powerful and slightly risky.
Regions are built in geographic objects usable as source or destination.
In practicePrefer an application filter over a long static group when the intent is categorical ("all peer to peer file sharing"), because it stays current. But know that a content update can then change what your rule permits without you editing it.
An EDL is a list the firewall fetches from a web server on a schedule and uses directly in policy, so the contents change without a commit.
Types: IP address, domain, URL, Predefined IP, Predefined URL, and Equipment Identity / Subscriber Identity on supporting platforms.
Palo Alto predefined EDLs ship with the platform and are maintained by Palo Alto, known malicious IPs, bulletproof hosting, Tor exit nodes, high risk IPs. They cost nothing to use and are a sensible first block rule.
Configuration: the source URL, a check for updates interval (five minutes to monthly), optional client certificate for authenticated sources, and an exception list for entries you want to ignore.
Test and verify with request system external-list show type ip name <name> and request system external-list refresh name <name>. The GUI has a Test Source URL button.
Limits are per platform: there is a maximum number of EDLs and a maximum total entries, so very large lists need checking against the model.
In practiceThe reason EDLs matter operationally: a threat intel feed updates the block list without a configuration change, so there is no commit, no change window and no approval cycle for each new indicator.

Dynamic policy without a commit EDLs pull lists from outside; tags drive Dynamic Address Groups from inside

Tags are labels with an optional colour, applied to address objects, rules and other objects. Beyond organisation, they are the mechanism behind dynamic membership.
A Dynamic Address Group (DAG) defines a match expression over tags using AND and OR, for example 'quarantine' and 'corporate'. Any IP carrying tags that satisfy the expression becomes a member, and membership changes take effect without a commit.
Tags reach an IP address three ways: manually, via the XML API (register and unregister calls), or from a log forwarding profile built in action that tags the source or destination of a matching log.
The auto quarantine patternA Threat log entry matches a log forwarding profile, whose built in action tags the source IP. The DAG picks it up, and a deny rule referencing that DAG isolates the host, within seconds, with no administrator action and no commit.
Verify with show object registered-ip all and show object dynamic-address-group all. Clear with debug object registered-ip clear all.
In practiceRegistered IP tags are runtime state, not configuration, so they do not survive a reboot and are not part of the saved config. If quarantine must persist, the tagging source has to reapply it.
Custom applications at Objects › Applications define your own App-ID for an in house or unrecognised application, using a signature based on context and pattern, or simply port and protocol. Because it is a real App-ID, threat inspection still applies, which is why it beats application override.
Custom objects › Spyware and Vulnerability let you write your own threat signatures with a threat ID in the 41000 to 45000 range, matched by pattern against a decoder context.
Custom URL categories are your own lists of URLs, usable in URL Filtering profiles and as a match condition in security policy. Types are URL List or Category Match.
Data patterns underpin Data Filtering: predefined patterns (credit card, social security number), regular expressions, and file properties.
Custom signatures are exported and imported as XML, which is how they are shared between firewalls outside Panorama.
In practiceWhen App-ID does not recognise an internal application, the ladder is: custom App-ID first (keeps inspection), application override only if that fails (loses inspection). Reaching straight for override is the common mistake.

✓ Check yourself objects and dynamic content

  1. What is the operational advantage of an EDL over an address group?
    The list contents change without a commit, no change window per indicator.
  2. Name three ways a tag gets applied to an IP address.
    Manually, via the XML API register call, or by a log forwarding profile built in action.
  3. Walk the auto quarantine chain.
    Threat log → log forwarding profile tags the source IP → DAG membership updates → deny rule referencing the DAG isolates the host. No commit.
  4. Do registered IP tags survive a reboot?
    No: they are runtime state, not configuration. The tagging source must reapply them.
  5. An internal app is unrecognised. Which do you build first, and why?
    A custom App-ID, because it keeps threat inspection. Application override loses it.
  6. What range do custom threat signature IDs use?
    41000 to 45000, for custom spyware and vulnerability objects.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Address objects: IP netmask, IP range, IP wildcard, FQDN.
  • Application filters are dynamic by category, risk and technology; groups are static.
  • EDL types: IP, domain, URL, predefined IP, predefined URL.
  • EDL and DAG changes need no commit.
  • DAG match expressions use AND / OR over tags.
  • Custom threat IDs: 41000 to 45000.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Expecting registered IP tags to persist across a reboot.
  • Forgetting EDL count and entry limits are per platform.
  • Not realising an application filter can change a rule's scope on a content update.
  • Using application override where a custom App-ID would keep inspection.
  • Assuming an FQDN object updates instantly. It follows a refresh interval.

18

Part 7 · App-ID and Content-ID

How App-ID Identifies Traffic

App-ID is what makes the firewall next generation rather than a port filter. Learn the identification sequence and the log values it produces, because both turn up constantly in troubleshooting.

Mode
App-ID identifies the application regardless of port, protocol, encryption or evasive technique. It works through four mechanisms applied in order, and the sequence matters because each stage can hand over to the next.
1 · Application signaturesTraffic is matched against signatures looking at unique properties and transaction characteristics, not just the port. This is where most applications are identified.
2 · TLS/SSH decryptionIf the traffic is encrypted and a decryption policy applies, it is decrypted so signatures can be applied to the payload. Without decryption, App-ID sees only the outer protocol: typically ssl or a partial identification from the certificate and SNI.
3 · Protocol decodersDecoders understand the protocol and look for applications tunnelled inside it. This is how the firewall finds a file transfer inside an HTTP session.
4 · HeuristicsBehavioural analysis for applications that deliberately evade signatures, encrypted peer to peer being the usual example.
Application shiftIdentification is iterative. A session may start as web-browsing, then be refined to facebook-base, then to facebook-chat. Each shift causes a fresh security policy lookup, which is why a session can be allowed initially and dropped moments later.
In practiceApplication shift explains the "it connected then died" symptom. The first App-ID matched a permissive rule; the refined App-ID did not. Check the Traffic log for the final application, not the one you expected.

App-ID values in the Traffic log four values that each point at a different root cause

Four values appear in the Application field instead of a real application name, and each points somewhere different:
incomplete: the TCP handshake never completed, so there was no payload to identify. A SYN with no SYN-ACK. This is a connectivity problem: routing, the server being down, or something upstream dropping the traffic.
insufficient data: the handshake did complete but there was not enough payload to identify the application before the session closed. Often entirely normal.
not applicable: the firewall denied the traffic before App-ID ever ran, because no rule permitted that port or service. This is a policy problem, and it is yours.
unknown-tcp / unknown-udp: real traffic that completed, but no signature matched. Usually an in house or bespoke application.
Filter for them in the Traffic log with ( app eq incomplete ) or ( app eq not-applicable ).
In practiceLearn the incomplete versus not applicable pair properly. One sends you to the network, the other to your own rulebase, and mixing them up wastes the first hour of an investigation.
For unknown-tcp or unknown-udp traffic, build a custom application at Objects › Applications. You define the protocol and port, and optionally a signature matching a pattern within a decoder context.
The advantage over application override is decisive: a custom App-ID is a real application, so threat inspection still applies. An override bypasses App-ID entirely and takes threat inspection with it.
You also set the application's characteristics: risk, category, subcategory, technology, whether it evades, tunnels other applications, or transfers files. These matter because application filters select on them.
Content updates and the new App-ID riskApplications and threats arrive in content updates. When Palo Alto releases new or modified App-IDs, traffic that previously matched one application may start matching a new, more specific one, and your rule may not list it.
PAN-OS mitigates this. The Applications and Threats update page shows which App-IDs are new, lets you disable new App-IDs on install and enable them later after review, and a content update schedule can set a threshold delay so updates are not installed the instant they are published.
In practiceThis is the one content update risk worth planning for. A working rule can break because the application it permits got split into a more specific one. Review new App-IDs before enabling, and use the threshold delay in production.

✓ Check yourself App-ID

  1. Name the four App-ID mechanisms in order.
    Application signatures → TLS/SSH decryption → protocol decoders → heuristics.
  2. A session connects then dies seconds later. What is the likely cause?
    Application shift. The refined App-ID triggered a fresh policy lookup and no longer matched the permissive rule.
  3. Distinguish incomplete from not applicable.
    incomplete = handshake never finished → connectivity problem. not applicable = denied before App-ID ran → policy problem.
  4. What does insufficient data mean, and is it a fault?
    Handshake completed but too little payload to identify before the session closed. Often normal.
  5. You see unknown-tcp for an in house app. What do you build, and why not an override?
    A custom App-ID: it keeps threat inspection. An override bypasses App-ID and inspection with it.
  6. How can a content update break a working rule, and what two controls help?
    A new App-ID may take over traffic your rule doesn't list. Use disable new App-IDs on install and a threshold delay on the update schedule.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Four mechanisms: signatures, decryption, decoders, heuristics.
  • Identification is iterative: each shift triggers a fresh policy lookup.
  • incomplete = no handshake. insufficient data = not enough payload.
  • not applicable = denied before App-ID ran. unknown-tcp = no signature.
  • Custom App-ID keeps threat inspection; override does not.
  • Content updates can introduce new App-IDs that change what a rule matches.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Confusing incomplete (network) with not applicable (policy).
  • Expecting App-ID to see inside TLS without decryption.
  • Treating insufficient data as an error when it is usually normal.
  • Installing content updates immediately in production with no threshold.
  • Reaching for application override before trying a custom App-ID.
Log valueMeansLook atUsually
incompleteHandshake never completedRouting, server, upstream dropsA network fault
insufficient dataToo little payload to identifyNothing, usuallyNormal behaviour

Part 7 · App-ID and Content-ID

Security Profiles · Antivirus to Vulnerability Protection

Where Content-ID becomes configuration. The distinction running through both pages: a rule decides whether traffic is allowed; a profile inspects what is inside it.

Mode
They do different jobs at different points. A security policy rule decides whether traffic is allowed at all, based on zone, address, user, application and service. A security profile decides what happens to the content inside traffic that has already been allowed.
So profiles attach to allow rules only. They never run on a deny rule: there is nothing left to inspect.
The profile types are Antivirus, Anti-Spyware, Vulnerability Protection, URL Filtering, File Blocking, WildFire Analysis and Data Filtering, with DNS Security and Enterprise DLP as licensed extensions.
Use Security Profile Groups rather than attaching profiles individually, so a rule references one group and every rule stays consistent. In Panorama, push a default group so no rule is ever created without inspection attached.
Palo Alto ships best practice profiles as a starting baseline, sensible, because building from empty means every signature decision is yours.
In practiceThe rule decides who gets through the door; the profile inspects what they are carrying once inside. If a rule denies traffic, no profile ever sees it, which is why threat logs are empty for denied sessions.

Where profiles sit in the flow zone protection acts before a session exists; profiles act last, on allowed traffic only

Antivirus scans allowed traffic for known malware using stream based scanning: inspecting as the file flows rather than buffering it whole, which is what keeps latency acceptable.
Actions are set per protocol decoder: FTP, HTTP, HTTP2, IMAP, POP3, SMB, SMTP. Available actions are default, allow, alert, drop, reset-client, reset-server, reset-both, where default uses the action the signature itself recommends.
It has two signature sources configured independently: standard antivirus signatures, and WildFire signatures generated from sandbox verdicts and pushed out within minutes.
Anti-Spyware · the other directionAntivirus catches malware arriving. Anti-Spyware catches an infection already inside talking out: command and control, beaconing, spyware phoning home. Rules are built by severity, each with an action and packet capture setting.
DNS sinkholingMalware usually resolves a domain before connecting out. Sinkholing spots the malicious lookup and forges a DNS response pointing at a sinkhole address you control.
The reason this matters is visibility. Without it the DNS query comes from your internal DNS server, so the log names the DNS server rather than the infected host. With sinkholing the infected host connects to the sinkhole itself, appearing in the Traffic log by source address, so you can identify patient zero.
In practiceSinkholing is the highest value feature on this page. Without it you know you have an infection somewhere; with it you know which machine.
This is the IPS function on a Palo Alto firewall. It protects against exploit attempts against known vulnerabilities: buffer overflows, illegal code execution, protocol anomalies, and scanning or probing behaviour.
Like Anti-Spyware, rules are built by severity with an action and packet capture. What differs is the scoping: you can narrow a rule by CVE, vendor ID, threat name, category and host type.
The host type field is the one worth knowing: client protects your users, server protects your published services. Server rules face the internet, so they normally carry stricter actions.
This profile generates most false positives, because bespoke and proprietary protocols trip generic vulnerability signatures. The fix is a threat exception scoped to that specific threat ID, restricted to the affected source or zone where possible, and documented. Never disable the profile.
In practiceVerify before excepting. Take the threat ID, confirm with a packet capture that it really is a false positive, then scope the exception as narrowly as the situation allows.

✓ Check yourself profiles, part one

  1. Why is the Threat log empty for traffic a rule denied?
    Profiles attach to allow rules and never run on a deny. There is nothing left to inspect.
  2. What does stream based scanning buy you?
    Inspection as the file flows rather than buffering it whole, which keeps latency acceptable.
  3. Antivirus versus Anti-Spyware in one line.
    Antivirus catches malware arriving; Anti-Spyware catches an infection already inside calling out.
  4. Why does DNS sinkholing identify patient zero when ordinary logging does not?
    Without it the query comes from your DNS server. With it, the infected host connects to the sinkhole and appears in the Traffic log by source.
  5. What does host type client versus server change on a vulnerability rule?
    Client protects your users; server protects published services. Server rules face the internet and are usually stricter.
  6. A bespoke app keeps tripping a vulnerability signature. Correct fix?
    A threat exception scoped to that threat ID, restricted by source or zone, verified first. Never disable the profile.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Profiles run on allowed traffic only, never on a deny.
  • Seven profiles, attached as a Security Profile Group.
  • Antivirus decoders: FTP, HTTP, HTTP2, IMAP, POP3, SMB, SMTP.
  • Antivirus has two signature sources: standard and WildFire.
  • Sinkholing reveals patient zero.
  • Vulnerability Protection is the IPS; scope by CVE and host type.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Expecting threat logs for denied traffic.
  • Attaching profiles individually instead of as a group.
  • Disabling a vulnerability profile to fix one false positive.
  • Excepting a threat ID without verifying it first.
  • Forgetting sinkholing needs an address you actually control.
ProfileCatchesKeyed byThreat log type
AntivirusKnown malware arrivingProtocol decodervirus
Anti-SpywareC2 and beaconing leavingSeverity; DNS sinkholingspyware
Vulnerability ProtectionExploit attempts, the IPSSeverity, CVE, host typevulnerability

Part 7 · App-ID and Content-ID

URL Filtering, Files, Data and Tuning

The remaining profiles, then the method that matters most in practice: identifying which profile blocked something, and tuning it without loosening protection wholesale.

Mode
Classifies web traffic into categories from the PAN-DB cloud database, with a local cache on the firewall and a cloud lookup for anything unknown.
Actions per category: allow, alert, block, continue, override. Two are worth explaining. continue presents an interstitial the user can click through, for categories you want to discourage rather than forbid. override requires a password, letting an administrator unlock a category for a specific user.
Beyond categories the profile handles URL category exceptions through allow and block lists, Safe Search enforcement, HTTP header insertion, and logging of the HTTP referer and user agent.
User Credential DetectionDetects when a user submits corporate credentials into an external website, which is how phishing actually succeeds. It works by IP to user mapping, group membership, or domain credential filter, and blocks the submission rather than the browsing.
The distinction people trip onURL category is used in two places. As a match condition in a security policy rule, where it influences whether traffic is allowed at all. And as an action inside the URL Filtering profile, where it controls what happens to allowed web traffic. Mixing them up is a common configuration error.
In practiceUser Credential Detection is the feature separating someone who has deployed URL filtering from someone who has read about it. Pair it mentally with Orion's passwordless work, one stops the credential being typed, the other removes it from the flow entirely.

Finding which profile blocked something the Threat log Type field names it, so you tune the right thing

File Blocking controls file types by direction: upload, download or both. Actions are alert, block, continue. A typical policy blocks executables inbound from the internet and blocks encrypted archives that cannot be inspected. Its value is stopping entire classes of file before any scanning is needed.
WildFire Analysis decides which files are forwarded to the sandbox for detonation, by application, file type and direction. The destination is either the public WildFire cloud or a private WF-500 appliance where data residency rules apply.
The relationship in one line: File Blocking decides whether the file is allowed at all; WildFire Analysis decides whether an allowed unknown file gets detonated. The verdict returns as an antivirus signature, closing the loop.
Data Filtering is the DLP style profile. It looks for sensitive data leaving, using predefined patterns (credit card, social security number) or custom regular expressions, with a weight per pattern and alert and block thresholds, so one match alerts and several in a transfer block. Enterprise DLP extends this as a licensed cloud service.
In practiceBlocking encrypted archives is the underrated File Blocking rule. If the firewall cannot open it, nothing downstream will inspect it either, so allowing it is a deliberate blind spot.
Start at the Threat log. The Type field names the profile that fired: virus, spyware, vulnerability, url, file, wildfire, data. That tells you which profile to tune without guessing.
From there the method is the same for any drop: take the threat ID, verify with a packet capture rather than assuming, create an exception scoped to that ID restricted by zone or source, and document the justification.
Rollout approachDeploy new profiles in alert only mode first, run a baseline period, review what they would have blocked, then move severity by severity to blocking actions.
That is not caution for its own sake. It is the only way to avoid taking out a production application with a signature you have never seen fire before.
Useful commands: show running security-policy to confirm which rule applied, and debug dataplane packet-diag set capture for the staged capture covered in the troubleshooting part.
In practiceThe discipline is evidence before action, fix narrowly, never loosen wholesale. It is the same method as any firewall drop, and the Threat log Type field is what makes it fast here.

✓ Check yourself profiles, part two

  1. What do the URL filtering actions continue and override each do?
    continue = interstitial the user clicks through. override = password required, so an admin can unlock a category.
  2. What does User Credential Detection stop, and how does it detect it?
    Corporate credentials submitted to external sites. By IP to user mapping, group membership, or domain credential filter: blocking the submission, not the browsing.
  3. Where are the two places URL category is used?
    As a match condition in a security rule (allowed at all?) and as an action in the URL Filtering profile (what happens to allowed web traffic).
  4. File Blocking versus WildFire Analysis in one line.
    File Blocking decides whether the file is allowed; WildFire Analysis decides whether an allowed unknown file gets detonated.
  5. Why block encrypted archives?
    If the firewall cannot open it, nothing downstream will inspect it either, allowing it is a deliberate blind spot.
  6. Which log field names the profile that blocked something?
    The Threat log Type field, virus, spyware, vulnerability, url, file, wildfire, data.

✓ Anchor facts

  • URL actions: allow, alert, block, continue, override.
  • User Credential Detection blocks the submission, not the browsing.
  • URL category works in a rule and in a profile: different effects.
  • File Blocking is by type and direction; WildFire Analysis picks what gets detonated.
  • Data Filtering uses pattern weight and thresholds.
  • Threat log Type names the profile. Fix with a scoped threat ID exception.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Confusing URL category in a rule with URL category in a profile.
  • Allowing encrypted archives that nothing can inspect.
  • Going straight to blocking on a new profile with no baseline.
  • Excepting a threat ID without a packet capture to confirm it.
  • Assuming WildFire Analysis blocks files. It selects what gets detonated.

WildFire AnalysisUnknown files for detonationApp, file type, directionwildfire

Part 8 · Certificates and Decryption

Purpose, Value and Network Design

Decryption is the densest topic in this guide, with five distinct subareas. Start with why you decrypt and where it sits in the design, because both shape every later decision.

Mode
The argument is simple: the overwhelming majority of traffic is encrypted, and everything the firewall does above Layer 4, App-ID refinement, antivirus, vulnerability signatures, URL categorisation of the full path, file blocking, WildFire, data filtering, operates on payload. Without decryption those engines see an opaque tunnel.
What you actually get by decrypting:
Threat visibility: malware and C2 hidden inside TLS become inspectable.
Accurate App-ID, without it the application is typically just ssl or a guess from the certificate and SNI.
Data loss control: Data Filtering cannot see what it cannot read.
Certificate verification: expired, untrusted and self signed server certificates get caught rather than silently accepted by the user.
The costs, stated honestlyCPU and latency: decryption is expensive, so it drives platform sizing more than almost anything else.
Privacy and legal obligations: some categories must not be decrypted.
Breakage: certificate pinning and mutual TLS fail when intercepted.
The resolution is not to choose an extreme. You decrypt selectively at a controlled inspection point, exclude what must not or cannot be decrypted, and size the hardware for what remains.
In practiceThe sentence that settles most design arguments: you cannot enforce policy on traffic you cannot read. The debate is then about which traffic, not whether.

The certificate objects, and what each one is for get these straight and the decryption pages become easy

Certificates live at Device › Certificate Management › Certificates. You can generate one on the firewall (self signed, or a CSR to be signed externally) or import one with its private key.
Key attributes when generating: common name, signed by (self signed or a local CA certificate), whether it is a Certificate Authority, the algorithm and key size, and Subject Alternative Names.
A certificate on the firewall carries usage flags, and these are the examinable part: Forward Trust Certificate, Forward Untrust Certificate, Trusted Root CA, and use for SSL Exclude Certificate.
PKI integrationSCEP lets the firewall obtain and renew certificates automatically from an enterprise CA, used heavily for GlobalProtect client certificates, since manual issuance does not scale.
Revocation checking is by CRL or OCSP, configured in a certificate profile. The firewall can also act as an OCSP responder.
The master keyEncrypts all private keys on the device. It must match across an HA pair and across Panorama managed firewalls. Set an expiry reminder, and be careful, a mismatched or lost master key is effectively unrecoverable.
In practiceGet the master key right before deploying certificates at scale. It is the one piece here where a mistake cannot be fixed by reissuing something.
These two are constantly confused, and the distinction is directional.
Certificate Profile defines how the firewall validates certificates presented to it. It lists the CA certificates to trust, enables CRL and/or OCSP checking, sets whether to block sessions on an unknown certificate status or on a revocation check timeout, and can require specific certificate attributes for username extraction.
Used for administrator certificate authentication, GlobalProtect client certificate checks, and captive portal: anywhere the firewall must decide whether someone else's certificate is acceptable.
SSL/TLS Service Profile governs how the firewall presents itself when it terminates TLS. It binds a certificate plus a minimum and maximum TLS version and the allowed cipher suites and algorithms.
Used for the management web interface, the GlobalProtect portal and gateway, and captive portal pages.
Setting minimum TLS 1.2 on management and GlobalProtect service profiles is the standard hardening step, and where Orion's quantum work eventually lands.
In practiceRemember it directionally. Profile in = certificate profile. Profile out = SSL/TLS service profile. If the question is "should I trust this certificate", it is the former; "which certificate do I show", the latter.

✓ Check yourself certificates and PKI

  1. Why does decryption matter for App-ID specifically?
    Without it the application is typically just ssl or a guess from the certificate and SNI, refinement needs payload.
  2. Name the three costs of decryption.
    CPU and latency, privacy and legal obligations, and breakage from certificate pinning and mutual TLS.
  3. Distinguish a certificate profile from an SSL/TLS service profile.
    Certificate profile validates certificates presented to the firewall; SSL/TLS service profile governs certificates the firewall presents outward.
  4. What is SCEP used for, and why does it matter?
    Automatic certificate issuance and renewal from an enterprise CA, essential for GlobalProtect client certificates, since manual issuance does not scale.
  5. What does the master key protect, and what must be true of it?
    All private keys on the device. It must match across an HA pair and Panorama managed firewalls. Losing it is unrecoverable.
  6. Name the certificate usage flags that matter for decryption.
    Forward Trust, Forward Untrust, Trusted Root CA, and SSL Exclude Certificate.

✓ Anchor facts

  • You cannot enforce policy on traffic you cannot read.
  • Certificate flags: Forward Trust, Forward Untrust, Trusted Root CA, SSL Exclude.
  • Certificate profile = validates inbound. SSL/TLS service profile = presents outward.
  • Revocation via CRL or OCSP; the firewall can be an OCSP responder.
  • SCEP automates issuance and renewal from an enterprise CA.
  • The master key must match across HA and Panorama managed devices.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Swapping certificate profile and SSL/TLS service profile.
  • Changing the master key without planning. It is unrecoverable.
  • Issuing GlobalProtect client certificates manually instead of via SCEP.
  • Leaving revocation check failure set to allow.
  • Sizing a platform without accounting for decryption CPU.
ObjectDirectionAnswersUsed by
Certificate: An identity the firewall holdsEverything below
Certificate profileInboundShould I trust this certificate?Admin auth, GP client certs, portal
SSL/TLS service profileOutboundWhich certificate do I present?Web UI, GP portal and gateway
