Part 8 · Certificates and Decryption

Forward Proxy, Forward Trust and Untrust

The outbound case, and the mechanism worth knowing explicitly. Forward trust versus forward untrust is the single most important pair on this page.

Mode
Used for outbound traffic: your users going to the internet. The firewall acts as a man in the middle you control, terminating the client's session and opening its own to the server.
The flowThe client connects. The firewall opens its own TLS session to the destination and inspects the real server certificate. It then generates a certificate on the fly for that site and presents it to the client. Two separate TLS sessions exist, and the firewall sees plaintext between them.
Forward Trust CertificateUsed when the real server certificate is valid and trusted. The firewall signs the impersonated certificate with this CA. Because this CA is installed in your clients' trust stores, the user sees no warning and browsing is seamless.
Forward Untrust CertificateUsed when the real server certificate is invalid: expired, self signed, untrusted issuer, name mismatch. The firewall signs with a CA that is deliberately not in client trust stores, so the user gets a browser warning.
That design is the clever part: it preserves the trust signal. Without two certificates, every site would appear valid to the user because the firewall resigned it, and you would have destroyed the very warning the browser exists to give.
Both must be flagged as Certificate Authority. The forward trust CA is distributed to clients by GPO or MDM; the forward untrust CA must never be distributed.
In practiceIf users report no warnings on genuinely broken sites, someone has distributed the forward untrust CA to the trust store. That silently removes a protection nobody will notice until it matters.

Forward proxy, and why there are two CAs the trust signal is preserved, not replaced

This is the recommended way to run forward proxy in an enterprise, and worth calling out explicitly.
The problem with a self signed forward trust CA: it is a brand new root that every client must be told to trust. You have to distribute it to every managed device, every BYOD device, every appliance and every application with its own trust store, and anything you miss breaks.
The subordinate CA approach: the firewall's forward trust certificate is signed by your existing enterprise root CA rather than being self signed. Because clients already trust that root, they automatically trust the firewall's certificates with no distribution step at all.
How you do it: generate a CSR on the firewall with the Certificate Authority flag set, have your enterprise CA sign it as a subordinate CA, import the signed certificate back, and flag it as Forward Trust Certificate.
The trade off to stateThe firewall now holds a subordinate CA key that can issue certificates trusted across your organisation. That is a significant concentration of trust, so it needs appropriate physical and administrative controls and careful master key handling.
In practiceThis is the difference between a decryption rollout that takes weeks and one that works on day one. If the question mentions an existing enterprise PKI, subordinate CA is almost always the expected answer.
The inbound case: internet users reaching your published servers. Mechanically different from forward proxy, and that difference is the exam point.
There is no impersonation and no certificate generation. Instead you import the server's actual certificate and private key onto the firewall. The firewall then decrypts the session passively using that key, inspects, and forwards.
Because it holds the real key, the client's TLS session is with the real server certificate, so the client sees nothing unusual and no CA distribution is needed anywhere.
Operational consequencesWhenever the server certificate is renewed, the new certificate and key must be reimported to the firewall. Miss that and inspection silently fails after renewal.
It also imposes a cryptographic constraint: ephemeral key exchange such as DHE or ECDHE defeats passive key based decryption, because the session key is not derivable from the server's private key. In those cases the firewall must proxy the session instead.
In practiceThe contrast worth memorising: forward proxy generates certificates; inbound inspection uses the real key. And add certificate renewal on protected servers to your change checklist, because it is the usual cause of inbound inspection quietly stopping.

✓ Check yourself forward proxy and inbound

  1. Why does forward proxy need two CA certificates?
    To preserve the trust signal. Valid sites are signed with the trust CA (no warning); invalid ones with the untrust CA (warning shown).
  2. Which CA must never be distributed to clients, and what breaks if it is?
    The forward untrust CA. Distributing it means broken sites stop producing warnings.
  3. What problem does a subordinate CA solve?
    No CA distribution. The forward trust certificate is signed by your existing enterprise root, which clients already trust.
  4. What is the trade off of the subordinate CA approach?
    The firewall holds a key that can issue org wide trusted certificates: a concentration of trust needing real controls.
  5. How does inbound inspection differ mechanically from forward proxy?
    No impersonation. You import the server's real certificate and private key; the firewall decrypts passively.
  6. Why can ephemeral key exchange defeat inbound inspection?
    With DHE or ECDHE the session key is not derivable from the server private key, so the firewall must proxy instead.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Forward proxy = outbound, two TLS sessions, certificate generated on the fly.
  • Forward Trust for valid server certs, Forward Untrust for invalid ones.
  • Both must be flagged Certificate Authority.
  • Subordinate CA from your enterprise root removes the distribution step entirely.
  • Inbound inspection = import the real certificate and key, no impersonation.
  • Ephemeral key exchange defeats passive inbound decryption.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Distributing the untrust CA and silently killing browser warnings.
  • Self signing the forward trust CA when an enterprise PKI exists.
  • Forgetting to reimport a renewed server certificate for inbound inspection.
  • Expecting inbound inspection to work with ECDHE.
  • Omitting the Certificate Authority flag on a forward certificate.
SSL Forward ProxySSL Inbound Inspection
DirectionOutbound: your users outInbound: internet to your servers
Certificate handlingGenerated on the flyReal certificate and key imported
Needs CA distribution?Yes, unless subordinate CANo

Part 8 · Certificates and Decryption

Policy, Profiles, Exclusions and SSH Proxy

Deciding what to decrypt, controlling how, and handling the traffic that must be left alone. Exclusions are not a workaround. They are part of the design.

Mode
Decryption policy at Policies › Decryption decides which traffic is decrypted. Like every rulebase it is top down, first match.
Match criteria: source and destination zone and address, source user, service, and URL category. URL category is the important one. It is how you exclude finance and health categories for privacy or legal reasons while decrypting everything else.
Rule types: SSL Forward Proxy, SSL Inbound Inspection, SSH Proxy, and No Decrypt.
SSH ProxyDecrypts SSH to detect SSH tunnelling: port forwarding and X11 forwarding used to smuggle other protocols inside an allowed SSH session. It does not inspect the shell session content for threats; the purpose is to identify and control tunnelled traffic.
No certificates are needed for SSH proxy: the firewall generates its own keys. But clients will see a changed host key, which needs communicating in advance or it looks exactly like an attack.
Verify with show session all filter ssl-decrypt yes and show counter global filter aspect ssl.
In practiceBuild the decryption rulebase as explicit No Decrypt rules first, then a broad decrypt rule. That way the exclusions are visible at the top rather than buried, and a reviewer can see exactly what is not inspected.
A decryption profile attaches to a decryption rule and controls how decryption behaves. It has three sections.
SSL DecryptionForward Proxy checks: block sessions with expired certificates, untrusted issuers, unknown certificate status, timed out revocation checks, unsupported cipher suites or versions, and client authentication (mutual TLS).
Inbound Inspection checks: unsupported modes and versions.
No Decryption checks: even on traffic you do not decrypt, you can still block expired or untrusted certificates: a useful control people forget exists.
SSH ProxyBlock on unsupported version or algorithm, and on SSH errors.
Protocol SettingsMinimum and maximum TLS version, allowed key exchange, encryption and authentication algorithms. This is where you refuse weak cryptography outright.
The No Decryption section is worth calling out: it lets you enforce certificate hygiene on excluded traffic without reading it.
In practiceApply a decryption profile to your No Decrypt rules too. Just because you are not inspecting the payload does not mean you should accept an expired or untrusted certificate.

What must be excluded, and the three ways to do it exclusions are part of the design, not a failure of it

Certificate pinning is an application that embeds the expected server certificate or its issuer and rejects anything else, including your forward proxy certificate, even though clients trust your CA. Banking apps, many mobile apps and some software updaters do this. There is no fix; you must exclude them.
Mutual TLS means the client presents its own certificate. A proxy cannot reproduce the client's private key, so the handshake fails. Also excluded.
Three exclusion mechanismsNo Decrypt rule, by zone, address, user or URL category.
SSL Decryption Exclusion list at Device › Certificate Management › SSL Decryption Exclusion, by hostname, for specific applications.
Predefined exclusions: Palo Alto maintains a list of known broken applications, updated with content.
Legal and privacy exclusions are a separate category and are usually done by URL category in a No Decrypt rule, which is auditable and easy to justify.
In practiceCheck the predefined exclusion list before writing your own. Most of the applications that break under decryption are already on it, and reproducing it by hand is wasted effort that goes stale.
Both solve the same problem: other security tools also want to see the plaintext, and you do not want each one decrypting independently.
Decryption Broker makes the firewall the single decryption point and forwards the decrypted traffic to one or more inline third party security chains, then reencrypts on the way back out. The firewall becomes the decryption service for the whole stack.
That removes duplicated decryption cost, gives you one place to manage certificates and exclusions, and means the other tools do not each need their own CA in the trust store.
Decryption Mirroring is simpler and passive: a copy of the decrypted traffic is sent out a dedicated Decrypt Mirror interface to a DLP system, IDS or packet capture appliance. It is a tap, not a chain, the mirrored copy cannot block anything.
Decrypt Mirror requires a supporting platform and a free licence, and carries obvious privacy implications since you are exporting plaintext.
In practiceThe distinction: broker is inline and can block; mirroring is passive and can only observe. Same as the tap versus inline logic from the interfaces part.

✓ Check yourself policy, profiles and exclusions

  1. Name the four decryption rule types.
    SSL Forward Proxy, SSL Inbound Inspection, SSH Proxy, No Decrypt.
  2. What is SSH Proxy actually for?
    Detecting SSH tunnelling: port and X11 forwarding smuggling protocols inside an allowed SSH session.
  3. Why does certificate pinning have no workaround?
    The app embeds the expected certificate and rejects any substitute, even from a trusted CA. You must exclude it.
  4. Name the three exclusion mechanisms.
    No Decrypt rule, the SSL Decryption Exclusion list by hostname, and Palo Alto's predefined exclusions.
  5. What can a decryption profile still enforce on No Decrypt traffic?
    Certificate hygiene: block expired and untrusted certificates without reading the payload.
  6. Broker versus mirroring?
    Broker is inline and can block; mirroring is passive and can only observe.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Decryption rule types: Forward Proxy, Inbound Inspection, SSH Proxy, No Decrypt.
  • Match on zone, address, user, service and URL category.
  • Profile sections: SSL Decryption, SSH Proxy, Protocol Settings.
  • The No Decryption section enforces certificate checks without reading payload.
  • Pinning and mutual TLS cannot be proxied: exclude them.
  • Broker = inline chain. Mirroring = passive copy.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Trying to "fix" certificate pinning instead of excluding it.
  • Leaving No Decrypt rules with no decryption profile attached.
  • Hand writing exclusions already in the predefined list.
  • Enabling SSH proxy without warning users about the changed host key.
  • Expecting mirrored traffic to block anything.
Exclusion methodGranularityMaintained byBest for
No Decrypt ruleZone, address, user, URL categoryYouPrivacy and legal categories
SSL Decryption ExclusionHostnameYouA specific broken application
Predefined exclusionsHostnamePalo Alto, via contentKnown broken apps, check first

Part 8 · Certificates and Decryption

Best Practices and Troubleshooting

How to roll decryption out without a support queue, and how to diagnose it when a single site fails. Both are named course sections.

Mode
Decryption breaks more things than any other feature, so the rollout method matters as much as the configuration.
1 · Prepare the trustUse a subordinate CA signed by your enterprise root if you have one. If not, distribute the self signed forward trust CA by GPO or MDM first, and account for the trust stores that are not Windows: macOS, Linux, Java, Python, mobile devices, appliances.
2 · Start narrowBegin with a pilot group and a limited URL category set, not everything at once. Exclude privacy sensitive categories from the outset so you never have to explain why you read them.
3 · Expand by categoryAdd categories in tranches, watching for breakage after each. Applications fail loudly and specifically, so incremental expansion localises the cause.
4 · Size for itDecryption is CPU intensive. Check show running resource-monitor as coverage grows, and know the platform's decryption throughput figure rather than its App-ID one.
Standing practicesCheck the predefined exclusion list before hand writing exclusions · attach a decryption profile to No Decrypt rules · set a minimum TLS version and refuse weak algorithms · keep exclusions documented and reviewed, since a temporary exclusion becomes permanent by default.
In practiceThe failure mode of a rushed rollout is a support queue full of unrelated looking complaints. Pilot group, tranches by category, and communicate before enabling, particularly for SSH proxy, where the changed host key looks like an attack.

One site fails under decryption work down this list rather than disabling the rule

The Decryption log at Monitor › Logs › Decryption is the starting point. It records each decrypted session with the error reason, the TLS version and cipher suite, and the certificate details, which usually names the problem outright.
Useful commands:
show session all filter ssl-decrypt yes, which sessions are being decrypted
show counter global filter aspect ssl: decryption counters and failure reasons
show system setting ssl-decrypt certificate: the forward certificates in use
debug dataplane show ssl-decrypt ssl-stats: decryption statistics
The common causes, in the order they occurPinning or mutual TLS: excluded, not fixed.
Client does not trust the forward trust CA: check that device's own trust store.
A profile check is blocking: expired, untrusted issuer, unsupported cipher or version.
Inbound certificate renewed and not reimported.
Resource exhaustion: decryption CPU maxed, showing as intermittent failures.
Fix with a targeted exclusion for the affected hostname or category, never by disabling the rule for everyone. Same discipline as threat exceptions.
In practice"It works on my laptop but not on the tablet" is a trust store problem, not a firewall one. Non Windows devices and applications with their own bundled trust stores are where decryption rollouts actually stumble.
The course treats network design as its own topic, and the question it answers is where in the path decryption should happen.
One controlled inspection point is the goal. Decrypting in several places multiplies CPU cost, certificate management and exclusion lists, and creates places where plaintext exists that nobody is tracking.
Design consequences worth knowing:
Place decryption where the traffic is aggregated: typically the internet edge for outbound, and in front of the server farm for inbound.
Avoid asymmetric paths. Decryption requires the firewall to see both directions of a session; asymmetric routing breaks it outright.
Account for the doubled session cost: forward proxy maintains two TLS sessions per client session.
Virtual wire and Layer 2 deployments can decrypt, so transparent insertion does not rule it out.
Where other tools need plaintext, use decryption broker rather than letting each tool decrypt for itself, the design argument for the single inspection point again.
In practiceAsymmetry is the design trap. A path that works for stateful inspection may still break decryption, because seeing one direction of a TLS session is useless.

✓ Check yourself best practices and troubleshooting

  1. What is the first question when one site fails under decryption?
    Is it pinning or mutual TLS? If so there is no fix, exclude it.
  2. Which log names the error, cipher and certificate?
    The Decryption log at Monitor › Logs › Decryption.
  3. Which command shows decryption counters and failure reasons?
    show counter global filter aspect ssl.
  4. "Works on my laptop, not on the tablet." What is it?
    A trust store problem on that device, not a firewall fault. Non Windows and app bundled stores are the usual gap.
  5. Why does asymmetric routing break decryption specifically?
    Decryption needs both directions of the session. Seeing one half is useless, even though stateful inspection might cope.
  6. Why prefer a single decryption point?
    Decrypting in several places multiplies CPU, certificate management and exclusions, and spreads plaintext nobody tracks.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Roll out by pilot group, then category tranches.
  • Non Windows and app bundled trust stores are the usual failure.
  • The Decryption log gives error, TLS version, cipher and certificate.
  • show counter global filter aspect ssl for failure reasons.
  • Decryption needs both directions: asymmetry breaks it.
  • Forward proxy holds two TLS sessions per client session.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Disabling the decryption rule to fix one site.
  • Distributing the CA to Windows only.
  • Enabling everything at once instead of by tranche.
  • Letting temporary exclusions become permanent undocumented ones.
  • Decrypting in several places instead of brokering from one.
SymptomLikely causeAction
One app fails, others fineCertificate pinningAdd to the SSL Decryption Exclusion list
Handshake fails with a client certMutual TLSNo Decrypt rule
Warnings on every site, one deviceTrust store missing the CADeploy the CA to that platform

Part 8 · Certificates and Decryption

Detecting and Decrypting Post Quantum Traffic

Everything on the rest of this page applies unchanged to PQC, the same two decryption modes, the same profiles. What's new is a way to know PQC is in use without decrypting, and a way to give it to clients without upgrading servers.

Mode
This is a genuinely different capability from everything else on this page: these signatures identify which cryptographic algorithm a session negotiated, without needing decryption to succeed or even be attempted. Content release version 8692, March 2023, added 25 new vulnerability signatures for exactly this.
What the signatures actually detectTwo families, all category info leak, default action alert, minimum PAN-OS 9.1.0:
  • Pure post quantum ciphers: signatures for Crystals-Kyber, BIKE, FrodoKEM, HQC detected in use.
  • Hybrid classical plus PQ combinations: e.g. ECDH and Crystals-Kyber, ECDH and HQC, ECDH and BIKE, ECDH and FrodoKEM detected together in the same negotiation.
  • Signature algorithms too: Crystals-Dilithium, Falcon, SPHINCS-Plus, and their hybrid pairings with ECDSA or RSA.
Why this matters even before any PQC policy existsThis is the concrete answer to the unsanctioned PQC problem raised back in Part 1: a signature firing on "Use of Post Quantum Cyphers ... detected" tells you a session used PQC whether or not you sanctioned it, purely from observable handshake behaviour, no decryption required.
What it looks like in the logsA Threat log entry names the exact cipher, e.g. Use of Post Quantum Cyphers Crystals-Kyber detected, severity informational. Cross referencing the matching Decryption log for the same session shows the mechanics directly: TLS 1.3, key exchange algorithm kyber512, authentication algorithm SHA384: the signature and the decryption log corroborate each other.
In practiceThese signatures work on traffic you are not decrypting at all. That's the entire point. A no-decrypt policy with logging enabled, paired with these signatures, is how you inventory PQC usage across an estate before deciding on any decryption or migration policy.
Decrypting PQC traffic follows exactly the same principles as decrypting standard traffic, the same two modes from earlier on this page apply unchanged:
  • SSL Forward Proxy secures known clients and host machines going out to the internet, HQ, branch, mobile, remote users.
  • SSL Inbound Inspection secures data center and cloud hosted applications from unknown clients arriving from the internet.
Hybrid PQC forward proxy, walked throughAn internal client establishes a session with the firewall negotiating a hybrid PQC cipher, e.g. x25519_mlkem768: classical X25519 combined with post quantum ML-KEM-768. The firewall decrypts to apply policy, then opens a separate, new connection to the actual external server, also negotiating x25519_mlkem768. Two distinct hybrid PQC sessions, managed independently, protect the entire path against both classical and quantum threats while the firewall keeps full visibility.
Cipher translation for PQC specificallyThis is the genuinely new trick: on inbound inspection, the firewall can decrypt external client traffic using the hybrid post quantum cipher the client offered, then reencrypt to the internal server using only the server's own classical cipher: X25519 alone, no PQC involved on that leg. External clients get quantum resistant protection immediately, with zero upgrade required on internal servers, enabling a genuinely phased migration rather than a synchronised cutover.
Where this is configuredThe Decryption Profile's SSL Protocol Settings tab gets a new Key Exchange Algorithms section, alongside the existing Classical one:
  • PQC-Standard and PQC-Experimental: separate checkboxes, enabled independently.
  • Preferred Session Types: Post Quantum SSL preferred for the client side session, and separately for the server side session.
One operational detail worth knowing: for unsupported modes or failures, session information is cached for 12 hours, so a repeat connection between the same host and server pair during that window is not decrypted again: check those boxes to force blocking those sessions instead if that caching behaviour isn't wanted.
In practiceCipher translation is the detail worth having ready: a firewall can offer PQC to the outside world today without touching a single internal server, because the PQC leg and the classical leg are two separate, independently managed sessions either side of the firewall, the same forward proxy and inbound inspection pattern already covered, just carrying a different cipher on one side.

✓ Check yourself PQC detection and decryption

  1. Do the PQC threat signatures require decryption to succeed?
    No. They identify the negotiated algorithm from observable handshake behaviour alone.
  2. What content release version added the PQC detection signatures, and when?
    Version 8692, March 2023: 25 new vulnerability signatures.
  3. In the hybrid PQC forward proxy example, how many separate hybrid PQC sessions does the firewall manage?
    Two: client to firewall and firewall to server, independently negotiated.
  4. What does cipher translation let a firewall do for inbound PQC traffic?
    Decrypt the external hybrid PQC session, then reencrypt to the internal server using only its classical cipher, no server upgrade needed.
  5. Where are PQC-Standard and PQC-Experimental configured?
    The Decryption Profile's SSL Protocol Settings, under Key Exchange Algorithms.
  6. What happens to session information for unsupported PQC modes or failures?
    It's cached for 12 hours: a repeat connection in that window is not decrypted again unless blocking is explicitly configured.

✓ Anchor facts

  • PQC threat signatures work on undecrypted traffic, behavioural detection only.
  • Signatures cover pure PQC (Kyber, BIKE, FrodoKEM, HQC) and hybrid classical+PQ combos.
  • Hybrid forward proxy = two independent hybrid PQC sessions either side of the firewall.
  • Cipher translation: external PQC in, internal classical out, no server upgrade.
  • PQC-Standard / PQC-Experimental are separate, independent checkboxes on the Decryption Profile.
  • Unsupported mode sessions cache for 12 hours before reevaluation.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Assuming PQC detection requires a decrypt policy. It doesn't.
  • Thinking cipher translation means the internal server also runs PQC. It never has to.
  • Missing that client side and server side PQC preference are configured separately.
  • Forgetting the 12 hour cache can mask a fix until it expires or is explicitly cleared.
CapabilityNeeds decryption?Configured on
PQC threat signaturesNoVulnerability Protection Profile
Hybrid PQC forward proxyYesDecryption Policy + Profile
Cipher translationYes (inbound)Decryption Profile, SSL Inbound Inspection
PQC-Standard / ExperimentalN/A, toggleDecryption Profile › SSL Protocol Settings

Part 9 · Authentication and Identity

Server Profiles, Authentication Profiles and Sequences

This page covers roles, profiles and sequences together. They stack in a specific order, and knowing which layer does what makes every later identity topic easier.

Mode
Three layers, each wrapping the one below. Getting them in order is most of the difficulty here.
1 · Server profile: where to askAt Device › Server Profiles. Defines how to reach an authentication server: addresses, port, timeouts and any bind credentials. Types are LDAP, RADIUS, TACACS+, Kerberos, SAML Identity Provider and the local database.
2 · Authentication profile: how to askAt Device › Authentication Profile. References one server profile and adds the logic around it: the allow list of permitted users and groups, username modifiers (whether to strip or add a domain), account lockout settings, a certificate profile where certificates are involved, and MFA factors.
3 · Authentication sequence: who to ask, in orderAn ordered list of authentication profiles, tried top down until one succeeds. This is how you support several identity sources from one login prompt, try the primary directory first, then a secondary, then local.
The behaviour worth memorisingA sequence moves to the next profile when a server is unreachable. Whether it moves on after an explicit reject depends on the configuration, so the order should be deliberate rather than arbitrary, put the authoritative source first.
Verify with test authentication authentication-profile <name> username <user> password, which is the fastest way to prove a profile works before wiring it into anything.
In practiceRemember it as where, how, who in what order. Server profile = where the server is. Authentication profile = how to use it. Sequence = which profiles to try and in what order.

The three layers, and the protocols underneath each wraps the one below it

LDAP: port 389, or 636 for LDAPS. Used both for authentication and, importantly, as the source for group mapping. A bind account is needed to read the directory.
RADIUS: UDP 1812 for authentication and 1813 for accounting. Encrypts only the password, not the rest of the packet. Commonly fronts MFA systems, and can return vendor specific attributes to assign an admin role.
TACACS+: TCP 49, and encrypts the entire payload. It separates authentication, authorisation and accounting, which is why it is preferred for device administration where per command authorisation matters.
Kerberos: ticket based against a KDC, used for single sign on. This is what Orion's passwordless feature builds on, with the firewall acting as a KCD agent.
SAML: browser redirect federation to an identity provider. The firewall is the service provider; the IdP handles the actual authentication and any MFA. Now the common choice for GlobalProtect and administrator access, because MFA and conditional access live at the IdP rather than on the firewall.
In practiceThe pair worth stating precisely: RADIUS encrypts only the password over UDP; TACACS+ encrypts the whole payload over TCP 49. That plus AAA separation is why TACACS+ wins for device administration.
Administrators are configured at Device › Administrators, authenticating against the local database, an authentication profile, or a certificate profile for certificate based login.
Role typesDynamic roles are built in and maintained by PAN-OS: superuser, superuser (read only), device administrator, device administrator (read only), and on multi-vsys platforms vsys administrator and its read only form.
Admin Role profiles are custom, letting you enable or disable each individual web interface tab, CLI access level and XML API capability. This is how you give a team visibility into logs without configuration rights.
Remote authentication can carry the role: RADIUS vendor specific attributes, TACACS+ attributes, or SAML attributes can map a user directly to an admin role, so role assignment lives in the directory rather than on each firewall.
Hardening around this: set minimum password complexity and failed attempt lockout, restrict management access by permitted IP, and enable Panorama based authentication so leavers are removed centrally.
In practiceCustom Admin Role profiles are what make least privilege real on a firewall. Everyone being superuser is the default drift, and it is the first thing an audit picks up.

✓ Check yourself the authentication stack

  1. Name the three layers and what each one defines.
    Server profile = where the server is. Authentication profile = how to use it. Sequence = which profiles to try, in order.
  2. When does a sequence move to the next profile?
    When a server is unreachable. Behaviour on explicit reject depends on configuration, order deliberately.
  3. RADIUS versus TACACS+ in one line.
    RADIUS encrypts only the password over UDP 1812/1813; TACACS+ encrypts the whole payload over TCP 49 and separates AAA.
  4. Which protocol is preferred for device administration, and why?
    TACACS+: full payload encryption plus AAA separation for per command authorisation.
  5. What is the firewall's role in SAML, and what does that buy you?
    The firewall is the service provider; the IdP authenticates. MFA and conditional access live at the IdP, not on the firewall.
  6. How do you grant log visibility without configuration rights?
    A custom Admin Role profile enabling only the required tabs, CLI level and API capabilities.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Stack order: server profile → authentication profile → sequence.
  • LDAP 389 / 636 · RADIUS UDP 1812 / 1813 · TACACS+ TCP 49.
  • RADIUS = password only. TACACS+ = full payload.
  • Sequences are tried top down, next on unreachable.
  • Dynamic roles are built in; Admin Role profiles are custom and granular.
  • RADIUS VSAs, TACACS+ and SAML attributes can carry the admin role.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Confusing which layer holds the allow list: it is the authentication profile.
  • Putting a fallback source first in a sequence.
  • Using RADIUS for device administration where per command authorisation is needed.
  • Leaving every administrator as superuser.
  • Forgetting username modifiers when the directory expects a domain prefix.
ProtocolTransport and portEncryptsBest for
LDAPTCP 389 / 636TLS if LDAPSAuth and group mapping
RADIUSUDP 1812 / 1813Password onlyMFA front end, user auth

Part 9 · Authentication and Identity

Authentication Policy, MFA and Passwordless

Authentication profiles say how to authenticate. Authentication policy decides when to demand it, and passwordless removes the credential from the exchange entirely.

Mode
Authentication Policy at Policies › Authentication triggers an authentication challenge for traffic that matches, which serves two purposes: identifying users User-ID could not map, and demanding step up authentication before sensitive resources.
It matches on source and destination zone and address, service, URL category, and references an Authentication Enforcement object, which in turn points at an authentication profile and a method.
Methodsbrowser challenge: transparent NTLM or Kerberos, no prompt if the browser can respond.
web form: an explicit login page.
no-captive-portal: matched traffic bypasses the challenge.
The authentication portal (historically captive portal) is configured at Device › User Identification › Authentication Portal Settings with a mode of redirect or transparent. Redirect mode is preferred because it supports authentication cookies and works far better with modern browsers.
A successful challenge creates a user to IP mapping, so it feeds User-ID rather than being separate from it. The timeout controls how long before the user is challenged again.
In practiceStep up is the more interesting use. You allow a finance application generally but demand fresh MFA at the point of access, rather than trusting a login that happened at 8am.
PAN-OS integrates MFA two ways, and the distinction matters.
Through RADIUS or SAML: the firewall simply authenticates against a provider that happens to enforce MFA. The firewall knows nothing about the factors. This is the common path for GlobalProtect and administrator access.
Native MFA integration: an MFA Server Profile at Device › Server Profiles › MFA, with vendor integrations, attached to an authentication profile. Up to four factors can be chained, and this path is what Authentication Policy uses for step up challenges inside the network.
The distinction: RADIUS and SAML MFA covers access to the firewall or VPN; native MFA with Authentication Policy covers access to resources through the firewall, which is where step up belongs.
Design notes: exempt service accounts and machine traffic that cannot respond to a challenge, and remember MFA cannot apply to non browser traffic that has no way to present a prompt.
In practice"MFA on the VPN" and "MFA before the finance app" are different mechanisms. The first is RADIUS or SAML at the gateway; the second is Authentication Policy with an MFA profile.

Two different MFA questions getting into the network, versus reaching a resource inside it

Introduced with PAN-OS 12.1 Orion and described as phishing resistant, for a straightforward reason: a credential that is never typed into an application cannot be phished from it or replayed.
The mechanismPasswordless Authentication for Kerberos Protected Applications. The firewall acts as a Kerberos Constrained Delegation (KCD) agent, obtaining a Kerberos ticket on the user's behalf so they reach Kerberos protected enterprise applications without repeatedly entering credentials.
Two benefits follow. Security: fewer password related vulnerabilities, because individual applications are not handling credentials. Usability: access to multiple Kerberos protected applications is streamlined behind a single authentication event.
It complements rather than replaces the rest of this part. You still need authentication profiles, you may still use SAML for GlobalProtect, and MFA still applies at the initial authentication.
The connection worth drawingPair it with User Credential Detection in URL Filtering. One feature stops users typing corporate credentials into external sites; passwordless removes the credential from the flow altogether. Two angles on the same problem.
In practiceKnow the shape rather than the configuration: the firewall gets the ticket for the user via KCD. That one sentence covers what the course section is teaching.
Certificates authenticate without a password, which is why they underpin most large GlobalProtect deployments and hardened administrator access.
The firewall validates a presented certificate using a certificate profile, which names the trusted CAs, enables CRL or OCSP revocation checking, and defines how to extract the username from a certificate field, typically subject, common name or a SAN.
For administrators: assign a certificate profile to the admin account so login requires the certificate rather than a password.
For GlobalProtect: machine or user certificates prove the device is corporate managed. Combined with SAML for the user, this gives you device identity plus user identity, which is the usual Zero Trust posture.
At scale the issuing problem dominates, which is why SCEP matters, certificates are enrolled and renewed automatically from the enterprise CA rather than by hand.
In practiceSet revocation checking to block on unknown status deliberately. Leaving it permissive means a revoked certificate keeps working, which defeats the point of having revocation at all.

✓ Check yourself authentication policy and MFA

  1. What two purposes does Authentication Policy serve?
    Identifying users User-ID could not map, and step up authentication before sensitive resources.
  2. Name the three authentication methods and which portal mode is preferred.
    browser challenge, web form, no-captive-portal. Redirect mode is preferred. It supports cookies and modern browsers.
  3. Distinguish the two MFA integration paths.
    RADIUS/SAML covers getting into the firewall or VPN. Native MFA with Authentication Policy covers reaching resources through it.
  4. How many factors can a native MFA profile chain?
    Up to four.
  5. What is the firewall's role in Orion's passwordless feature?
    A Kerberos Constrained Delegation (KCD) agent, obtaining a ticket on the user's behalf.
  6. Why set revocation checking to block on unknown status?
    Otherwise a revoked certificate keeps working, which defeats having revocation at all.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Authentication Policy triggers a challenge; success creates a user to IP mapping.
  • Methods: browser challenge, web form, no-captive-portal.
  • Portal modes: redirect (preferred) or transparent.
  • Native MFA chains up to four factors.
  • Passwordless = firewall as KCD agent for Kerberos protected apps.
  • Certificate profiles extract the username from a certificate field.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Conflating VPN MFA with step up MFA: different mechanisms.
  • Applying Authentication Policy to traffic that cannot present a prompt.
  • Using transparent portal mode with modern browsers.
  • Leaving revocation checking permissive.
  • Issuing GlobalProtect certificates by hand rather than via SCEP.
RequirementMechanismWhere configured
MFA to connect the VPNRADIUS or SAML providerAuthentication profile on the GP gateway
MFA before a sensitive appAuthentication Policy + MFA profilePolicies › Authentication

Part 9 · Authentication and Identity

User-ID Mapping Methods

User-ID turns an IP address into a username so policy can be written about people. Group mapping, user to IP mapping and user context all start here.

Mode
User-ID does two separate jobs, and conflating them is the usual source of confusion.
1 · User to IP mappingLearning that 10.1.1.50 is currently domain\alice. This is dynamic, changes constantly, and is what lets a security rule match a user.
2 · Group mappingLearning that alice is in the Finance group. This comes from LDAP, is relatively static, and is what lets a rule reference a group rather than individuals.
Both must work for a group based rule to function. A rule for "Finance" fails if the user is mapped but the group membership is not, and equally if the groups are known but the IP is unmapped.
Where mappings are usedSecurity policy as a match condition · logs and reports, so the Traffic log shows a name instead of an address · Authentication Policy · QoS and PBF, which can both match on user.
Enable User-ID per zone, and use the zone's include and exclude lists to constrain which subnets are mapped. Enabling it on an untrusted zone is a well known bad practice. You do not want to accept mapping data from outside.
Verify with show user ip-user-mapping all, show user group list, and show user group name "cn=finance,...".
In practiceWhen a user based rule misbehaves, establish which half is broken first: show user ip-user-mapping ip <addr> for the mapping, show user group name for membership. They fail independently and have different fixes.

The mapping methods, and what each one can actually see most estates need several

Security log monitoring: reads domain controller security event logs for logon events. The classic Windows method. Covers domain joined devices and misses everything else.
GlobalProtect: the user authenticated to the firewall, so the mapping is authoritative. The most reliable source available, and it solves remote users completely.
Syslog listener: parses login events from any system that logs them: 802.1X and NAC, wireless controllers, VPN concentrators, proxies, Linux hosts. Needs a syslog parse profile defining the regex and fields. This is how you cover non Windows.
XML API: a script or system posts mappings directly. Useful for bespoke sources and automation.
Authentication portal: challenge the user and map on success, as covered on the previous page.
Client probing (WMI and NetBIOS), the firewall or agent probes endpoints. Deprecated in practice: noisy, slow, and a security concern because it sends credentials to endpoints. Avoid it.
Terminal Services agent: where many users share one IP, mapping is done by source port range instead of address.
In practiceReal estates use several methods at once: security log monitoring for the Windows fleet, GlobalProtect for remote users, and a syslog listener for everything else. Client probing should not be one of them.
Two deployment models for reading domain controller logs, and the choice is about scale.
Windows User-ID agent: software installed on a Windows server that reads DC logs and sends mappings to the firewall. Suits large environments and many domain controllers, because one agent serves many firewalls and the load sits off the firewall.
Agentless (PAN-OS integrated): the firewall reads the domain controller logs itself, with no software to deploy. Simpler, and the usual choice for smaller environments or a handful of DCs. The cost is management plane load on the firewall.
Either way the account used needs the rights to read security event logs, and configuration lives at Device › User Identification.
Two related agents worth naming: the Terminal Services agent for shared IP environments, and the credential enforcement component supporting User Credential Detection in URL Filtering.
Check with show user user-id-agent statistics and show user server-monitor statistics.
In practiceThe scaling rule: agentless for a few domain controllers, agent when there are many. Agentless is simpler but every DC it monitors is work the firewall's management plane is doing instead of something else.

✓ Check yourself User-ID mapping

  1. Name the two halves of User-ID and how each fails.
    User to IP mapping (dynamic, who is at this address) and group mapping (static, from LDAP). A group rule needs both.
  2. Which mapping method is the most authoritative, and why?
    GlobalProtect: the user authenticated to the firewall directly, so there is no inference.
  3. How do you map users on non Windows systems?
    A syslog listener with a parse profile, reading login events from 802.1X, wireless, VPN or proxies.
  4. Why is client probing discouraged?
    Noisy, slow, and it sends credentials to endpoints: a security concern in its own right.
  5. How are users mapped on a terminal server?
    By source port range via the Terminal Services agent, because many users share one IP.
  6. When do you choose the agent over agentless?
    Many domain controllers or large scale. Agentless puts the load on the firewall's management plane.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Two halves: user to IP mapping and group mapping.
  • Group mapping comes from LDAP and is relatively static.
  • Methods: DC log monitoring, GlobalProtect, syslog, XML API, portal, probing, TS agent.
  • Terminal servers map by port range.
  • Enable User-ID per zone, never on an untrusted zone.
  • Agentless for few DCs, agent for many.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Assuming a user based rule failure is always the IP mapping.
  • Enabling User-ID on an untrusted zone.
  • Relying on DC log monitoring alone and missing non domain devices.
  • Leaving client probing enabled.
  • Expecting per user policy on a terminal server without the TS agent.
MethodCoversReliabilityNotes
Security log monitoringDomain joined WindowsGoodAgent or agentless
GlobalProtectRemote and mobile usersHighestUser authenticated to us
Syslog listenerNon Windows, NAC, wirelessGoodNeeds a parse profile
XML APIBespoke sourcesAs good as the sourceScripted or automated

Part 9 · Authentication and Identity

Group Mapping, Redistribution and User Context

How identity gets from the source to every firewall that needs it. Redistribution and segments is the part most often skipped.

Mode
Group mapping at Device › User Identification › Group Mapping Settings tells the firewall which groups a user belongs to, so policy can reference a group rather than a list of names.
It needs an LDAP server profile, a bind account with read rights, and a search base (a distinguished name such as dc=corp,dc=example,dc=com). You then choose an update interval: the default is one hour, since group membership changes slowly.
The include list matters at scaleBy default the firewall retrieves every group in the search base. In a large directory that is an enormous amount of data the firewall does not need. Use the group include list to retrieve only the groups actually referenced in policy.
Custom groups can be defined from an LDAP filter where the directory's own groups do not match how you want to write policy.
Two gotchas. Nested groups need the directory to be queried appropriately or members of child groups are missed. And primary group membership in Active Directory is stored differently from other memberships, so "Domain Users" frequently appears empty.
Verify with show user group list and show user group name "<dn>".
In practiceThe classic symptom is a rule for "Domain Users" matching nobody. That is primary group membership, not a broken mapping, reference a different group or adjust the mapping configuration.
User context is the broader picture the firewall holds about an identity, beyond the bare IP to name mapping: which groups the user is in, where the mapping came from and when, the device they are on, and their HIP posture if GlobalProtect is in use.
Richer context enables finer policy. Rather than "Finance can reach the finance app", you can write "Finance, on a compliant corporate device, during business hours", which is the practical shape of Zero Trust on a firewall.
PAN-OS 12.1 Orion raised the ceiling to 10 million user contexts, a 20x increase, specifically so user based policy stays usable in very large and cloud scale environments.
Device-IDThe parallel idea for devices rather than users. It identifies endpoints and lets policy match on device attributes (category, profile, vendor, model, OS) which matters for IoT and unmanaged equipment that will never have a user logged into it. Orion expanded Device-ID capabilities.
Together they answer the two questions policy actually wants: who is this, and what are they on.
In practiceDevice-ID is the answer for anything that cannot authenticate, cameras, sensors, printers, building systems. You cannot write user policy for a device with no user, but you can write device policy.

Redistribution and segments learn the mapping once, use it everywhere

A firewall that learns a user to IP mapping can redistribute it to other firewalls, so identity is learned once and used everywhere.
Why it existsThe domain controllers may only be reachable from some firewalls. Remote sites may have no local identity source at all. And you do not want every firewall polling every domain controller: that scales badly and loads the directory.
How it is configuredOn the sending device, enable redistribution under Device › Data Redistribution and choose what to share: IP-User mappings, IP-Tag and User-Tag mappings, HIP information, and quarantine list data.
On the receiving device, add the sender as an agent of type Redistribution. Panorama can act as a redistribution point, which is the usual design in a large estate.
Redistribution is hierarchical: a receiver can pass mappings on again, up to 10 hops. Keep the design shallow anyway; every hop adds delay before a mapping is usable.
Segments, the part people missA segment names a region or site, so mappings are scoped to where they came from. Without them, overlapping private address ranges collide: 10.1.1.50 in London and 10.1.1.50 in Frankfurt are different people, and the mappings would overwrite each other.
With segments the firewall keeps them distinct and applies the right identity in the right place. Any estate with repeated RFC 1918 ranges across sites needs this.
Verify with show user user-id-agent statistics and inspect a specific address with show user ip-user-mapping ip <addr>, which shows the source of the mapping.
In practiceSegments are the detail that separates someone who has read about redistribution from someone who has deployed it across sites. If two sites reuse the same private range, mappings without segments will actively give you the wrong username.

✓ Check yourself group mapping and redistribution

  1. A rule for "Domain Users" matches nobody. Why?
    Primary group membership is stored differently in Active Directory, so that group often appears empty.
  2. Why use a group include list?
    By default the firewall retrieves every group in the search base, in a large directory that is far more data than policy needs.
  3. Give three reasons redistribution exists.
    DCs are only reachable from some firewalls; remote sites have no local source; and you avoid every firewall polling every DC.
  4. What can be redistributed besides IP-User mappings?
    IP-Tag and User-Tag mappings, HIP data, and the quarantine list.
  5. How many redistribution hops are supported?
    Up to 10: but keep it shallow, since each hop delays when a mapping becomes usable.
  6. What problem do segments solve?
    Overlapping private ranges. Without them, the same IP at two sites collides and you get the wrong username.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Group mapping needs LDAP profile, bind account, search base; default update 1 hour.
  • Use a group include list at scale.
  • Redistribution shares IP-User, IP-Tag, User-Tag, HIP and quarantine data.
  • Panorama can be a redistribution point.
  • Hierarchical up to 10 hops.
  • Segments scope mappings so overlapping ranges do not collide.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Blaming group mapping for the Domain Users primary group behaviour.
  • Pulling every group in a large directory.
  • Missing nested group members.
  • Deploying redistribution across sites with no segments.
  • Building deep redistribution chains and wondering why mappings lag.
Redistributed dataCarriesUsed for
IP-User mappingWho is at an addressUser based policy and logs
IP-TagTags applied to addressesDynamic Address Groups, quarantine
User-TagTags applied to usersDynamic user groups
HIPEndpoint postureCompliance based rules
Quarantine listIsolated devicesConsistent isolation estate wide

Part 9 · Authentication and Identity

Cloud Identity Engine, SCIM and Health Alerts

The Identity course is built around the Cloud Identity Engine. It answers the problem on premises User-ID cannot: identity for users who never touch your network.

Mode
The Cloud Identity Engine (CIE) is a cloud delivered identity service that collects identity data centrally and makes it available to firewalls, Panorama and Prisma Access, without each device connecting to the directory itself.
The problem it solvesTraditional User-ID assumes users are on your network, authenticating against domain controllers you can reach. That assumption has broken down: users are remote, directories are in the cloud, and applications are SaaS. A firewall in a branch office may have no path to a domain controller at all.
CIE connects to the identity sources instead, Azure AD / Entra ID, Okta, PingOne, Google Workspace, and on premises Active Directory through a lightweight agent, and distributes the result.
What it providesDirectory sync for users, groups and attributes · authentication via SAML or OIDC through the cloud · a single consistent identity source across on premises firewalls, Prisma Access and Panorama.
The benefits follow from being cloud delivered: no directory connection per firewall, works for users who never touch the corporate network, and one place to configure identity rather than repeating it on every device.
It does not replace on premises User-ID. They coexist, and most real estates run both, CIE for cloud and remote identity, traditional User-ID for on network mapping from domain controller logs.
In practiceThe one line framing: on premises User-ID answers "who is at this IP on my network"; CIE answers "who is this user, wherever they are". Different questions, both usually needed.

Cloud Identity Engine identity collected once in the cloud, consumed everywhere

SCIM, System for Cross domain Identity Management, is a standard for automated user and group provisioning between an identity provider and a service.
The distinction that matters: SCIM is provisioning, not authentication. SAML authenticates a user at login. SCIM keeps the user and group data itself in sync, so when someone joins Finance or leaves the company, the change arrives without waiting for a scheduled sync.
It is push rather than pull, which is why it is faster and lighter than polling a directory on an interval, a leaver is removed promptly rather than at the next cycle.
The two management modesThe course covers CIE in two deployments.
Panorama Managed: CIE configured and consumed through Panorama, distributing identity to an on premises firewall estate.
Prisma Access Cloud Managed: CIE configured through the cloud management console, serving cloud delivered infrastructure.
The identity data and the sources are the same; what differs is where you configure it and which infrastructure consumes it. Estates running both on premises firewalls and Prisma Access encounter both.
In practiceRemember the pairing: SAML authenticates, SCIM provisions. They are complementary, SAML at login time, SCIM continuously in the background.
CIE health alerts notify you when identity collection degrades, a directory sync failing, an agent losing connectivity, or authentication errors rising.
They exist because identity failure is silent. When mappings go stale, traffic does not error: it simply stops matching user based rules and falls through to something more general, or gets denied for reasons nobody connects to identity. Without alerting you discover it from user complaints.
Preventing use of stolen credentialsA named course section, and the controls are ones already covered here, assembled into a strategy:
User Credential Detection in URL Filtering, blocking submission of corporate credentials to external sites.
MFA, so a stolen password alone is insufficient.
Passwordless and certificates, so there is no reusable credential to steal.
Authentication Policy for step up before sensitive resources.
Credential based Dynamic Address Group quarantine when a compromise is detected.
The through line: reduce what a stolen password gets you, and detect when one is being used.
In practiceSet the health alerts up when you deploy CIE, not after an incident. Stale identity is the failure mode you will not notice until policy has been quietly wrong for days.

✓ Check yourself Cloud Identity Engine

  1. What assumption of traditional User-ID has broken down?
    That users are on your network authenticating against reachable domain controllers. Remote users and cloud directories break it.
  2. Does CIE replace on premises User-ID?
    No: they coexist. CIE for cloud and remote identity, traditional User-ID for on network DC log mapping.
  3. SAML versus SCIM.
    SAML authenticates at login; SCIM provisions user and group data continuously by push rather than scheduled pull.
  4. Name the two CIE management modes.
    Panorama Managed for the on premises estate, and Prisma Access Cloud Managed via the cloud console.
  5. Why do health alerts matter specifically for identity?
    Identity failure is silent: traffic stops matching user rules rather than erroring, so you find out from complaints.
  6. List three controls that reduce the value of a stolen password.
    MFA, passwordless or certificates, and step up Authentication Policy, plus User Credential Detection to stop submission.

✓ Anchor facts

  • CIE sources: Entra ID, Okta, PingOne, Google Workspace, on prem AD via agent.
  • Provides directory sync and SAML/OIDC authentication from the cloud.
  • SCIM provisions; SAML authenticates.
  • Modes: Panorama Managed and Prisma Access Cloud Managed.
  • CIE does not replace on premises User-ID: they coexist.
  • Orion supports 10 million user contexts.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Treating CIE as a replacement for User-ID.
  • Confusing SCIM provisioning with SAML authentication.
  • Deploying CIE without health alerts.
  • Assuming stale identity produces errors. It produces wrong rule matches.
  • Relying on MFA alone while credentials are still submitted to external sites.
On premises User-IDCloud Identity Engine
AnswersWho is at this IP, on my networkWho is this user, wherever they are
SourceDC logs, GP, syslog, APICloud IdPs and on prem AD via agent
ConnectionEach firewall to the directoryCloud service, no per firewall link

Part 10 · VPN and Tunnels

IKE, IPSec and the Site to Site Build

IPSec, quantum resistant cryptography and GRE are covered here as equals. Start with the two phases every tunnel depends on, then the objects PAN-OS builds them from.

Mode

Read the topic, recall what you can, then reveal. Mark what's solid to track what still needs work. Controls act across every page.

IKE negotiates the keys that IPSec uses. It runs in two phases, and PAN-OS separates the configuration to match.
Phase 1, the IKE GatewayEstablishes a secure, authenticated channel between the two peers. Configured at Network › Network Profiles › IKE Gateway: peer IP or FQDN, local and peer identification, authentication (pre shared key or certificate), IKE version (IKEv1, IKEv2, or IKEv2 preferred with v1 fallback), and an IKE Crypto Profile.
The IKE Crypto Profile sets DH group, encryption algorithm, authentication (hash) algorithm, key lifetime, and an IKEv2 authentication multiple for rekey timing.
Phase 2, the IPSec TunnelNegotiates the actual data plane security association that encrypts traffic, running inside the Phase 1 channel. Configured at Network › IPSec Tunnels: the tunnel interface, the IKE Gateway to use, and an IPSec Crypto Profile setting ESP or AH, encryption, authentication and lifetime, plus Perfect Forward Secrecy via its own DH group.
IKEv1 versus IKEv2IKEv1 splits Phase 1 into Main Mode (6 messages, identity protected) or Aggressive Mode (3 messages, faster but exposes identity). IKEv2 collapses this into fewer exchanges, has built in NAT-T and dead peer detection, and is the current recommendation. It is also the version the quantum resistant options attach to.
Verify with show vpn ike-sa for Phase 1 and show vpn ipsec-sa for Phase 2, and troubleshoot with show vpn flow and the System log filtered to the ike subtype.
In practiceWhen a tunnel won't come up, check which phase failed first. No IKE SA means Phase 1 parameters mismatch, DH group, encryption, PSK, or peer ID. An IKE SA that exists but no IPSec SA points at Phase 2, proxy IDs or the IPSec crypto profile.

Two phases, two PAN-OS objects Phase 1 builds the channel; Phase 2 runs inside it

A tunnel interface is logical, with no physical port, assigned a zone and a router like any other interface. It is what routing and security policy actually reference, the IPSec tunnel object below it is the encryption machinery.
Route based versus policy based VPNPAN-OS is route based: traffic reaches the tunnel because a route points at the tunnel interface, and what gets encrypted is whatever the routing table sends there. Many other vendors, including older Check Point and some ASA configurations, are policy based: an access list defines exactly which traffic is interesting, and that definition is the encryption domain.
Proxy IDs bridge the two worldsWhen the far end is policy based, it expects a specific source and destination network pair as the encryption domain. Proxy IDs on the PAN-OS tunnel let you specify that pair explicitly, so the two mismatched models agree on what "interesting traffic" means.
The trap: a route based peer with no proxy ID configured sends a default 0.0.0.0/0 to 0.0.0.0/0 proxy ID, which many policy based devices reject outright, or accept and then encrypt far more than intended.
Multiple proxy ID pairs create multiple Phase 2 SAs over one Phase 1 SA, which is normal and expected when several subnets need to cross the same tunnel to a policy based peer.
In practiceIf Phase 1 comes up but Phase 2 never does against a specific vendor, check proxy IDs first. It is the most common route based to policy based mismatch, and it is invisible until you look for it.
Two separate mechanisms detect a failed VPN, operating at different layers.
Dead Peer Detection (DPD)An IKE layer keepalive. If the peer stops responding to DPD messages, the IKE and IPSec SAs are torn down. Configured on the IKE Gateway, with an interval and retry count. This detects a peer that has genuinely gone away.
Tunnel MonitoringA PAN-OS feature that sends ICMP probes across the tunnel itself to a destination IP on the far side, configured on the tunnel interface. It detects something DPD cannot: the IKE and IPSec SAs are still up, but traffic is not actually passing: a routing problem or an ACL on the far end blocking the probe's return path.
On failure, the action is wait-recover (keep retrying, tunnel interface stays up) or failover (bring the tunnel interface down so routing fails over to a backup path or route).
The design point: tunnel monitoring is what makes a backup VPN path actually useful. Without it, a tunnel with dead SAs but no traffic flowing would sit there looking healthy while a static route through it kept sending traffic into a black hole.
Verify with show vpn ike-sa, show vpn ipsec-sa, and show vpn flow tunnel-name <name> for tunnel monitor state specifically.
In practiceDPD answers "is the peer alive". Tunnel monitoring answers "is traffic actually getting through". A tunnel can pass DPD and still be useless, that gap is exactly what tunnel monitoring closes.

✓ Check yourself IKE, IPSec and proxy IDs

  1. Which PAN-OS object corresponds to IKE Phase 1, and which to Phase 2?
    IKE Gateway = Phase 1. IPSec Tunnel (with its IPSec Crypto Profile) = Phase 2, running inside the Phase 1 channel.
  2. A tunnel has no IKE SA. Which phase, and what would you check?
    Phase 1. DH group, encryption, PSK or certificate, and peer ID mismatch.
  3. Is PAN-OS route based or policy based, and what does that mean?
    Route based. Whatever the routing table sends to the tunnel interface gets encrypted; there is no access list defined encryption domain.
  4. What do proxy IDs solve?
    They let a route based PAN-OS tunnel agree with a policy based peer on the exact source/destination pair to encrypt.
  5. Distinguish DPD from tunnel monitoring.
    DPD (IKE layer) detects a dead peer. Tunnel monitoring (ICMP across the tunnel) detects SAs that are up but traffic not actually passing.
  6. What are the two tunnel monitor actions on failure?
    wait-recover (keep retrying) or failover (bring the interface down so routing fails over).

✓ Anchor facts

  • IKE Gateway = Phase 1. IPSec Tunnel = Phase 2, inside Phase 1.
  • PAN-OS is route based; the routing table decides what gets encrypted.
  • Proxy IDs bridge to policy based peers by naming the encryption domain.
  • No proxy ID configured defaults to 0.0.0.0/0 to 0.0.0.0/0.
  • DPD = is the peer alive. Tunnel monitoring = is traffic passing.
  • IKEv2 has built in NAT-T and DPD, and is the current recommendation.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Assuming a passed DPD means the tunnel is actually useful.
  • Forgetting proxy IDs when peering with a policy based device.
  • Confusing which show command checks which phase.
  • Leaving tunnel monitoring off a backup path that depends on failover.
  • Using Aggressive Mode where identity exposure matters.
DPDTunnel Monitoring
LayerIKEData plane, ICMP
DetectsPeer unreachableSAs up but traffic not passing
Configured onIKE GatewayTunnel interface
On failureTears down SAswait-recover or failover

Part 10 · VPN and Tunnels

GRE and Quantum Resistant Cryptography

GRE is the plain tunnelling protocol IPSec is layered onto for confidentiality; quantum resistant crypto is where Orion's headline theme actually attaches to a configuration.

Mode
GRE encapsulates one packet inside another so payloads that would not otherwise cross a network can be carried, multicast, non IP protocols, or simply a stable logical link across a path that changes underneath.
Configured at Network › GRE Tunnels: local and peer address, a tunnel interface, keepalive settings, and an optional copy ToS header option so QoS marking survives encapsulation.
The point worth stating plainlyGRE provides encapsulation. It provides no encryption and no authentication. Traffic inside a plain GRE tunnel is as visible on the wire as unencrypted traffic anywhere else.
The standard pattern is therefore GRE over IPSec: GRE does the encapsulation, carrying whatever needs carrying, and IPSec wraps around it for confidentiality and integrity. This is also how you get multicast routing protocols across a VPN, since plain IPSec alone will not carry multicast, but GRE will, and IPSec can then protect the GRE tunnel.
Protocol number 47, and it adds roughly 24 bytes of overhead, worth accounting for in path MTU, same discipline as the MSS clamping covered on the interfaces page.
In practiceIf a design needs multicast, dynamic routing protocols, or non IP traffic across a VPN, the answer is GRE over IPSec, not IPSec alone. State that GRE alone is unencrypted before anyone has to ask.
This is where Orion's harvest now, decrypt later concern (covered in Part 1) becomes an actual tunnel setting rather than a slide.
RFC 8784, Post Quantum Pre Shared Keys, the actual mechanismRFC 8784 adds an extra round to key generation, not a replacement for the existing one:
  • Performed after the normal IKEv2 Diffie-Hellman key exchange, as an additional round.
  • Mixes in a static PPK string alongside the DH derived SKEYSEED.
  • The resulting key is not based on primes, so it is not vulnerable to Shor's algorithm even if the DH portion eventually is.
What the PPK itself actually isThe security of the whole scheme rests on this one secret:
  • 32 to 128 characters (16 to 64 bytes), a genuinely complex secret, not a passphrase.
  • A strong random string, e.g. a hex value, not something a person composes.
  • Never sent in the IKEv2 exchange itself, so it cannot be intercepted on the wire.
  • A separate PPK KEYID is sent as a reference, telling the peer which PPK to use without revealing the PPK itself.
RFC 8784 isn't perfectThree real limitations, and they're operational, not cryptographic:
  • Not scalable: manually configuring PPKs gets genuinely tough past roughly 25 peers.
  • The PPK must stay secret, and a leaked or lost one significantly raises risk. You can control your own staff; you cannot control a partner's employees who also hold the same PPK.
  • Humans are bad at this: a PPK needs high entropy (minimum 32 characters) and must resist a dictionary attack, which makes it hard to remember, and a genuinely tough PPK is exactly the kind that gets written down somewhere and eventually lost or leaked.
RFC 9242 and RFC 9370, advancing to hybrid KEM solutionsThese exist specifically as the precursor to PQ IKEv2 hybrid keys, and RFC 8784 can be layered with them during the PQC transition for added insurance rather than one replacing the other.
  • Together they're IKEv2 enhancements supporting key exchange with large amounts of data: something the original IKEv2 exchange wasn't sized for.
  • They add up to 7 additional key exchange rounds on top of the classic one.
What actually runs in those roundsThe classic round is Diffie-Hellman, Group 21. The additional PQ KEM rounds, in order, are named directly:
  • Round 1: ML-KEM, "Kyber".
  • Round 2: BIKE.
  • Round 3: HQC.
  • … up to Round 7: KEM X.
Best practiceis one classic round (DH) plus one or more PQC rounds, not seven by default. Each additional round buys crypto agility (if one KEM is later broken, the others still hold) at the cost of larger exchanges, so the number actually used is a deliberate design choice, not a maximum to chase.
Which PAN-OS ships which RFCThe two mechanisms landed in different releases, and it's worth knowing which is which:
  • PAN-OS 11.1 (Cosmos): Post Quantum Pre Shared Keys, RFC 8784. The fastest way to a quantum resistant VPN, and it needs no crypto upgrade on either side.
  • PAN-OS 11.2 (Quasar): PQC Hybrid Keys, RFC 9242 and RFC 9370. Up to 7 additional KEMs, and it's what actually provides crypto agility.
Both are described as defence in depth: they can be used independently or together, and combining them is what meets current government recommendations rather than either alone.
Where these sit relative to conventional parametersAll of this is additive on the IKE Crypto Profile and IKE Gateway, alongside the DH group, encryption and hash settings already covered. It does not replace conventional IKEv2 negotiation; it strengthens the key material that negotiation produces. Practical requirement: both peers must support the same options, so this is generally deployed between PAN-OS devices you control, or where a third party peer has confirmed support. It is not yet a universal default.
This connects directly to Part 1's Quantum Readiness Dashboard: it is what tells you which tunnels are carrying long lived confidential data and should be prioritised for these settings first.
In practiceKnow the version split cold: RFC 8784 (PPK) is 11.1 Cosmos, no crypto upgrade needed. RFC 9242/9370 (hybrid KEM) is 11.2 Quasar, up to 7 extra rounds. Both defend against harvest now decrypt later, and layering both is the recommended posture, not a choice between them.

Classic plus PQ KEM rounds, and which PAN-OS ships which RFC best practice is one classic round plus one or more PQC rounds, not all seven

✓ Check yourself GRE and quantum resistant crypto

  1. Does GRE provide encryption on its own?
    No. GRE is encapsulation only. It is paired with IPSec for confidentiality and integrity.
  2. Why is GRE over IPSec used for multicast across a VPN?
    Plain IPSec cannot carry multicast; GRE can, and IPSec then wraps around the GRE tunnel to protect it.
  3. GRE's protocol number and approximate overhead?
    Protocol 47, roughly 24 bytes of overhead, account for it in path MTU.
  4. When exactly does RFC 8784's extra keygen round happen, and what does it mix in?
    After the normal IKEv2 DH exchange. It mixes in a static PPK string, producing a key not based on primes.
  5. How long must a PPK be, and why does it never appear in the IKEv2 exchange?
    32 to 128 characters (16 to 64 bytes). It's never sent, so it can't be intercepted, only a PPK KEYID is sent, as a reference.
  6. Name two real limitations of RFC 8784 in practice.
    Any two: doesn't scale past ~25 peers, partner employees also hold the secret, or humans write down PPKs tough enough to resist a dictionary attack.
  7. What's the classic round in a hybrid key exchange, and what's the recommended number of PQC rounds?
    Classic: Diffie-Hellman, Group 21. Best practice is 1 or more PQC rounds, not all 7 by default.
  8. Which PAN-OS release ships RFC 8784, and which ships RFC 9242/9370?
    11.1 Cosmos ships RFC 8784 (PPK). 11.2 Quasar ships RFC 9242/9370 (hybrid KEM).
  9. Should you choose between PPK and hybrid KEM, or use both?
    Both: they're defence in depth, independently or together, and combining them meets current government recommendations.

✓ Anchor facts

  • GRE = encapsulation only, no encryption. Protocol 47, ~24 bytes overhead.
  • GRE over IPSec is the standard pattern for multicast or dynamic routing across a VPN.
  • RFC 8784: extra round after DH, mixes a 32 to 128 char PPK, PPK never sent (only its KEYID).
  • RFC 8784 breaks down past ~25 peers, and depends on partner side PPK secrecy too.
  • RFC 9242/9370: up to 7 extra KEM rounds; best practice is DH + 1 or more, not all 7.
  • PAN-OS 11.1 Cosmos = RFC 8784. PAN-OS 11.2 Quasar = RFC 9242/9370.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Assuming a GRE tunnel is private on its own.
  • Trying to carry multicast over plain IPSec.
  • Forgetting the ~24 byte GRE overhead when sizing MTU.
  • Thinking RFC 8784/9242/9370 replace conventional DH rather than adding to it.
  • Treating RFC 8784 as scalable at enterprise size. It isn't, past roughly 25 peers.
  • Mixing up which PAN-OS ships which RFC, 11.1 is the PPK, 11.2 is the hybrid KEM.
