Part 10 · VPN and Tunnels

Configuring Quantum Resistant IPSec, and a Different Answer Entirely

The previous page covered what RFC 8784 and the hybrid KEM do. This page covers actually building them in the GUI, the specific algorithms behind the hybrid KEM, and a completely different mechanism, QKD, that answers the same weaknesses by physical means instead of maths.

Mode
RFC 8784, the IKE Gateway buildUnder Network › Network Profiles › IKE Gateways › Add, the version has to be set correctly first: IKEv2 only mode aborts the connection if the peer doesn't support IKEv2; IKEv2 preferred mode falls back to IKEv1 if the peer doesn't, but post quantum VPN features require IKEv2 and simply won't be available if the session falls back.
On the Advanced Options tab, a new PQ PPK section holds Enable Post Quantum Pre Shared Key (PPK), disabled by default and supported only on IKEv2. Clicking Add opens the Add Post Quantum Pre Shared Key popup: a PPK KeyID, the PPK Secret and its confirmation, an Activate checkbox, and a PPK length field. PAN-OS will generate a strong PPK for you at the length you set (32 to 128 characters) via a Generate Strong PPK button, then auto copies it so it can be pasted straight into the peer's own configuration, genuinely useful given the entropy requirements covered on the previous page.
Two details worth being precise aboutPPK is independent of the authentication method: the gateway can still use Pre Shared Key or Certificate authentication as normal; PPK is an addition, not a replacement for that choice. And up to ten PPK KEYID pairs can be configured at once, each individually activated or deactivated; with multiple active keys, the initiator selects one at random for each negotiation.
Negotiation modePreferred permits fallback to classic IKEv2 if the peer can't do PQ PPK; Mandatory forces the PQ PPK path and refuses the connection otherwise, the same preferred versus mandatory pattern as the IKEv2 versus IKEv1 choice above it.
The hybrid KEM build, on top of RFC 8784Under the same Advanced Options, a PQ KEM tab holds Enable Post Quantum Key Exchange, also disabled by default, with a further Block IKEv2 if vulnerable cipher is used option to refuse a negotiation that falls back to something no longer considered safe. The IKE Crypto Profile is where the actual KEMs to use are selected. Because PQ KEM material is large, enable IKEv2 fragmentation when using PQC KEMs, and set both VPN termination devices to the same fragmentation values: a mismatch here is a real, practical cause of failed negotiation.
The actual NIST round 4 candidates supportedThese are the algorithms crypto agility is built around, if one is later found vulnerable, swap to another without waiting for a whole new RFC:
  • ML-KEM: Kyber512, Kyber768, Kyber1024.
  • BIKE: L1, L3, L5.
  • Classic McEliece: 348864, 348864f.
  • HQC: 128, 192, 256.
  • FrodoKEM: 640-AES, 640-SHAKE, 976-AES, 976-SHAKE, 1344-AES, 1344-SHAKE.
  • NTRU-Prime: sntrup761.
The limitation worth stating plainly: if a previously harvested session used a KEM that later proves vulnerable, swapping the KEM going forward does not protect that already captured traffic: crypto agility protects future sessions, not the harvest now decrypt later exposure of past ones.
Growing industry support for RFC 8784worth knowing this isn't a PAN-OS only feature: Cisco Catalyst 8000V, 8300 Series and ASR 1000 Series from IOS XE 17.11, JunOS SRX from 22.4R1, Aruba VIA Client v4.3, Samsung Galaxy on Android 11, and the open source StrongSwan v5.9 and Libreswan v3.25 projects all support it, genuine interoperability rather than a proprietary extension.
In practiceThe fragmentation mismatch detail is worth having ready specifically: PQ KEM payloads are large enough that IKEv2 fragmentation has to be enabled and matched on both ends, or negotiation fails in a way that looks unrelated to fragmentation at first glance.
Everything on this page so far (PPK, hybrid KEM) is post quantum cryptography: new maths, run on an ordinary classical computer, believed hard for a quantum computer to break. QKD is not that. It's a physically different mechanism, and it exists because IKEv2 itself has structural weaknesses PQC alone doesn't fully close.
The three IKEv2 weaknesses QKD is built to answer
  • Key derivation: IKEv2 uses Diffie-Hellman to derive the symmetric encryption key, and DH is a classical algorithm vulnerable to a quantum computer, exactly the harvest now decrypt later exposure covered throughout this guide.
  • Key exchange and payload share one path: IKEv2 sends the key exchange and the encrypted data payload over the same communication path. An attacker who eavesdrops on the key exchange has everything needed to later decrypt the payload too.
  • No built in tamper or MITM detection: IKEv2 has no mechanism to detect an attacker intercepting or altering traffic, so interception can happen without being noticed at all.
What QKD actually does about itA pair of dedicated QKD devices generate keys from genuine quantum random numbers, and instead of sending those keys over IKEv2 with everything else, the devices synchronise them out of band, over a physically separate quantum channel that cannot be intercepted the way a normal network path can. Only a key ID, never the key itself, travels over IKEv2 to the responder, which is what closes the same path weakness above.
ETSI-014 is the interoperability layera standardised API that lets an ordinary key management system request and receive keys from QKD hardware without needing to understand the quantum mechanics underneath, the firewall talks ETSI-014, not physics.
The six step exchange
  • 1: Initiator sends a key request to its own QKD device.
  • 2: The QKD device returns a key and a key ID.
  • 3: The two QKD devices sync the key and key ID between themselves, over the isolated quantum channel.
  • 4: The key ID (not the key) is sent to the responder over ordinary IKEv2 + IPSec.
  • 5: The responder requests the matching key from its own QKD device, using that key ID.
  • 6: The same key is returned to the responder, both ends now hold an identical key that never crossed the network they're protecting.
The practical constraint worth knowing: this needs dedicated QKD hardware at both ends and a genuine physical quantum channel between them. It is not a software feature you simply enable, the way PPK or hybrid KEM are.
In practiceKeep the distinction sharp if asked: PPK and hybrid KEM are post quantum cryptography, new maths on classical hardware. QKD is a different mechanism entirely, distributing keys over a physical quantum channel so the key itself never travels the path an attacker could eavesdrop on. They solve overlapping problems by genuinely different means.

✓ Check yourself configuring PQ VPN and QKD

  1. What happens if IKEv2 preferred mode falls back to IKEv1, with PQ PPK configured?
    Post quantum VPN features are unavailable: they require IKEv2, and a fallback session won't have them.
  2. What does PAN-OS do to help generate a compliant PPK?
    A Generate Strong PPK button creates one at the configured length and auto copies it for pasting into the peer's config.
  3. Why must both VPN termination devices share the same IKEv2 fragmentation settings for PQ KEM?
    PQ KEM key material is large; a fragmentation mismatch is a real, practical cause of failed negotiation.
  4. Name three of the NIST round 4 KEM candidates supported for the hybrid exchange.
    Any three: ML-KEM, BIKE, Classic McEliece, HQC, FrodoKEM, NTRU-Prime.
  5. Does swapping to a different KEM protect traffic already harvested under a now vulnerable one?
    No. Crypto agility protects future sessions, not past harvest now decrypt later exposure.
  6. What are the three IKEv2 weaknesses QKD specifically addresses?
    DH key derivation vulnerable to quantum attack, key exchange and payload sharing one path, and no MITM/tamper detection.
  7. In the QKD exchange, what actually crosses the IKEv2 path: the key, or something else?
    Only the key ID: the key itself is synced separately, out of band, over the quantum channel.
  8. Is QKD a software feature you simply enable, like PPK or hybrid KEM?
    No: it needs dedicated QKD hardware at both ends and a physical quantum channel between them.

✓ Anchor facts

  • PQ VPN features need IKEv2; a fallback to IKEv1 loses them entirely.
  • PAN-OS generates and auto copies a compliant PPK for you.
  • Up to 10 PPK KEYID pairs; multiple active keys are chosen at random by the initiator.
  • PQ KEM needs matched IKEv2 fragmentation on both ends.
  • RFC 8784 has real industry support: Cisco, JunOS, Aruba, StrongSwan, Libreswan and more.
  • QKD ≠ PQC. QKD is a physical mechanism; only a key ID ever crosses IKEv2.
  • ETSI-014 is the API that lets ordinary key management talk to QKD hardware.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Leaving the IKE Gateway on preferred mode and assuming PQ features still apply after an IKEv1 fallback.
  • Forgetting to match fragmentation settings on both VPN termination devices.
  • Treating KEM crypto agility as retroactive protection for already harvested traffic.
  • Confusing QKD with post quantum cryptography. They solve overlapping problems by entirely different means.
  • Assuming QKD is a config checkbox rather than dedicated hardware and a physical channel.

RFC 8784 (PPK)Nothing, PPK never sentNone beyond the firewallShor's algorithm on the DH portion

Part 11 · GlobalProtect

Architecture and the Sequential Build

Portals, gateways, authentication and split tunnelling are the four pieces covered here. Learn the architecture first, then the order you actually build it in, the order matters, because later steps depend on objects the earlier ones create.

Mode
Three components, and the distinction between the first two is the one most often blurred.
PortalThe single point of first contact. The agent connects here once to receive its configuration: which gateways exist, how to choose between them, client certificate settings, and the agent software itself for initial install and updates. The portal authenticates the user but does not carry their tunnel traffic.
GatewayWhere the actual VPN tunnel terminates and where security policy is enforced on the user's traffic. A deployment can have one portal and many gateways: regional gateways for latency, or internal versus external gateways for different access models.
Agent (GlobalProtect app)Runs on the endpoint. Contacts the portal first, downloads its configuration, then connects to the selected or best available gateway based on that configuration. Available for Windows, macOS, Linux, iOS, Android and Chrome OS.
Both portal and gateway are typically configured as a loopback interface with an SSL/TLS service profile, though a gateway can also sit on a physical or subinterface directly facing where clients connect from.
Internal versus external gatewaysAn external gateway serves remote users over the internet. An internal gateway serves users already inside the corporate network, and is what makes HIP based internal enforcement possible, the same posture check applies whether someone is remote or sitting at their desk.
In practiceSay it as one line if asked to summarise: the portal hands out the map, the gateway is the destination on it. Confusing the two is the most common conceptual slip on this topic.

The sequential build each step depends on an object the previous step created

Portals, gateways, authentication and split tunnelling are separate concerns, but they are not built in that order. Here is the order that actually works, and why each step has to come before the next.
1 · Certificates firstGenerate or import the portal certificate and gateway certificate(s), and if using certificate based client authentication, a client certificate or CA to validate against. Everything downstream references these through an SSL/TLS service profile, so they have to exist before anything else is configured.
2 · InterfacesCreate the loopback interfaces the portal and each gateway will bind to, with the correct zone, router and an Interface Management Profile allowing HTTPS. Most deployments use loopbacks specifically because they are always up and decoupled from any single physical interface.
3 · Gateway(s) before the portalThis is the step people get backwards. Build the gateway object first: its interface, IP pool for client addresses, DNS servers to push, tunnel zone, authentication profile, and split tunnel settings. The gateway must exist as a configured, addressable object before the portal can reference it.
4 · PortalNow build the portal: its interface and certificate, the agent configuration naming which gateways to offer and how to choose between them (manual, or automatic by lowest latency or a priority list), and the authentication profile users authenticate against to fetch that configuration.
5 · Security policyTraffic arriving through the tunnel lands in the tunnel zone. Without a security rule permitting tunnel zone to the internal zones it needs, the VPN connects successfully and then every application fails: a very common early support call.
6, 7, 8 · Client settings, HIP, then roll outRefine the client configuration (split tunnel, connect method) on the gateway's agent config. Add HIP objects and profiles if posture based policy is required, referencing the security rules from step 5. Finally test with a pilot group before pushing the configuration estate wide.
In practiceState the order as a sentence if asked directly: certificates, interfaces, gateway, portal, policy, then client settings and HIP, then a piloted rollout. The one detail worth emphasising is that the gateway is built before the portal, which is the reverse of how people usually think about it since the portal is what users see first.
Once the agent has the portal's configuration, it has to decide which gateway to use, and PAN-OS gives three models.
User-Logon (Always On): the tunnel connects automatically at login and stays up, before the user does anything. Highest security posture, standard for managed corporate devices.
Pre-Logon: establishes a machine only tunnel using a machine certificate before the user logs in, so domain login and startup scripts work over the VPN. Solves the chicken and egg problem of a remote laptop needing network access to authenticate the user who would normally establish that access.
On Demand: the user manually triggers the connection. Lowest friction, lowest assurance, typically reserved for contractor or BYOD scenarios.
Choosing between gatewaysThe portal's agent config can list gateways as manual (user picks from a list), or with automatic selection by priority and calculated latency: the agent probes each candidate gateway and connects to the best performing one that the user is authorised for.
External versus internal gateway detection: the agent can determine whether the endpoint is already inside the corporate network and behave differently, skip the tunnel entirely, or connect to an internal gateway for consistent policy enforcement rather than an external one.
In practicePre-Logon is the answer whenever a scenario mentions domain login scripts, group policy, or any process that needs to run before a human authenticates on a remote machine.

✓ Check yourself architecture and the build order

  1. In one line, distinguish the portal from the gateway.
    The portal hands out configuration (which gateways, how to connect); the gateway terminates the tunnel and enforces policy on the traffic.
  2. Which is built first, the gateway or the portal, and why?
    The gateway. The portal's agent configuration references gateway objects, so they must already exist.
  3. A user connects successfully but every internal application fails. What was missed?
    Security policy permitting the tunnel zone to the internal zones it needs. The connection working does not mean traffic is permitted.
  4. Which connect method solves domain login scripts on a remote laptop?
    Pre-Logon: a machine certificate tunnel established before the user authenticates.
  5. What makes an internal gateway useful beyond serving on network users?
    It enables HIP based enforcement on network, applying the same posture checks whether someone is remote or in the office.
  6. State the full build order in one sentence.
    Certificates, interfaces, gateway, portal, security policy, then client settings and HIP, then a piloted rollout.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Portal = configuration. Gateway = tunnel termination and policy.
  • Build order: certs → interfaces → gateway → portal → policy → client settings/HIP → pilot.
  • Both typically bind to a loopback interface.
  • Connect methods: User-Logon, Pre-Logon, On Demand.
  • Pre-Logon uses a machine certificate, before user authentication.
  • Internal gateways enable HIP enforcement on network.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Building the portal before the gateways it needs to reference.
  • Forgetting security policy for the tunnel zone entirely.
  • Using On Demand where domain login scripts are required.
  • Confusing the portal's job (configuration) with the gateway's (traffic).
  • Rolling out to the whole estate before piloting.

Part 11 · GlobalProtect

Split Tunnelling, Authentication and HIP

The two named subobjectives left: authentication, and split tunnelling. Then HIP and clientless access, which round out what a GlobalProtect deployment actually enforces.

Mode
Split tunnelling decides which traffic goes through the tunnel versus straight to the internet from the endpoint. Configured in the gateway's Client Settings, under three separate mechanisms that can combine.
By route (network): Include and Exclude lists of networks. Include narrows the tunnel to specific destination subnets; Exclude carves exceptions out of an otherwise full tunnel. No entries in Include means everything routes through the tunnel: full tunnel is the default, not split.
By domain: DNS based: Include and Exclude Domain lists send or exempt entire domains, resolving through the tunnel or not accordingly. This is how you keep, say, all corporate SaaS domains in the tunnel without needing to know their address ranges.
By application: identifies specific applications on the endpoint (by process name or path) and includes or excludes their traffic from the tunnel regardless of destination. The most granular option, useful for exempting a specific piece of software that should never traverse the corporate network.
The trade off to stateFull tunnel maximises visibility and control, since all traffic can be inspected, but costs bandwidth at the gateway and adds latency to internet bound traffic. Split tunnel is efficient and low latency for excluded traffic, but that traffic receives none of the firewall's inspection.
The three mechanisms are commonly combined: exclude well known SaaS domains by domain based split tunnel to save bandwidth, while keeping everything else full tunnelled for inspection.
In practiceThe default with nothing configured is full tunnel, which surprises people expecting split to be the default. And route based split with no Include entries doesn't split anything. It is a no-op, not an error.
Authentication happens at both the portal and, if configured separately, the gateway, and it draws directly on the authentication stack from Part 9.
The methods availableLocal database or LDAP/RADIUS via a standard authentication profile. Certificate based, using a client certificate issued via SCEP or imported, validated against a certificate profile. SAML, where the firewall is the service provider and an external IdP performs the actual authentication, including any MFA the IdP enforces.
Why SAML is now the common choiceMFA, conditional access and session policy all live at the identity provider rather than needing separate configuration on the firewall. It also integrates naturally with the Cloud Identity Engine covered in Part 9, the same identity source serving both on premises User-ID and GlobalProtect authentication.
Combining methodsClient certificate plus SAML is a common pattern: the certificate proves the device is corporate managed, SAML proves the user, and together they give device and user assurance without either check alone being sufficient. This is the practical shape of Zero Trust remote access.
An authentication cookie can be issued on successful login so the user is not reprompted on every reconnect within the cookie's lifetime, balancing security against a VPN that constantly demands reauthentication.
In practiceIf a scenario names an external identity provider, MFA, or conditional access, the answer is SAML. If it names device compliance or corporate managed devices specifically, add certificate based auth alongside it.

Split tunnel: three mechanisms, one decision and what nothing configured actually means

Host Information Profile (HIP) is endpoint posture assessment: the agent collects data about the device and reports it, so policy can differ for a compliant device versus a risky one.
Two layer object model. A HIP Object checks one category of thing, disk encryption, patch management, antivirus status, firewall enabled, a specific process running or not running, mobile device attributes, custom registry or plist checks. A HIP Profile combines HIP objects with boolean logic (AND/OR/NOT) into a named condition, such as "Corporate-Compliant" or "High Risk".
The HIP Profile is then referenced as a match condition in security policy, the same way a user or application is. A rule can permit a corporate application only for traffic matching the "Corporate-Compliant" HIP profile, and deny or restrict it otherwise.
The data itself: the agent collects it and sends it to the gateway, which evaluates HIP profiles and can log a HIP Match event, visible in the Traffic and HIP Match logs, useful for auditing posture even where policy does not yet act on it.
Ties directly back to Part 9: HIP data can be redistributed between firewalls the same way user to IP mappings are, so posture based policy remains consistent estate wide rather than being known only to the gateway a user happened to connect to.
In practiceThe two layer model is the detail worth stating precisely: objects check individual facts, profiles combine them with logic into something policy can reference. That is exactly the pattern security profile groups use for content inspection.
Clientless VPN provides browser based access to specific internal web applications without installing the GlobalProtect agent, reverse proxying the application through the gateway.
The use case is narrow and deliberate: unmanaged devices (personal laptops, kiosk machines), contractors who should not have full network access or agent software installed, or quick access to a single web application rather than the whole network.
Configured via Clientless VPN application objects on the gateway, defining exactly which internal web applications are published, referencing standard HTTP/HTTPS.
The limitations worth stating unpromptedIt only supports web based applications, no native client server traffic, no arbitrary ports, nothing that is not HTTP or HTTPS under the hood. Complex JavaScript heavy applications can break under the URL rewriting the reverse proxy performs. No HIP data is available, since there is no agent to collect it, so posture based policy cannot apply to a clientless session.
The trade off in one line: it trades network level access and posture visibility for zero client side footprint, which is exactly the right trade for a small set of published web apps reached from devices you do not manage.
In practiceIf a scenario mentions contractors, BYOD, or "access to one specific application without a full VPN client", clientless is the answer. If it mentions any non web protocol or needs posture checking, it is not.

✓ Check yourself split tunnel, auth, HIP, clientless

  1. What does an empty route based Include list actually do?
    Nothing splits: the default is full tunnel. It is a no-op, not an error.
  2. Name the three split tunnel mechanisms.
    By route, by domain, by application. They can be combined.
  3. Why is client certificate combined with SAML a common pattern?
    The certificate proves the device is corporate managed; SAML proves the user. Together, device and user assurance.
  4. Distinguish a HIP object from a HIP profile.
    An object checks one fact (disk encryption, AV status). A profile combines objects with AND/OR/NOT into a named condition used in policy.
  5. Why does clientless VPN carry no HIP data?
    There is no agent to collect it, clientless is browser based reverse proxy only.
  6. A scenario mentions contractors needing access to one internal web app with no client install. What's the answer?
    Clientless VPN: the narrow, browser based, no footprint case.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Split tunnel: route, domain, application: combinable.
  • Default with nothing configured is full tunnel.
  • SAML: firewall is the service provider; MFA lives at the IdP.
  • Certificate + SAML = device assurance + user assurance.
  • HIP: objects check facts, profiles combine them with logic.
  • Clientless VPN: web apps only, no HIP, reverse proxied.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Assuming split tunnel is the default. It is not, full tunnel is.
  • Excluded split tunnel traffic still getting zero inspection by design.
  • Expecting HIP data from a clientless session.
  • Trying to publish a non web protocol through clientless VPN.
  • Forgetting HIP data can be redistributed like User-ID mappings.

App based splitProcess / path on endpointMost granularExempt one specific app
Clientless VPNReverse proxyPer published web appContractors, BYOD, unmanaged

Part 12 · High Availability

Active/Passive, the HA Links and Election

Active/active, active/passive, and link and path monitoring are the three pieces covered here. Start with the simpler mode and the links both modes share, then split off active/active.

Mode
Two firewalls, one active and processing all traffic, one passive and holding a synchronised configuration and session state, ready to take over. Active/Passive is the default recommendation for most designs: failover behaviour is predictable, and troubleshooting a single active path is far simpler than reasoning about two.
HA1, control linkCarries heartbeats, hello messages, and configuration synchronisation. This is how each peer knows the other is alive and how a change made on one is replicated to the other. Can be a dedicated port or, on smaller platforms, shared with a data interface as HA1 backup.
HA2, data linkCarries session state synchronisation: the session table, forwarding table, IPSec security associations, ARP table, and User-ID mappings. This is what makes failover stateful: existing sessions survive rather than needing to reconnect. Can run in HA2 keepalive mode to detect a data link failure without waiting for something worse.
HA3, packet forwarding (Active/Active only)Used in active/active for forwarding packets between the two units when a session's return traffic arrives on the peer that is not its session owner. Not present in active/passive, since there is only ever one active path.
Backup linksBoth HA1 and HA2 can have a dedicated backup link, so a single link failure does not force an unnecessary failover. Best practice is to run HA1 and HA2 on separate physical interfaces, with backups for both where the platform allows it.
Verify with show high-availability state, show high-availability link-monitoring, and show high-availability control-link state.
In practiceIf HA1 fails but HA2 stays up, the peers cannot exchange heartbeats but sessions keep synchronising blind, a classic split brain precursor. This is why HA1 backup is worth the extra port far more than HA2 backup in most designs.

The three HA links, and what each one carries HA3 only exists in active/active

An HA firewall moves through a defined set of states, and knowing the sequence is what lets you read show high-availability state output correctly.
Initial → the firewall is starting HA and has not yet determined its role.
Active / Passive → the steady states in active/passive mode.
Active-Primary / Active-Secondary → the equivalent steady states in active/active mode.
Tentative → a transitional state, typically entered by a unit rejoining after a failure, while it resynchronises before becoming fully active.
Suspended → the administrator has manually taken the unit out of the HA pair, or a failure condition has forced it out.
Non functional → a hardware or software fault has made the unit unable to participate at all.
ElectionDecided by device priority (lower value wins) and whether preemption is enabled. With preemption on, a unit that returns after being down and has a better (lower) priority will reclaim active status. With preemption off, whichever unit is active stays active even if the original primary returns, avoiding an unnecessary second failover.
Most designs run with preemption disabled for exactly that reason: a flapping link that keeps triggering failover and preemption back is worse than staying on the current active unit until a deliberate, planned failback.
Heartbeat polling and hello interval settings control how quickly a peer is declared dead, trading detection speed against false positives from transient network noise.
In practiceIf a scenario mentions a unit "flapping" between active and passive after a maintenance window, the first thing to check is whether preemption is enabled and whether the priority difference is intentional.
Two failure detection mechanisms beyond the HA1 heartbeat itself detect different classes of failure.
Link MonitoringWatches a group of physical interfaces on the active unit. If a monitored interface goes down, the unit's priority is degraded or it triggers failover outright, depending on configuration. Interfaces are grouped, and you choose whether failover requires any link in the group to fail or all of them, "any" is stricter, "all" tolerates a single redundant link going down.
Path MonitoringSends ICMP pings to a specified destination beyond the firewall's own interfaces, the next hop router, or further upstream. This catches a failure link monitoring cannot see: the local interface is up, but the path beyond it is broken, so an interface only check would report everything fine while the unit is actually unreachable from the network's point of view.
Path monitoring can be configured at the virtual router level (monitoring the general health of routes via that router) or at the destination IP level (monitoring reachability to one specific address), giving finer control over exactly what "the path is broken" means for this deployment.
Both mechanisms feed the same outcome: a degraded or failed condition causes the unit to lower its priority, which, combined with the election rules from the previous topic, causes the healthier peer to become or remain active.
In practiceThe pairing to remember: link monitoring answers "is my interface up", path monitoring answers "can I actually reach something through it". A unit can pass the first and fail the second, and only path monitoring catches that.

✓ Check yourself HA links, states and monitoring

  1. Which HA link carries session state, and why does that matter for failover?
    HA2. It is what makes failover stateful: existing sessions survive rather than resetting.
  2. What happens if HA1 is lost but HA2 stays up?
    Peers cannot exchange heartbeats but keep syncing state blind, the precursor to split brain.
  3. Which HA link exists only in active/active mode?
    HA3, for forwarding packets when return traffic lands on the non owner peer.
  4. With preemption disabled, what happens when the original primary rejoins?
    The currently active unit stays active. No automatic failback.
  5. Distinguish link monitoring from path monitoring.
    Link monitoring: is the interface up. Path monitoring: can I actually reach something beyond it, via ICMP.
  6. A unit's interface is up but the network beyond it is broken. Which mechanism catches this?
    Path monitoring: link monitoring alone would report the interface as healthy.

✓ Anchor facts

  • HA1 = control, heartbeats, config sync. HA2 = session state. HA3 = A/A packet forwarding only.
  • Best practice: HA1 and HA2 on separate interfaces, each backed up.
  • States: Initial, Active/Passive (or Active-Primary/Secondary), Tentative, Suspended, Non functional.
  • Election: lower priority wins; preemption controls failback.
  • Most designs disable preemption to avoid flapping.
  • Link monitoring = interface state. Path monitoring = ICMP beyond the interface.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Running HA1 and HA2 on the same physical link with no backup.
  • Enabling preemption without understanding the flap risk.
  • Assuming link monitoring alone catches an upstream path failure.
  • Confusing Tentative (transitional) with Suspended (deliberately out).
  • Forgetting HA3 only applies to active/active.
LinkCarriesPresent inLoss causes
HA1Heartbeats, config syncBoth modesSplit brain risk
HA2Session, forwarding, ARP, IPSec SAsBoth modesFailover loses state
HA3Inter peer packet forwardingActive/Active onlyN/A in active/passive

Part 12 · High Availability

Active/Active and HA Upgrades

Active/active is worth its own page. It trades the simplicity of active/passive for both boxes doing useful work, at the cost of real design complexity.

Mode
In active/active, both firewalls process traffic simultaneously. That immediately raises a question active/passive never has to answer: for any given session, which of the two units is actually responsible for it? PAN-OS splits the answer into two separate roles.
Session ownerThe unit that owns the session for the lifetime of that session: performs the security processing, holds the session table entry, and logs it. Assigned when the session is created and does not move.
Session setupThe unit that handles the initial setup of a new session, the first packet processing. This can be a different unit from the eventual session owner, decided by the configured session setup method: IP Modulo (a hash of source and destination IP decides which unit) or Primary Device (one designated unit always handles setup, then hands ownership across if needed).
If setup and ownership end up on different units, or if return traffic for an existing session arrives on the non owner peer, that traffic is forwarded to the owner across HA3, which is exactly why HA3 exists only in this mode.
Floating IPsAn IP address that can move between the two units, so a client facing gateway address is always answered by whichever unit currently owns it, without the client needing to know which physical firewall that is. Typically one floating IP is assigned to each unit as its "home", and it moves to the peer if that unit fails. ARP load sharing is the alternative mechanism, where the two units answer ARP for a shared virtual address using a hash based or round robin split.
Verify session distribution and ownership with show session all filtered per unit, and show high-availability interface ha3 for the forwarding link's own state.
In practiceThe complexity active/active adds is exactly this session owner versus setup split, and it is why active/passive remains the default recommendation for critical paths, predictable failover is worth more than using both boxes' capacity, and it is far easier to troubleshoot when something is wrong.

Active/Active session handling two roles, and why HA3 exists to bridge them

HA1 carries configuration synchronisation: a commit on one peer is automatically replicated to the other, so both units run identical policy. This happens continuously, not only at failover.
Status is visible per object in the GUI and via show high-availability state, which reports whether the peer's configuration is in sync. A firewall that has drifted out of sync, typically from a change made while HA1 was down, needs a manual full sync push to correct it once the link is restored.
Split brainThe failure state where both units believe they are active simultaneously, because they have lost the ability to communicate (HA1 down) but each still considers itself healthy. Both then process traffic and, worse, may have diverging configuration if changes were made independently during the outage.
The defences: a backup HA1 link so a single link failure does not cause a communication loss; keeping HA1 and HA2 on separate physical paths so one fault does not take out both simultaneously; and, on some platforms, an out of band heartbeat backup mechanism using a different transport entirely for an extra layer of certainty.
After any split brain event, the recovery step is to identify which unit has the correct, most recent configuration and push a full sync from it, never assume both sides converged safely on their own.
In practiceSplit brain is rare precisely because HA1 backup exists to prevent it. When it is asked about in an interview, the expected answer names the cause (communication loss with both sides believing they're healthy) and the specific defence (a backup control link), not just the definition.
Upgrading PAN-OS on an HA pair follows a specific order, and skipping steps is the most common way to cause an avoidable outage.
1 · Suspend the passive unitTake the passive unit out of the HA pair manually request high-availability state suspend so it stops being a failover target mid upgrade.
2 · Upgrade the passive unitInstall the new PAN-OS image and reboot. It comes back up still suspended, running the new version, while the active unit keeps serving all traffic unaffected on the old version.
3 · Verify, then unsuspendConfirm the upgraded unit is healthy before rejoining the pair request high-availability state functional. It comes up as passive, now on a different PAN-OS version than its active peer, a deliberately mismatched, temporary state.
4 · Fail over deliberatelyManually trigger failover so the now upgraded unit becomes active. Traffic moves to it, and this is the moment to watch closely, let it run and stabilise before touching the other unit.
5 · Upgrade the former activeWith the newly active unit (upgraded) now carrying traffic, upgrade the remaining unit the same way: suspend, upgrade, verify, rejoin. Both units end up on the same version.
Panorama can orchestrate this entire sequence from the UI rather than requiring each CLI step by hand, and current PAN-OS additionally supports hot, warm and cold patches for targeted fixes that do not require the full sequence at all, both covered in Part 1's Orion material.
In practiceThe step people skip is letting the newly active unit run and stabilise before upgrading the second one. Upgrading both back to back with no pause removes your only safety net if the new version has a problem.

✓ Check yourself active/active and upgrades

  1. Distinguish session owner from session setup.
    Setup = handles the first packet. Owner = processes and logs for the session's lifetime. They can be different units.
  2. Why does HA3 only exist in active/active?
    To forward return traffic to the session owner when it arrives on the non owner peer, a scenario only possible with two active units.
  3. What causes split brain, and what is the primary defence?
    Loss of HA1 communication while both units believe they are healthy. Defence: a backup HA1 link and separating HA1/HA2 paths.
  4. Why does a config sync mismatch need a manual full push?
    Drift typically happens when HA1 was down during a change, automatic sync could not run, so a manual push corrects it after the link returns.
  5. In the HA upgrade sequence, which unit is upgraded first?
    The passive unit, suspended first so it is not a failover target during the upgrade.
  6. What step do people skip, and why does it matter?
    Letting the newly active (upgraded) unit run and stabilise before upgrading the second, skipping it removes your rollback safety net.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Session owner processes for the session's life. Session setup handles the first packet.
  • Setup method: IP Modulo or Primary Device.
  • Mismatch between owner and traffic path → forwarded via HA3.
  • Floating IP per unit, or ARP load sharing on a shared address.
  • Split brain: both active, HA1 lost. Defence: backup HA1 link.
  • Upgrade order: suspend passive → upgrade → verify → rejoin → fail over → stabilise → upgrade the other.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Treating active/active as strictly better because both boxes do work.
  • Forgetting HA3 has one job: bridging owner/setup mismatches.
  • Assuming config always stays in sync with no verification.
  • Upgrading both HA units back to back with no stabilisation pause.
  • Not knowing Panorama can orchestrate the upgrade sequence.
StepActionState after
1Suspend passive unitOut of the pair, not a failover target
2Upgrade passive unitNew version, still suspended
3Verify, rejoinPassive, mismatched version (temporary)
4Fail over deliberatelyUpgraded unit now active
5Let it stabilise, then upgrade the otherBoth on the same version

Part 13 · Virtual Systems

VSYS Concepts and What Lives Inside One

Interfaces and zones, virtual routers, logical routers, and inter VSYS routing and security are the four pieces covered here. Start with what a VSYS actually is before the routing between them.

Mode
A virtual system (VSYS) is a logically separate firewall running on shared physical hardware, with its own interfaces, zones, security policy, NAT, routing, and set of administrators. One appliance can host multiple VSYS, each behaving as an independent firewall to whoever manages it.
This is the Palo Alto answer to the same problem Cisco solves with ASA security contexts: put several logically distinct firewalls on one box rather than buying separate hardware for each.
Why organisations use itMulti tenancy: a managed service provider running separate customers on shared appliances, each customer's administrators only able to see and change their own VSYS.
Internal separation: isolating a regulated business unit, a lab environment, or an acquired company's network from the rest of the estate on the same physical pair.
Delegated administration: giving a team control of their own segment's policy without touching the shared platform configuration.
VSYS is licensed per platform, with a maximum count that varies by model: smaller appliances may support none or a handful, larger and chassis based platforms support many more. Enabling multi-vsys mode is a device wide setting under Device › Setup › Management.
A vsys1 exists by default even on a single-vsys firewall; enabling multi-vsys mode is what exposes the ability to create additional ones alongside it.
In practiceThe mental model that makes the rest of this part make sense: a VSYS is a firewall within a firewall. Everything that follows (which objects it owns, how traffic crosses between VSYS, who administers it) falls out of that one idea.

One appliance, several independent firewalls what belongs to a VSYS, and what is shared across all of them

Every physical or logical interface is assigned to exactly one VSYS when multi-vsys mode is enabled, the assignment happens on the interface itself, before zone configuration. A VSYS's zones can only contain interfaces that already belong to that VSYS.
Zones are VSYS scoped: a zone named TRUST in VSYS1 is a completely different object from a zone named TRUST in VSYS2, even sharing the same name. They cannot reference each other's interfaces.
Virtual and logical routersEach VSYS gets its own routing instance, a virtual router under legacy routing, or a logical router under the Advanced Routing Engine, exactly as covered in Part 4. A VSYS's routing table is independent: VSYS1 and VSYS2 can both run entirely different routing protocols, both use overlapping private address space, and neither is aware of the other's routes by default.
One router can technically be shared across VSYS in some configurations, but the normal and recommended design gives each VSYS its own router, preserving full routing isolation between tenants.
Security policy is per VSYSEach VSYS has its own complete security rulebase, its own NAT rulebase, and its own implicit intrazone-allow / interzone-deny rules, independently overridable per VSYS.
In practiceThe isolation is the whole point: a misconfiguration or compromise inside one VSYS's zones, routing table, or policy has no direct visibility into another VSYS's traffic, which is exactly the guarantee multi tenancy requires.
Not everything on a multi-vsys firewall has to be duplicated. Objects can be created at two scopes.
VSYS specific: visible and usable only within the VSYS it was created in. This is the default and the right choice for tenant specific address objects, application groups, or anything that should stay isolated with the rest of that VSYS's configuration.
Shared: created at the Shared location and visible to every VSYS on the device. Useful for genuinely common objects: a security profile group every VSYS should use as a baseline, an EDL of known malicious IPs relevant regardless of tenant, or standard logging settings.
The trade off is exactly the isolation guarantee from the previous topic: a shared object is a shared dependency. Change it once, and it changes for every VSYS using it, which is convenient for a genuinely universal threat feed, and dangerous for anything a tenant should control independently.
Objects can be cloned from shared into a VSYS if a tenant needs their own independently editable copy going forward, breaking the shared link deliberately.
Certificates, in particular, are commonly kept at the Shared location so a single enterprise CA issued certificate for decryption or GlobalProtect can serve every VSYS without reimporting it per tenant.
In practiceDefault to VSYS specific unless there is a deliberate reason for shared. The failure mode people hit is a change to a shared object landing unexpectedly across every tenant, when only one tenant's behaviour should have changed.

✓ Check yourself VSYS concepts

  1. What is a VSYS, in one line?
    A logically separate firewall (own interfaces, zones, policy, NAT, router, admins) running on shared hardware.
  2. What is the Cisco equivalent of a VSYS?
    ASA security contexts. Same purpose: several logical firewalls, one physical box.
  3. Can two VSYS have zones with the same name?
    Yes: zones are VSYS scoped, so identically named zones in different VSYS are entirely separate objects.
  4. Do VSYS share a routing table by default?
    No. Each VSYS has its own independent virtual or logical router; overlapping address space between VSYS is fine.
  5. What is the risk of a shared object?
    It is a shared dependency: a change made once affects every VSYS using it, which can surprise a tenant expecting isolation.
  6. What is the default scope for a new object on a multi-vsys firewall?
    VSYS specific, unless deliberately created at the Shared location.

✓ Anchor facts

  • VSYS = logical firewall on shared hardware; equivalent to ASA contexts.
  • Interfaces belong to one VSYS; zones are VSYS scoped and cannot cross.
  • Each VSYS gets its own virtual/logical router: independent, can overlap addressing.
  • Security policy, NAT and implicit rules are per VSYS.
  • Shared objects are visible to all VSYS; a change affects everyone using it.
  • Certificates are commonly kept Shared to avoid reimporting per tenant.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Assuming VSYS share a routing table or can see each other's routes.
  • Editing a Shared object without realising every VSYS is affected.
  • Forgetting interface to VSYS assignment happens before zone config.
  • Expecting a same named zone in another VSYS to be the same object.
  • Not licensing enough VSYS for the platform before designing around them.
ScopeVisible toChange impactDefault for
VSYS specificOne VSYS onlyThat VSYS aloneNew objects, by default
SharedEvery VSYSAll VSYS using itCertificates, common EDLs, baseline profiles

Part 13 · Virtual Systems

Inter VSYS Routing, External Zones and Administration

If VSYS are fully isolated by default, how does traffic ever get from one to another? This is where most of the practical configuration actually sits.

Mode
By design, VSYS cannot see each other's traffic. Two mechanisms deliberately open a controlled path between them, and they solve different problems.
External zone, VSYS talking to VSYSAn external zone is created in one VSYS and configured to reference another VSYS as its "external" system. Traffic can then be routed between the two VSYS through this zone, and, critically, security policy still applies on both sides, exactly as it would for any other zone to zone traffic. This is how you deliberately allow VSYS1 to reach a specific service in VSYS2 without collapsing the isolation between them entirely.
Each VSYS needs a route pointing toward the other via this external zone relationship, and the traffic is genuinely routed. It is not a shortcut that bypasses inspection.
Shared gateway, VSYS sharing one internet exitA shared gateway is a special interface, owned by no single VSYS, that multiple VSYS route through to reach a common external network: typically the internet. Rather than every VSYS needing its own dedicated internet facing interface and public IP allocation, they all route out through one shared exit point.
The shared gateway has its own zone and its own security policy layer, so traffic from any VSYS crossing it is still inspected at that boundary, in addition to whatever policy the originating VSYS itself applied.
The distinction to holdExternal zone connects two specific VSYS to each other. Shared gateway connects many VSYS to one common external network. A design can use both: external zones for VSYS that genuinely need to talk to one specific peer, a shared gateway for the internet exit every VSYS needs but none should own individually.
In practiceIf a scenario says "two departments need to reach a common database", that is an external zone between their two VSYS. If it says "every tenant needs internet access but we only have one public IP block", that is a shared gateway.

Two controlled paths through the isolation VSYS to VSYS versus VSYS to the outside world

A VSYS administrator role, configured at Device › Administrators, is scoped to one or more specific VSYS rather than the whole device. An admin assigned only to VSYS2 cannot see or change VSYS1's configuration, logs, or objects at all, from their perspective, the shared hardware and every other VSYS simply do not exist.
This is the delegated administration use case from the first topic made concrete: a managed service provider gives each customer's team a VSYS admin account, and no customer can see another customer's policy, traffic, or logs, even though all of them run on the same physical appliance pair.
Above VSYS admins sits the device wide superuser, who can see and configure every VSYS, the shared objects, and the platform level settings (licensing, PAN-OS version, HA) that no VSYS admin can touch regardless of role.
Resource limitsBecause VSYS share physical resources, PAN-OS lets you cap what each VSYS can consume: maximum sessions, maximum security rules, and maximum number of zones, among other limits, configured per VSYS. This prevents one noisy or misconfigured tenant from starving the session table or rule capacity that every other VSYS on the box also depends on.
Overall platform maximums (total sessions, total rules the hardware can hold) are fixed by the model; VSYS resource limits divide that fixed pool deliberately rather than leaving it to whichever VSYS happens to consume it first.
In practiceThe two together are the actual multi tenancy guarantee: administrative isolation (a VSYS admin cannot see other tenants) plus resource isolation (one tenant cannot exhaust shared capacity). Neither alone is sufficient for a genuine multi tenant design.
This is a genuine design judgement question, and it is worth having the trade off ready rather than just the mechanics.
VSYS makes sense whenTenants or business units need genuine policy and administrative separation but do not individually need dedicated hardware, the workloads are modest enough that sharing a platform's capacity is efficient, and the cost or rack space of separate physical appliances per tenant is not justified.
It also makes sense where centralised lifecycle management matters: one PAN-OS image, one set of hardware to patch and maintain, versus that multiplied by however many separate firewalls a fully physical design would need.
Separate firewalls make more sense whenA tenant has compliance requirements demanding physical separation, not just logical, some regulatory regimes specifically require this, and VSYS logical isolation does not satisfy it. Or when a tenant's traffic volume is large enough that sharing platform resource limits with other VSYS becomes a genuine capacity risk regardless of how carefully those limits are tuned. Or when a single point of hardware failure affecting every VSYS simultaneously is not an acceptable risk profile for that workload.
A shared HA pair running multi-vsys still shares the fate of that pair: if the platform itself has a catastrophic failure, every VSYS on it goes down together, which physically separate firewalls per tenant would not.
In practiceI would frame it as: VSYS buys efficient logical separation on shared hardware; separate firewalls buy true physical and fault isolation at the cost of managing more boxes. The choice comes down to what the compliance and blast radius requirements actually demand, not just how many tenants there are.

✓ Check yourself inter VSYS routing and administration

  1. Distinguish external zone from shared gateway.
    External zone connects one VSYS to another specific VSYS. Shared gateway connects many VSYS to one common external network.
  2. Does traffic crossing an external zone or shared gateway bypass security policy?
    No. Both are genuinely routed, and policy is enforced at each crossing.
  3. A scenario says "two departments need to reach a shared database". Which mechanism?
    External zone between their two VSYS.
  4. What can a VSYS administrator see outside their assigned VSYS?
    Nothing: other VSYS and shared platform settings are invisible to them entirely.
  5. Why does PAN-OS let you set per VSYS resource limits?
    To stop one VSYS exhausting shared capacity (sessions, rules, zones) that every other VSYS on the platform also depends on.
  6. Name one reason to choose separate physical firewalls over VSYS.
    A compliance requirement for physical, not just logical, separation, or an unacceptable shared blast radius on hardware failure.

✓ Anchor facts

  • External zone = VSYS to a specific other VSYS, policy still applies.
  • Shared gateway = many VSYS to one common external network.
  • Neither bypasses inspection, both are genuinely routed.
  • VSYS admins see only their assigned VSYS; the superuser sees all.
  • Resource limits cap sessions, rules, zones per VSYS.
  • VSYS shares the fate of the underlying hardware/HA pair.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Assuming external zone traffic skips policy because it's "internal".
  • Confusing external zone (VSYS to VSYS) with shared gateway (VSYS to internet).
  • Believing VSYS gives physical isolation. It does not.
  • Not setting resource limits and letting one VSYS starve the rest.
  • Forgetting a VSYS admin cannot even see other VSYS exist.
External zoneShared gateway
ConnectsOne VSYS to anotherMany VSYS to one external network
Owned byA VSYS, referencing anotherNo single VSYS
Policy enforced?Yes, both sidesYes, at the gateway boundary

Part 14 · Web Proxy and Logging

Standard Inspection, Explicit and Transparent Web Proxy

Configuring web proxy on PAN-OS is a broad topic in practice, covering two genuinely different deployment models with different client requirements and different visibility.

Mode
The distinction underneath all three modes is whether the firewall terminates the TCP session at all. Get this one fact right and the rest of the comparison follows from it.
Standard network inspection (Routed / Gateway)This is the default mode almost every deployment runs in, whether the interfaces are Virtual Wire, Layer 2 or Layer 3. The firewall sits as a gateway the traffic routes through, inspecting the same client to server TCP session as it passes. It never ends that session and starts a new one. App-ID, Content-ID and decryption all still run in full, but structurally the firewall is a Layer 3 hop, not an endpoint the client is talking to.
Web proxy mode is a genuinely different architecture: the firewall becomes an actual TCP endpoint. It terminates the client's connection, then opens a separate, new connection to the destination server on the client's behalf. That's what makes proxy mode a true man in the middle at the application layer, rather than an in path inspector, and it's why proxy configuration lives apart from ordinary security policy. The two proxy modes below only differ in whether the client knows it's talking to a proxy at all.
Explicit proxyThe client is deliberately configured to send its web traffic to the firewall's proxy address and port, either by manual browser settings or, far more commonly at scale, a PAC (Proxy Auto Configuration) file that the browser fetches and evaluates to decide which requests go to the proxy.
Because the client is aware, explicit proxy naturally supports proxy authentication: the browser can present credentials directly to the proxy, independent of any other identification method. It is configured with a dedicated proxy port and a PAC file location the firewall serves out.
Transparent proxyThe client sends traffic normally, with no awareness of a proxy. The firewall intercepts it in path (the traffic was already routed through it as a Layer 3 gateway) and proxies it without any client side configuration at all.
Transparent mode's advantage is zero endpoint configuration: nothing to push to every device, nothing that can be bypassed by a user changing their own proxy settings. Its cost is that proxy level authentication is harder, since the client never explicitly addressed a proxy and so has no natural place to present credentials to it; identification typically falls back to User-ID mapping instead.
The distinction worth stating plainlyStandard inspection passes one continuous session through and never terminates it. Explicit and transparent proxy both terminate the client's connection and originate a new one: they differ only in whether the client is aware that's happening. All three still get full App-ID, Content-ID and decryption; the mode decides how traffic arrives and is structured, not what happens to it once there.
In practiceIf a scenario describes ordinary firewall deployment with no proxy configuration mentioned, that's standard inspection, the default. If it mentions PAC files or proxy credentials, that's explicit proxy. If it mentions BYOD, unmanaged devices, or "no client configuration" specifically for web traffic, that's transparent proxy.

Three modes, one real dividing line does the firewall terminate the TCP session, or just inspect it in flight?

A PAC (Proxy Auto Configuration) file is a JavaScript file a browser fetches and evaluates for every request, returning which proxy (if any) to use. The firewall hosts and serves the PAC file, and clients are pointed at its URL, typically via DHCP option 252 or Group Policy pushing the setting centrally rather than configuring each browser by hand.
The PAC file's logic can differentiate by destination, sending internal traffic direct and only proxying internet bound requests, or by other conditions the script defines, giving central control over proxy behaviour without touching every endpoint individually when the logic changes.
Proxy authenticationUnder explicit proxy, the firewall can require the client to authenticate directly to the proxy before requests are served, using Basic, NTLM, or Kerberos authentication methods, tied back to the same authentication profiles and server profiles covered in Part 9.
This gives an additional identification path alongside User-ID mapping, useful specifically where a device is not on a domain and cannot be mapped by the usual DC-log or agent methods, but the user can still present credentials directly to the proxy.
Successful proxy authentication can also feed a user to IP mapping, the same way an Authentication Policy challenge does, so downstream security policy written against users benefits even when the identification method was the proxy layer rather than User-ID's usual sources.
In practicePAC based deployment via DHCP or GPO is what makes explicit proxy viable at scale, manually configuring proxy settings on every browser does not survive contact with a real estate. Kerberos proxy auth in particular gives single sign on with no separate login prompt for domain joined machines.
PAN-OS separates logs by subject matter, and knowing which log a given event lands in is what makes an investigation fast rather than a search across everything.
Traffic log: every session matching a security rule with logging enabled: source, destination, application, rule matched, action, bytes, and the session end reason covered in the troubleshooting material. The broadest log, and the starting point for most investigations.
Threat log: anything a security profile caught, with the Type field naming the profile (virus, spyware, vulnerability, url, file, wildfire, data), exactly as covered in Part 7.
URL Filtering log: a more detailed view specifically of URL category decisions, sometimes consolidated into the Threat log depending on configuration.
Decryption log, from Part 8: cipher, TLS version, certificate details, and the error reason for any decryption failure.
System log: device level events: HA state changes, commit results, licence expiry, interface up/down.
Configuration log: every configuration change, who made it, and what changed, which is the audit trail for compliance.
GlobalProtect log: connection and authentication events from Part 11's VPN material.
HIP Match log: posture evaluation results, also from Part 11.
User-ID log: mapping events: source, method, and timing of every user to IP mapping learned.
Each log type can be viewed at Monitor › Logs with its own filter syntax, and each can be independently forwarded to different destinations via log forwarding profiles, covered next.
In practiceMatch the investigation to the log by what you are actually asking. "Was this allowed" is Traffic. "What did a profile catch" is Threat. "Who changed this rule" is Configuration. Picking the wrong log wastes time searching in the wrong place.

✓ Check yourself standard inspection, proxy modes and log types

  1. What's the fundamental structural difference between standard network inspection and either proxy mode?
    Standard inspection passes one continuous TCP session through and never terminates it. Both proxy modes terminate the client's session and originate a new one to the server.
  2. What is the core difference between explicit and transparent proxy?
    Whether the client is aware it is talking to a proxy. Explicit is configured deliberately; transparent intercepts unannounced.
  3. Which mode naturally supports direct proxy authentication, and why?
    Explicit: the client knowingly addresses the proxy, giving it a place to present credentials. Transparent relies on User-ID instead.
  4. How is a PAC file typically distributed at scale?
    DHCP option 252 or Group Policy, rather than manual per browser configuration.
  5. Which log names the security profile that caught a threat?
    The Threat log, via its Type field.
  6. Which log is the audit trail for who changed what configuration?
    The Configuration log.
  7. A scenario says "no client configuration allowed, BYOD environment". Which proxy mode?
    Transparent: zero endpoint configuration is its defining advantage.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Standard inspection = default mode, one session, never terminated. Both proxy modes terminate and reoriginate.
  • Explicit = client aware, PAC file, supports direct proxy auth.
  • Transparent = client unaware, in path, relies on User-ID.
  • PAC distributed via DHCP option 252 or GPO.
  • Proxy auth methods: Basic, NTLM, Kerberos.
  • Ten log types, each independently forwardable.
  • Match the investigation to the log by what question you're asking.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Assuming transparent proxy supports proxy level auth as easily as explicit.
  • Manually configuring browsers instead of using a PAC file at scale.
  • Searching the Traffic log for something that only appears in Threat.
  • Forgetting App-ID and decryption apply the same regardless of proxy mode.
  • Confusing System log (device events) with Configuration log (change audit).
LogRecordsKey field
TrafficEvery logged sessionApp, rule, action, session end reason
ThreatSecurity profile hitsType names the profile
DecryptionTLS sessions and failuresCipher, version, error reason
SystemDevice level eventsHA, commits, licensing
ConfigurationConfig changesAdmin, what changed
User-IDMapping eventsSource, method, timing

Part 14 · Web Proxy and Logging

Log Forwarding Profiles and Built in Actions

Logs are only useful once they leave the firewall. Forwarding profiles decide where each log goes, and built in actions turn a log event into an automatic response.

Mode
A Log Forwarding Profile at Objects › Log Forwarding decides where logs go once generated. It is attached to security rules (for Traffic and Threat logs) or configured at a system level (for System, Configuration and other device logs), and without one attached, logs stay local to the firewall.
Inside a profile, you define match list profiles (one per log type (Traffic, Threat, URL Filtering, WildFire, Decryption, and so on)) each specifying a filter for which log entries qualify, and a list of forwarding destinations: syslog, email, SNMP trap, HTTP, and the Panorama or Strata Logging Service pipeline covered on the next page.
Filtering, not just forwarding everythingA match list's filter narrows what actually gets sent. A common pattern is forwarding only high and critical severity Threat log entries to an email destination for immediate attention, while sending the full unfiltered log to the long term Strata Logging Service or Panorama collector for retention and later analysis.
This is the same principle as tuning a security profile to reduce noise, applied to what gets sent onward rather than what gets blocked.
Verify a profile is actually attached and working with show log-settings and by checking the destination directly, a syslog server with nothing arriving usually means the profile was never attached to the rule generating the traffic, not a network problem between the firewall and the collector.
In practiceThe most common early deployment gap is creating a well configured Log Forwarding Profile and forgetting to attach it to the security rules that need it. The profile existing changes nothing on its own.

The auto quarantine pattern, revisited with the actual objects this is the same chain from Part 6, now with the forwarding profile named

A match list's built in action is what turns a log event from passive record keeping into an active response, and it is configured directly inside the log forwarding profile rather than needing an external script or SIEM to react.
The tag action, the one to know coldOn a match, the firewall can automatically tag the source or destination IP address involved in that log entry. This is exactly the mechanism covered as the auto quarantine pattern in Part 6: a Threat log entry matching "critical severity" triggers a tag, a Dynamic Address Group already built around that tag picks up the newly tagged address with no commit required, and a deny rule already referencing that DAG isolates the host within seconds.
The whole chain from detection to containment requires no administrator to be watching at the moment it happens, the objects were built once, and they react automatically every time the pattern matches again.
Forwarding actionsBeyond tagging, a match list forwards matching entries to one or more destinations: syslog servers (feeding a SIEM), email for direct alerting, SNMP trap for integration with existing monitoring, or HTTP for a webhook style integration with an external system.
Multiple actions can attach to the same match list, tag the address and forward to syslog and send an email, all from one matching log entry.
In practiceThe tag action is the detail that turns "we log everything" into "we respond automatically". When a scenario describes automatic quarantine or isolation with no human intervention, this built in tag action attached to a match list is the mechanism, not a separate orchestration platform.
Pulling the log types and forwarding mechanisms together into an actual design, the questions worth answering deliberately rather than defaulting on.
What needs immediate attention versus long term retentionCritical and high severity Threat entries, HA state changes, and licence expiry warnings typically warrant email or SNMP trap for immediate visibility. The full, unfiltered log volume goes to Panorama or the Strata Logging Service for retention, correlation and later investigation, different urgency, different destination.
What feeds automated responseAnything with a Dynamic Address Group waiting on it needs the tag built in action on a tightly scoped match list, too broad a filter tags too much and quarantines legitimate traffic; too narrow misses genuine incidents.
What feeds the SIEMSyslog forwarding to a central platform, typically with most or all log types included, since correlation across Traffic, Threat, User-ID and Configuration logs is exactly what a SIEM is for and narrowing the feed defeats that purpose.
Retention and complianceHow long logs are kept is often a compliance driven decision (from Part 8's material on this) rather than a technical one, PCI-DSS and similar frameworks specify minimum retention periods that the forwarding destination needs to actually support.
I would design it as: tag and quarantine narrow and precise, SIEM feed broad and inclusive, retention driven by whatever compliance framework applies, and confirm every profile is actually attached before assuming it is working.
In practiceThis is where logging strategy stops being a checklist item and becomes a design decision with real trade offs, over broad automated tagging causes outages, under broad SIEM feeds miss the correlation that justified buying a SIEM in the first place.

✓ Check yourself forwarding profiles and built in actions

  1. What does a Log Forwarding Profile actually contain?
    Match list profiles: one per log type, each with a filter and a set of forwarding destinations or actions.
  2. Why does a syslog server sometimes receive nothing despite a correctly built profile?
    The profile was never attached to the security rule (or system log setting) generating the traffic.
  3. Which built in action drives the auto quarantine pattern from Part 6?
    The tag action: tagging the source or destination address, which a waiting Dynamic Address Group picks up automatically.
  4. Name the four forwarding destination types a match list can send to.
    Syslog, email, SNMP trap, HTTP.
  5. Why would you filter the email destination to critical/high severity only, but not the SIEM feed?
    Email is for immediate human attention: too much noise defeats it. The SIEM needs broad, correlatable data to do its job.
  6. What risk does an overly broad tag and quarantine filter create?
    It tags and isolates legitimate traffic, turning an automated defence into a self inflicted outage.

✓ Anchor facts

  • A Log Forwarding Profile holds match list profiles, one per log type.
  • A profile does nothing until attached to a rule or log setting.
  • Built in actions: tag (address), plus forward to syslog/email/SNMP/HTTP.
  • The tag action is the mechanism behind auto quarantine.
  • Filter narrowly for automated response; keep the SIEM feed broad.
  • Retention is often a compliance driven decision.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Building a profile and forgetting to attach it anywhere.
  • Filtering the SIEM feed too narrowly and losing correlation value.
  • An overly broad tag filter quarantining legitimate hosts.
  • Assuming a match list can only do one thing, several actions can stack.
  • Ignoring compliance mandated retention when designing the destination.
NeedFilter breadthDestinationAction
Immediate human alertNarrow: critical/high onlyEmail, SNMP trapForward
SIEM correlationBroad: most/all entriesSyslogForward
Automated quarantinePrecise: tightly scopedLocal DAGTag
Long term retentionFull, unfilteredPanorama / Strata Logging ServiceForward
