Part 14 · Web Proxy and Logging

Strata Logging Service, Collectors and Collector Groups

Strata Logging Service, log forwarding, and log collectors and collector groups are the three pieces covered here. The cloud path and the on premises path, and how they relate.

Mode
Strata Logging Service (SLS) is Palo Alto's cloud delivered log storage and management platform, firewalls send logs directly to the cloud rather than to on premises log collectors, and it is licensed by retention volume and period rather than by hardware.
Why it existsOn premises Panorama log collectors require sizing, patching, storage expansion and hardware lifecycle management. SLS removes that entirely, Palo Alto operates the storage and scaling, and the firewall estate just sends logs to a cloud endpoint rather than managing collector capacity locally.
Firewalls are onboarded to SLS via a Customer Support Portal tenant association, and log forwarding to it is configured the same way as any other destination in a Log Forwarding Profile, SLS is simply one more forwarding target alongside syslog, Panorama, email and the rest.
The relationship with PanoramaPanorama can query SLS directly for log viewing, reporting and ACC dashboards, rather than needing its own local collector to hold the data. This means an architecture can be Panorama for management and policy, SLS for log storage, decoupling the two roles that a traditional on premises Panorama with local collectors would combine.
SLS also underpins several of the AI driven and cloud native features referenced elsewhere, Cortex integration, AIOps, and cross session correlation from Orion in Part 1 all depend on logs actually being centrally available at cloud scale, which is precisely what SLS is built to provide.
In practiceThe one line contrast: Panorama log collectors are infrastructure you own and size; SLS is a service you subscribe to and never size at all. Which one a design uses is largely a build versus buy decision on log infrastructure.

Two log storage models, and where each firewall's logs actually land build it yourself, or subscribe to it

A Log Collector is a Panorama role, either a dedicated M-Series/CN-Series appliance running in Log Collector mode, or Panorama itself acting as its own local collector for smaller deployments, that receives and stores logs forwarded from managed firewalls.
Multiple collectors are organised into a Log Collector Group, which provides two things a single collector cannot: redundancy, so a collector failure does not lose incoming logs, and capacity, since log volume is distributed across the group rather than limited to one appliance's storage.
How firewalls choose a collectorA managed firewall is assigned a Collector Group to forward to (not an individual collector), and within that group PAN-OS uses forwarding preference settings and current load to decide which specific collector actually receives each log stream, giving the group both load distribution and automatic failover if the preferred collector becomes unreachable.
Collectors within a group replicate configuration and can be configured for log redundancy, so a single collector's hardware failure does not mean permanent loss of the logs it held, depending on the redundancy settings chosen for that group.
Sizing a collector group is a genuine design exercise: expected log rate, retention period, and desired redundancy level together determine how many collectors and how much storage the group needs, exactly the sizing burden SLS on the previous page removes entirely.
In practiceThe layering to keep straight: firewall → Collector Group (not an individual collector) → specific collector chosen by preference and load within that group. Misconfiguring the group assignment, rather than an individual collector, is the usual root cause when logs stop arriving from a specific site.
Forwarding preference is the setting that decides, within a Collector Group holding several collectors, which one a given firewall prefers to send its logs to first, before falling back to others in the group.
This lets you deliberately steer a site's logs toward a nearby collector for latency reasons, a branch office preferring the regional collector over one across a WAN link, while still having the rest of the group available as automatic failover if that preferred collector becomes unreachable.
Redundancy within the groupPAN-OS supports configuring log redundancy across collectors in a group, so a given log entry is stored on more than one collector rather than existing as a single copy. The trade off is direct: redundancy protects against losing logs to a hardware failure, at the cost of the storage capacity and write throughput that duplication consumes.
Whether redundancy is worth that cost depends on what the logs are for, compliance mandated retention (Part 8's material) usually justifies it, while a purely operational troubleshooting log with a short retention window may not.
Health of a Collector Group is monitored via show log-collector-es-cluster health and Panorama's own collector status dashboard, which reports per collector storage utilisation, connection state, and whether the group's configured redundancy level is actually being maintained.
In practiceForwarding preference is a latency and locality decision; redundancy is a durability versus cost decision. They are configured together in the same Collector Group but answer two genuinely different design questions.

✓ Check yourself SLS, collectors and collector groups

  1. What is the fundamental difference between SLS and a Panorama log collector?
    SLS is cloud delivered and unsized, subscribed to by volume/period. A collector is infrastructure you own and must size and maintain.
  2. Can Panorama query logs stored in SLS?
    Yes: Panorama can query SLS directly for viewing, reporting and ACC, decoupling management from log storage.
  3. Does a firewall forward to an individual collector or to a group?
    A Collector Group. The specific collector within it is chosen by forwarding preference and current load.
  4. What two things does grouping collectors provide that one collector cannot?
    Redundancy (survives a collector failure) and capacity (log volume spread across the group).
  5. What does forwarding preference actually control?
    Which collector in the group a firewall prefers first, typically for locality/latency, before falling back to others.
  6. What is the cost of enabling log redundancy across a collector group?
    Storage capacity and write throughput: each log entry is stored on more than one collector.

✓ Anchor facts

  • SLS = cloud delivered, licensed by volume/period, no sizing.
  • Panorama can query SLS or local collectors for the same views.
  • Firewalls forward to a Collector Group, not an individual collector.
  • Groups provide redundancy and capacity a single collector cannot.
  • Forwarding preference = locality/latency. Redundancy = durability vs cost.
  • show log-collector-es-cluster health for group status.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Assuming logs missing from a site means a single collector fault, not a group assignment issue.
  • Treating SLS and Panorama as mutually exclusive rather than combinable.
  • Enabling redundancy without accounting for the storage cost.
  • Not setting forwarding preference, leaving a branch defaulting across a WAN link.
  • Sizing on premises collectors for peak rather than sustained log rate.
Panorama Log CollectorStrata Logging Service
Where it runsOn premises, your hardwareCloud delivered
SizingYou size and maintain itNone, scales automatically
LicensingHardware + PanoramaVolume and retention period
Grouped forRedundancy, capacityN/A, managed by Palo Alto

Part 15 · Panorama and ACC

Modes, Onboarding and Device Groups

Panorama, templates and device groups, and pre/post rulesets are the three pieces covered here. Start with what Panorama actually is, then the container that holds shared policy.

Mode

Read the topic, recall what you can, then reveal. Mark what's solid to track what still needs work. Controls act across every page.

Panorama is centralised management for an estate of firewalls: policy, objects, software, licensing, and logging can all be controlled from one place instead of touching every device individually. It runs in one of three modes.
Management only: Panorama's role is purely configuration and policy management; log storage is handled elsewhere, either by dedicated Log Collectors or by Strata Logging Service.
Panorama mode (management + local log collector): the default for a standalone M-Series or VM appliance, combining management with its own local log collection.
Log Collector mode: a dedicated appliance running only as a Log Collector, with no management function, joined to a Panorama management instance and a Collector Group as covered in Part 14.
Adding a firewall to PanoramaThree things need to line up: the firewall's management connectivity to Panorama (reachable, correct Panorama IP configured on the firewall), the firewall added to Panorama's managed devices list by serial number, and the firewall assigned into a device group and a template stack so it actually receives shared configuration rather than sitting unmanaged.
Once connected, the firewall's local configuration and Panorama pushed configuration coexist: templates and device groups push their content, but a firewall retains some settings that only exist locally and are never overridden.
Verify with show panorama-status on the firewall (connection state, last push and commit results) and the Managed Devices health view in Panorama itself.
In practiceA firewall showing as "connected" in Panorama but not receiving policy updates is almost always a device group or template assignment gap, not a connectivity problem, connectivity and configuration assignment are two separate things that both have to be right.

Two hierarchies, two jobs device groups carry policy and objects; templates carry device and network settings

A Device Group is a logical grouping of managed firewalls that share security policy, NAT policy, and objects. It is the vehicle for pushing rules and address/service/application objects to many firewalls at once rather than configuring each individually.
HierarchyDevice groups can be nested: a child device group inherits the shared policy and objects of its parent, and can add its own on top. A common design uses a top level group for organisation wide baseline rules (block known bad EDLs, compliance requirements) with child groups per region, business unit, or function adding their own specific rules beneath that baseline.
A firewall is assigned to exactly one device group, typically the most specific one relevant to it, and receives the full inherited chain of policy from every ancestor group above it in the hierarchy, not just its own group's content.
Shared is the topmost, implicit level above all device groups, for objects and policy meant to apply organisation wide regardless of hierarchy position.
Objects can be defined at Shared, at any device group level, or locally on the firewall itself, and PAN-OS resolves naming conflicts by a defined precedence, with more specific (closer to the firewall) generally winning over more general.
In practiceDesign the hierarchy around what should genuinely be common versus what is truly local, over centralising into Shared makes every change affect the whole estate; under using hierarchy means duplicating the same rule across many device groups by hand.
Within a device group, the policy and object model mirrors what a standalone firewall has, but scoped to however many firewalls belong to that group rather than one device.
Objects: address, service, application, and security profile objects created at the device group level are pushed to every member firewall, keeping definitions consistent estate wide rather than drifting per device.
Policies: security and NAT rules created here push to every firewall in the group (and its descendants, given inheritance). A rule referencing an object defined at the same device group level or above resolves correctly on push; a rule accidentally referencing a purely local object on one firewall will not push consistently to others that lack it.
OverridesA specific firewall can be given a local override of a pushed object or, in some cases, policy behaviour, for a genuine device specific exception. This should be the deliberate exception, not the normal pattern, overusing overrides defeats the purpose of centralising through a device group in the first place.
Managing which firewalls belong to which device group, and reviewing what each group actually pushes, is done from Panorama › Device Groups, with a Preview Changes function letting you see the exact configuration diff a commit and push will produce before it happens.
In practiceAlways use Preview Changes before a device-group push affecting many firewalls at once. It is the equivalent of Terraform's plan step, showing the delta before it is applied, and it is the single best way to avoid an unintended estate wide policy change.

✓ Check yourself Panorama modes and device groups

  1. Name Panorama's three modes.
    Management only, Panorama mode (management + local collector), Log Collector mode (dedicated, no management).
  2. A firewall shows connected in Panorama but never receives policy. Most likely cause?
    Missing device group or template stack assignment: connectivity and configuration assignment are separate.
  3. How many device groups can one firewall belong to?
    Exactly one, typically the most specific, but it inherits from every ancestor above it.
  4. What is the topmost, implicit level above all device groups?
    Shared: for objects and policy meant to apply organisation wide.
  5. What does Preview Changes let you do before a push?
    See the exact configuration diff a commit and push will produce, before it actually happens.
  6. Should local overrides on a firewall be common or exceptional?
    Exceptional. Overusing them defeats the purpose of centralising through device groups.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Modes: management only, Panorama (mgmt + collector), Log Collector only.
  • A firewall needs both a device group and a template stack assigned.
  • Device groups carry policy, NAT, objects; hierarchical, one group per firewall.
  • Shared sits above all device groups.
  • Firewalls inherit the full ancestor chain, not just their own group.
  • Preview Changes shows the diff before a push, use it every time.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Assuming "connected" means "configured". They are separate checks.
  • Over centralising everything into Shared.
  • Pushing policy referencing an object that does not exist on every target firewall.
  • Skipping Preview Changes on a broad push.
  • Treating local overrides as the normal pattern rather than the exception.
ModeManages policy?Stores logs?
Management onlyYesNo, elsewhere
Panorama (default)YesYes, locally
Log Collector modeNoYes, only this

Part 15 · Panorama and ACC

Pre/Post Rulesets and Templates

Two named subitems in one page: how device group rules interleave with a firewall's own local rules, and how templates push device level configuration.

Mode
This is the most examinable Panorama concept, because it determines whose rules win when both Panorama and a local firewall administrator write policy.
Every device group's rulebase splits into Pre Rules and Post Rules, and a managed firewall's own local rulebase sits between the two when everything is combined at evaluation time.
The full evaluation order on a managed firewall is:
1› Device group Pre Rules (from every ancestor in the hierarchy, most general first, most specific last) → 2› the firewall's own local rules → 3› device group Post Rules (most specific first, most general last) → 4› the two implicit rules (intrazone-allow, interzone-deny) from Part 6.
Why this ordering existsPre Rules are for mandatory, organisation wide policy that must be evaluated first and cannot be bypassed by a local rule, block known malicious EDLs, deny to sanctioned bad categories, enforce a compliance baseline. Because they run before local rules, a local administrator cannot accidentally or deliberately create a rule that overrides them.
Post Rules are for catch all policy that should apply only if nothing more specific matched first: a default deny and log everything else rule, or logging requirements that need to see whatever local rules did not already handle. Because they run after local rules, they act as a safety net rather than a blocker.
A local administrator's own rules sit in the middle, giving them freedom to write site specific policy without being able to override the organisation wide Pre Rules above them, and knowing their rules will still be caught by the Post Rules safety net if something falls through.
In practiceState the order precisely if asked: Pre Rules, then local rules, then Post Rules, then the implicit rules. This is the mechanism that makes centralised governance actually enforceable rather than advisory, a local admin physically cannot write a rule that bypasses a Pre Rule sitting above it in evaluation order.

The full evaluation order on a managed firewall Pre Rules cannot be bypassed; Post Rules catch what falls through

A Template pushes device and network configuration to managed firewalls: interfaces, zones, virtual/logical routers, HA settings, log forwarding profiles applied at the device level, and other settings from Device and Network tabs, the configuration a device group's policy and objects model does not cover.
Template stacksA firewall is assigned a Template Stack, an ordered list of one or more individual templates, rather than a single template directly. Where multiple templates in a stack configure the same setting, the template higher in the stack order wins the conflict.
This lets you layer configuration the same way device groups layer policy: a base template with organisation wide standards (NTP servers, syslog destination, a baseline interface naming convention) with a site specific template stacked on top adding or overriding only what that site genuinely needs to differ.
Variables can be defined in a template and given a different value per firewall (or per template stack), a single template referencing a variable for "management IP" or "site specific VLAN ID" can be reused across many firewalls, each substituting its own value, rather than needing a nearly identical separate template per site.
As with device groups, a firewall can hold local overrides of template pushed settings for genuine device specific exceptions, visible and manageable from the firewall's own configuration view.
In practiceThe variables feature is what makes a small number of templates scale across a large, heterogeneous estate, without it you would need a near duplicate template per site just to change one IP address, defeating the purpose of centralising the configuration at all.
Pulling the last two topics together into an actual design decision, since the real test is whether you can architect this, not just define the terms.
Device group designStart with a top level group for genuinely universal policy (Pre Rules blocking known bad categories and EDLs, compliance mandated baseline rules) then branch into child groups by whatever dimension actually varies: region, business unit, or security tier. Keep the hierarchy as shallow as the estate genuinely needs; excessive depth makes it harder to reason about what a given firewall actually inherits.
Template designMirror the same instinct: a base template for settings true everywhere (NTP, DNS, syslog, a naming standard), with site or role specific templates stacked above it for genuine local variation, using variables rather than near duplicate templates wherever only a single value differs.
Where Pre/Post Rules fit into the designPut anything that is a compliance or security mandate, the kind of control from Part 8's compliance material that should never be bypassable, into Pre Rules at the top level device group. Put catch all logging and default deny policy into Post Rules so local rules still get their chance to match first.
The design failure to avoid: putting too much in Pre Rules removes local flexibility entirely and turns every site specific need into an exception request to the central team; putting too little there means the "mandatory" baseline is not actually enforceable, since a local rule evaluated first could override it.
In practiceThis is the Panorama equivalent of the automation design question from Part 6's Policy Optimizer material, centralise what should genuinely be centralised, leave room for legitimate local variation, and use the tooling (Preview Changes, variables, hierarchy) to keep the whole thing auditable rather than accumulating undocumented exceptions.

✓ Check yourself pre/post rules and templates

  1. State the full four stage evaluation order on a managed firewall.
    Device Group Pre Rules → local rules → Device Group Post Rules → implicit rules.
  2. Why can a local administrator never bypass a Pre Rule?
    Pre Rules are evaluated before local rules in the order, so nothing local is ever reached first.
  3. What is the purpose of Post Rules specifically?
    A safety net for logging or default deny that should apply only if nothing more specific (including local rules) already matched.
  4. What does a Template push that a Device Group does not?
    Device and network configuration: interfaces, zones, routers, HA, not policy or objects.
  5. In a template stack with a conflict, which template wins?
    The one higher in the stack order.
  6. What do template variables solve?
    Letting one template be reused across many firewalls with per firewall values, avoiding near duplicate templates per site.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Order: Pre Rules → local → Post Rules → implicit.
  • Pre Rules = mandatory, unbypassable. Post Rules = safety net.
  • Templates = device/network config. Assigned via a Template Stack.
  • Stack conflict: higher template wins.
  • Variables let one template serve many firewalls with different values.
  • Local overrides exist on both device groups and templates, keep them rare.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Forgetting local rules sit between Pre- and Post Rules, not after both.
  • Assuming Post Rules can override something. They only catch what's left.
  • Duplicating near identical templates instead of using variables.
  • Putting too much in Pre Rules and eliminating local flexibility.
  • Putting compliance mandated rules in Post Rules where they can be bypassed.
StageSourcePurposeBypassable by local rules?
1Device Group Pre RulesMandatory baselineNo
2Local rulesSite specific policyN/A, this is local
3Device Group Post RulesCatch all, logging, default denyYes, by design
4Implicit rulesintrazone-allow, interzone-denyYes, always last

Part 15 · Panorama and ACC

The Commit Model, Panorama HA and Log Collection

Not a single feature by itself, but the single most operationally important Panorama concept, commit and push are two separate actions, and confusing them is the most common real world mistake.

Mode
Panorama has its own candidate configuration, separate from any managed firewall's candidate configuration, and the relationship between changing something and it actually taking effect on a firewall involves two distinct actions that are easy to conflate.
CommitApplies Panorama's candidate configuration to Panorama's own running configuration: this makes the change real on Panorama itself: visible in the GUI, saved, versioned. It does not, by itself, send anything to a single managed firewall.
PushA separate action that takes Panorama's already committed device group and template configuration and sends it out to the managed firewalls assigned to those device groups and templates, where it becomes part of their candidate configuration, which they then must commit locally (automatically, as part of the push, in normal operation) to make active.
The critical implicationA Panorama commit alone changes nothing on any firewall. Only a push does. It is entirely possible to commit a change on Panorama and never push it, leaving every managed firewall running exactly what it was before, which is sometimes deliberate (staging several changes before pushing them together) and sometimes an operator simply forgetting the second step.
Commit Type options at push time let you choose which device groups, templates, or even individual firewalls receive this particular push, rather than always pushing to the entire managed estate at once, useful for staged rollouts of a risky change.
LocksPanorama supports configuration locks the same way a standalone firewall does, preventing two administrators from committing conflicting changes simultaneously, scoped per device group or globally.
In practiceThe two step model is the answer whenever a scenario says "I made a change in Panorama and nothing happened on the firewall", the change was committed but never pushed. This is the single most common Panorama operational mistake, and it is worth stating that plainly if asked.

Commit versus push two separate actions, and the mistake that happens when only one is remembered

Panorama supports Active/Passive HA between two Panorama instances, following the same conceptual shape as firewall HA from Part 12, applied to the management layer itself, because Panorama managing an entire estate is itself a single point of failure worth protecting.
Configuration synchronisation keeps the candidate and running configuration identical between the active and passive Panorama, and both instances can independently receive logs from managed firewalls (log data is not something that only flows through the active peer, unlike firewall session state over HA2).
Failure detection and election use the same underlying concepts as firewall HA (heartbeats, priority, and a defined active/passive relationship) though the specific link architecture differs since Panorama is a management platform rather than an inline traffic device.
Where this connects backIf Panorama itself is the single point of policy control for an entire estate, its own HA design deserves the same rigour as any critical piece of infrastructure covered elsewhere in this guide, and it is often the piece people forget to make redundant while carefully planning firewall HA everywhere else.
Verify with show high-availability state on Panorama itself (the same command family used for firewall HA), and the GUI's dedicated Panorama HA status view.
In practiceThe point worth making explicitly: losing Panorama does not stop firewalls from passing traffic, they continue operating on their last pushed configuration, but it does stop any centralised policy change or new firewall onboarding until Panorama is restored. Panorama HA protects management continuity, not traffic continuity.
This topic connects directly to Part 14: Panorama is the management point for the Log Collector Groups, forwarding preferences, and redundancy settings already covered there, but seen now from the Panorama configuration side rather than the collector side.
From Panorama › Collector Groups, you define which collectors belong to which group, configure the forwarding preference and redundancy settings per group, and assign managed firewalls to the appropriate Collector Group so their logs land in the right place.
Log forwarding profiles, revisitedThe Log Forwarding Profiles from Part 14 are themselves objects that can be pushed via device groups, ensuring every firewall in an estate applies the same forwarding logic (which log types go where, which built in actions fire) without configuring it individually per device, centralising the logging strategy the same way security policy is centralised.
Panorama's own log view at Monitor › Logs queries across every managed firewall's forwarded logs in one place, whether stored on local Collector Groups or in Strata Logging Service, giving a single estate wide search rather than needing to check each firewall's local logs individually.
This centralised view is also what feeds ACC, covered on the next page, the dashboards and reports there are only as complete as the logs actually being forwarded to Panorama or SLS in the first place.
In practiceIf ACC or a Panorama report looks incomplete for a given firewall, check whether that firewall's log forwarding profile and Collector Group assignment are actually correct before assuming the reporting feature itself is broken.

✓ Check yourself commit model, HA and log collection

  1. Does a Panorama commit alone change anything on a managed firewall?
    No. Commit makes the change real on Panorama only; push is what sends it to firewalls.
  2. What must happen on the firewall after a push for the change to become active?
    A local commit on the firewall, which happens automatically as part of normal push behaviour.
  3. What does losing Panorama do to firewalls already passing traffic?
    Nothing immediately: they keep running their last pushed configuration. Panorama HA protects management, not traffic.
  4. Does log data only flow through the active Panorama HA peer?
    No: unlike firewall HA2 session state, both Panorama peers can independently receive logs.
  5. Can Log Forwarding Profiles be pushed via device groups?
    Yes: centralising logging strategy the same way security policy is centralised.
  6. ACC looks incomplete for one firewall. What would you check first?
    That firewall's log forwarding profile and Collector Group assignment, not assume ACC itself is broken.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Commit = real on Panorama. Push = sent to firewalls.
  • Firewall still needs a local commit, automatic during a normal push.
  • Commit without push is the most common real world Panorama mistake.
  • Panorama HA protects management continuity, not traffic flow.
  • Both Panorama HA peers can independently receive logs.
  • Log Forwarding Profiles push via device groups, same as policy.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Assuming a committed change is already live on firewalls.
  • Forgetting to scope Commit Type and pushing to the whole estate unintentionally.
  • Thinking Panorama going down stops traffic. It does not.
  • Blaming ACC for gaps caused by a missing forwarding profile.
  • Not planning Panorama's own HA while carefully planning firewall HA.
ActionAffectsRequires a follow up step?
Commit (Panorama)Panorama's own running configYes, push, or nothing reaches firewalls
PushManaged firewalls' candidate configLocal commit, usually automatic
Commit (firewall, local)That firewall's running configNo, final step

Part 15 · Panorama and ACC

Administration, ACC Dashboards and Troubleshooting

This page covers building ACC dashboards and custom reports. Pair it with RBAC and access domains, then close the part with how to troubleshoot Panorama itself.

Mode
Panorama administration mirrors the firewall administration model from Part 9's Admin Role profiles, but adds one concept specific to managing an estate: Access Domains.
Admin roles on PanoramaBuilt in dynamic roles (Superuser, Panorama Administrator, and read only variants) plus custom Admin Role profiles defining exactly which tabs, features and commit/push capabilities an administrator has, the same granular model covered for firewalls, applied at the Panorama layer.
Access Domains, the Panorama specific pieceAn Access Domain restricts an administrator's visibility and control to specific device groups and templates, rather than the entire managed estate. An administrator assigned to an Access Domain covering only the EMEA device groups cannot see, commit to, or push configuration affecting APAC firewalls at all, even though both are managed by the same Panorama instance.
This is the Panorama equivalent of VSYS scoped administrators from Part 13, the same delegated administration pattern, applied across an estate of separate physical firewalls rather than within one shared appliance.
Access Domains and Admin Role profiles combine: an administrator's role defines what actions they can take, and their Access Domain defines what they can take those actions on, a regional network team might get a role permitting full policy edits, scoped by Access Domain to only their own region's device groups.
Authentication for Panorama administrators uses the same authentication profile and sequence model from Part 9, so RADIUS, SAML or certificate based login all apply here exactly as they do on a standalone firewall.
In practiceAccess Domains are the answer whenever a scenario describes regional teams, managed service provider customers, or any requirement that one Panorama instance serve multiple administrative boundaries without those boundaries seeing each other's configuration.

ACC: built from logs, not policy and the workflow from a dashboard widget to a saved report

The Application Command Center (ACC) is a dashboard built from logged data (Traffic, Threat, URL Filtering and the other log types from Part 14) presenting it as widgets: top applications by session count, top threats, top source and destination users, rule usage, geographic breakdowns of traffic.
On Panorama, ACC operates in Aggregated Monitoring mode: the same widgets and drill down interactions, but scoped across the entire managed estate rather than one firewall's logs, which is the whole reason to view it from Panorama rather than logging into each device individually.
InteractivityWidgets are interactive: clicking an entry (a specific application, a specific threat) filters the rest of the dashboard to that context, letting you narrow from "what does overall traffic look like" down to "show me everything related to this one application" without leaving the view.
Custom ReportsBeyond the built in ACC widgets, Monitor › Manage Custom Reports (or the Panorama equivalent) lets you define your own report against specific log types and filters, then schedule it to run on a recurrence and email the results automatically, turning an ad hoc investigation into a recurring, hands off piece of operational reporting.
Report Groups combine several individual reports into one scheduled bundle, useful for a weekly security summary combining threat trends, top denied applications, and admin activity into a single email rather than several separate ones.
Because ACC and reports are built entirely from forwarded logs, their completeness depends directly on the log forwarding and Collector Group configuration from the previous page, a firewall not forwarding logs correctly will simply be under represented or absent from these views, with no error to flag it.
In practiceBuilding ACC dashboards and custom reports is really about turning raw logs into something a non technical stakeholder can read on a schedule. Scheduled, emailed Custom Reports are the concrete deliverable that answers that.
A structured approach for the most common Panorama problems, following the same evidence before action discipline used throughout this guide's troubleshooting material.
Firewall not receiving policyCheck, in order: is it connected at all (show panorama-status on the firewall), does it have both a device group and template stack assigned, and was the change actually pushed after being committed, the three part chain from earlier in this part, any of which failing produces the same symptom.
Commit or push failuresCheck the Task Manager in Panorama for the specific commit or push job's error detail, a failed push often names exactly which firewall and which validation error caused it, rather than requiring guesswork.
Logs missing from ACC or MonitorWork backward through Part 14's chain: is the Log Forwarding Profile attached to the relevant rules, is the firewall's Collector Group assignment correct, and is that Collector Group itself healthy (show log-collector-es-cluster health).
Panorama itself unreachable or degradedCheck Panorama's own system resources the same way Part 1 covers for a firewall's management plane, a Panorama instance managing a very large estate can itself become resource constrained, and its symptoms (slow GUI, delayed pushes) look exactly like the management plane symptoms covered there.
Configuration sync issues between an HA pair of Panoramas, or between Panorama and a managed firewall, generally resolve with a manual full sync push once the underlying connectivity problem causing the drift has been fixed, the same recovery pattern as firewall HA config sync from Part 12.
In practiceNearly every Panorama problem in this guide reduces to one of three things: a missing assignment (device group, template, Collector Group), a forgotten push, or a resource constraint. Check those three before assuming something more exotic is wrong.

✓ Check yourself administration, ACC and troubleshooting

  1. What does an Access Domain restrict, specifically?
    An admin's visibility and control to specific device groups and templates, not the whole managed estate.
  2. What is the VSYS equivalent of a Panorama Access Domain?
    VSYS scoped administrators from Part 13, same delegated visibility pattern, different scale.
  3. What mode does ACC run in on Panorama, and what does it show?
    Aggregated Monitoring: the same widgets, scoped across the entire managed estate.
  4. What turns an ad hoc ACC investigation into recurring reporting?
    A Custom Report, scheduled with a recurrence and emailed automatically. Report Groups bundle several together.
  5. ACC is missing data for one firewall with no error shown. What do you check?
    That firewall's log forwarding profile and Collector Group assignment: incomplete forwarding shows as absence, not an error.
  6. Name the three most common root causes behind nearly every Panorama problem.
    A missing assignment (device group/template/collector group), a forgotten push, or a resource constraint.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Access Domains scope an admin to specific device groups/templates.
  • ACC on Panorama = Aggregated Monitoring, estate wide.
  • Widgets are interactive: click to filter and drill down.
  • Custom Reports can be scheduled and emailed; Report Groups bundle them.
  • ACC completeness depends entirely on log forwarding being correct.
  • Most Panorama issues: missing assignment, forgotten push, or resource constraint.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Confusing Access Domain scoping (visibility) with Admin Role (permissions). They combine.
  • Assuming an empty ACC widget means an error rather than missing forwarding.
  • Forgetting Report Groups exist and building several separate scheduled reports instead.
  • Not checking Task Manager for the actual push failure detail.
  • Treating Panorama's own resource exhaustion as a mysterious fault rather than the same pattern as firewall management plane load.
SymptomCheck first
Firewall not receiving policyConnection → device group/template assignment → was it pushed
Push or commit failsTask Manager for the specific error
Logs / ACC data missingLog forwarding profile and Collector Group assignment
Panorama slow or unresponsivePanorama's own system resources

Part 15 · Panorama and ACC

Quantum Readiness Across the Managed Estate

The same inventory problem the Quantum Readiness Dashboard solved for one firewall, back in Part 1, needs an answer at fleet scale too. This is that answer, inside Strata Cloud Manager.

Mode
This is an alternative view of the Command Center in Strata Cloud Manager, reached via the settings panel, and it's built to solve four specific problems at once rather than being a single report.
What it actually solves
  • Eliminates the inventory blind spot: the same "you don't know what crypto you're using" problem the Quantum Readiness Dashboard answers at the individual firewall level, here rolled up across the whole managed estate.
  • Provides a single pane of glass: one view rather than checking each firewall's own posture separately.
  • Delivers actionable remediation steps, not just a finding, a next action.
  • Tracks migration progress: the same multi year sequencing problem covered in Part 1, now with something to actually measure against.
Evaluating posture: three statesthe view classifies cryptographic posture into three tiers, based on the algorithms and protocols actually detected across the network:
  • Secure: quantum safe algorithms.
  • Weak: algorithms at quantum risk, still functional today but on the list to migrate.
  • Vulnerable: deprecated algorithms, the highest priority remediation targets.
Minimum requirements, worth knowing preciselyTo use the Quantum Readiness view at all, NGFWs onboarded into SCM must be:
  • At least generation four hardware.
  • Running PAN-OS 11.1 or higher.
  • Configured with a no-decrypt decryption policy rule that enables both successful and unsuccessful session logging: the same no decrypt plus logging pattern that makes the threat signatures covered on the Decryption page usable without actually decrypting anything.
  • Carrying a vulnerability protection profile.
The view itself is interactive: widgets update based on cursor position or click, and links drill through to more detailed data rather than everything being visible on one static screen.
In practiceThe requirements list is the part worth having exact, gen4+ hardware, PAN-OS 11.1+, a no-decrypt rule logging both outcomes, and a vulnerability protection profile. Missing any one of the four means the view either won't populate or won't show the full picture, and it's a genuinely common setup gap.

✓ Check yourself Quantum Readiness view

  1. Name the three posture states the Quantum Readiness view classifies algorithms into.
    Secure, Weak, Vulnerable.
  2. What's the minimum PAN-OS version and hardware generation to use this view?
    PAN-OS 11.1 or higher, on generation four hardware or later.
  3. What kind of decryption policy rule does the view require, and what must it log?
    A no-decrypt rule, logging both successful and unsuccessful sessions.
  4. What fourth requirement, beyond hardware/OS/logging, does the view need?
    A vulnerability protection profile attached.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Secure / Weak / Vulnerable: the three posture tiers.
  • Requires gen4+ hardware, PAN-OS 11.1+, a no-decrypt rule logging both outcomes, and a vulnerability protection profile.
  • It's an alternative Command Center view in SCM, not a separate product.
  • Solves the same inventory blind spot as the single firewall dashboard, at fleet scale.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Enabling the view without a no-decrypt rule that logs unsuccessful sessions too, only logging successes leaves a gap.
  • Assuming any onboarded firewall qualifies, gen4+ hardware and 11.1+ are hard floors.
  • Forgetting the vulnerability protection profile requirement entirely.

Part 16 · Software Updates

Content Updates, PAN-OS Upgrades and Backup

A recurring operational duty that touches almost everything else in the guide, rather than a single self contained topic. HA upgrade sequencing itself was covered in Part 12 · T119. This page covers what runs on top of that: content, version paths, and getting back to a known good state.

Mode

Read the topic, recall what you can, then reveal. Mark what's solid to track what still needs work. Controls act across every page.

PAN-OS separates content from the underlying software image. Content updates are smaller, more frequent packages, App-ID signatures, Threat and Anti-Spyware signatures, Antivirus signatures, WildFire signature updates, and URL category data, that refresh what the firewall can recognise without a full PAN-OS upgrade.
Managed from Device › Dynamic Updates: each content type shows its current version, can be checked for new releases, downloaded, and installed, with a schedule for automatic download and installation at a defined recurrence and time.
The risk that matters most, new App-IDsA content update can introduce new application signatures that PAN-OS did not previously recognise. Traffic that used to match a broad rule via application-default or a general category can suddenly be identified more specifically and match a different, more restrictive rule, or no rule at all, and get denied. This is a genuine, if narrow, availability risk from what looks like routine maintenance.
The mitigation PAN-OS providesContent release notes explicitly flag which App-IDs are new in that release. The disciplined pattern is: review the release notes before installing, use threshold based deployment (install content only after it has been available for a defined number of days, giving Palo Alto's own telemetry time to surface any widespread issue), and stage installation to a pilot device or group before an estate wide push via Panorama.
Antivirus and WildFire content are commonly set to install more aggressively (same day) since the risk of a missed threat signature usually outweighs the App-ID reclassification risk, while App-ID and Threat content are more often given a short delay for exactly this reason.
In practiceIf a scenario describes traffic that was working suddenly breaking with no policy or network change made, a recent content update introducing a new App-ID match is one of the first things to check, alongside show system info for the content version and the Traffic log's application field for what the session is now being classified as.

Content updates versus PAN-OS upgrades different cadence, different risk, different rollback story

PAN-OS releases are versioned major.minor (for example 11.1, 12.1), and you generally cannot skip a major version when upgrading, the firewall must pass through each intermediate major release's base image before installing the next, even if you never intend to run that intermediate version day to day.
Palo Alto publishes an official upgrade path per release on the support portal, and following it matters: jumping straight from an old major version to a much newer one is not supported and can leave the device in an inconsistent state, because each major release can carry schema or migration changes that the installer applies incrementally.
Preferred versus latestPalo Alto marks certain releases as a Preferred Release (a maturity signal, distinct from simply the newest available build) and the general guidance for production is to run a Preferred Release rather than chasing the latest release the day it ships, mirroring the same "let the ecosystem catch problems first" thinking behind content threshold deployment.
Before any upgradeCheck the release notes for known issues and deprecated features, confirm hardware and feature compatibility (a feature relied on in the current configuration is occasionally deprecated in a newer release), verify sufficient disk space for the new image, and back up the configuration, the topic covered next.
Verify the current and available versions with show system info and request system software info, and check the upgrade path itself against the support portal's published matrix rather than assuming.
In practiceThe specific trap worth naming: an admin upgrading a firewall that is several major versions behind and attempting to jump straight to current will find the installer refuses or the device behaves unpredictably. The fix is following the published path step by step, not forcing a shortcut.
PAN-OS automatically saves a configuration snapshot at every commit, visible and downloadable from Device › Setup › Operations, but relying only on automatic snapshots is not the same as a deliberate backup discipline.
Export optionsExport named configuration snapshot downloads the current running configuration as an XML file. Export configuration version lets you pull any specific historical commit's saved version, useful for comparing what changed between two points in time. Both should be taken and stored off device before any risky change, a config backup that only exists on the device being changed does not help if that device is the thing that fails.
RestoreLoad named configuration snapshot loads a previously exported file back into the candidate configuration, which then still needs a commit to become active, the same candidate/running distinction that governs every other configuration change on the platform. This means you can load an old configuration, review the diff against what is currently running, and decide deliberately before committing, rather than an immediate irreversible rollback.
Panorama managed firewallsA firewall's local configuration and its Panorama pushed configuration (device groups, templates) are distinct, and a full recovery needs both: restoring the firewall's local backup, and confirming its device group and template assignment in Panorama is intact so the next push reapplies the shared configuration correctly.
Before any major change (a PAN-OS upgrade, a significant policy restructure, a decryption rollout) the standard practice is: export a named snapshot immediately beforehand, store it somewhere the firewall's own failure cannot also take out, and confirm you know the exact restore procedure before you need it under pressure.
In practiceThe habit worth having cold for an interview answer: before any risky change, export a named configuration snapshot off device first. The automatic per commit history is a safety net, not a substitute for a deliberate, exportable, offsite backup taken at the moment that actually matters.

✓ Check yourself content updates, upgrade paths and backup

  1. Why can a content update break traffic with no policy change made?
    A new App-ID can reclassify traffic that used to match a broader rule, causing it to hit a different, more restrictive rule, or none.
  2. Name two mitigations for the new App-ID risk.
    Threshold based deployment (delay installation by a set number of days) and reviewing content release notes before installing.
  3. Can you generally skip a PAN-OS major version when upgrading?
    No: you must follow the published upgrade path through each intermediate major release.
  4. What does Palo Alto's "Preferred Release" designation mean?
    A maturity signal for production use, distinct from simply the newest available build.
  5. After loading a named configuration snapshot, is it immediately active?
    No: it loads into the candidate configuration and still needs a commit.
  6. Why is an on device automatic commit history not sufficient as a backup strategy?
    It does not help if the device itself fails: a deliberate, exportable, off device backup is needed before risky changes.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Content updates: App-ID, Threat, AV, WildFire, URL, frequent, no reboot.
  • Biggest content risk: new App-IDs reclassifying existing traffic.
  • PAN-OS upgrades: cannot skip major versions, follow the published path.
  • Preferred Release ≠ latest release.
  • Export a named snapshot off device before any risky change.
  • A restored snapshot loads to candidate; still needs a commit.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Assuming AV/WildFire and App-ID/Threat content should follow the same deployment delay.
  • Attempting to jump PAN-OS versions without following the published path.
  • Relying only on automatic per commit history as the backup strategy.
  • Forgetting Panorama pushed configuration is separate from a firewall's local backup.
  • Not checking hardware/feature compatibility before an upgrade.
Change typeCadenceNeeds a reboot?Primary risk
Content updateFrequent, often dailyNoNew App-ID reclassifies traffic
PAN-OS upgradePlanned, infrequentYesCannot skip major versions
Config restoreAs neededNo, until committedLoads to candidate, not live immediately

Part 17 · Cloud and Orchestration

Deployment Across AWS, Azure and GCP, and Bootstrapping

Two deployment models exist for every major cloud, and confusing them is the most common design mistake. Then the mechanics of getting a VM-Series instance from zero to configured with no human logging in at all.

Mode

Read the topic, recall what you can, then reveal. Mark what's solid to track what still needs work. Controls act across every page.

Every public cloud offers the same fundamental choice, and it is the first design decision on any cloud security project: who manages the firewall instance itself?
Self managed VM-SeriesYou deploy the VM-Series image yourself (from the AWS, Azure or GCP marketplace, or via Terraform) into your own VPC or VNet. You own the instance: sizing, patching, PAN-OS upgrades, and scaling are all your responsibility, typically managed through Panorama the same way an on premises estate is. This gives full configuration control, including features a managed service might not expose.
Cloud NGFW, the managed serviceCloud NGFW for AWS and Cloud NGFW for Azure are Palo Alto managed services, consumed natively through each cloud provider's own marketplace and billing, with the underlying firewall infrastructure, scaling, and PAN-OS version entirely managed by Palo Alto rather than the customer. Policy is still defined by the customer (via the cloud provider's own console, Panorama, or Strata Cloud Manager) but there is no instance to patch or size.
The trade off in one lineSelf managed gives full control at the cost of operational overhead. Cloud NGFW gives zero infrastructure management at the cost of some configuration depth. A team already running Panorama at scale, with established automation, often prefers self managed for consistency. A team wanting cloud native simplicity, or without deep firewall operations experience, often prefers Cloud NGFW.
Both models still rely on the same underlying App-ID, Content-ID and threat prevention engine covered throughout this guide, the difference is entirely in who operates the infrastructure, not in inspection capability.
In practiceIf a scenario asks about patching cadence, instance sizing, or PAN-OS version control in the cloud, that is self managed VM-Series. If it asks about a firewall with no instance to manage at all, consumed like any other cloud native service, that is Cloud NGFW.

Who operates the firewall the same inspection engine, two very different operational models

Bootstrapping is how a VM-Series instance goes from a blank image to a configured, licensed, managed firewall with no administrator logging in at all: essential for cloud auto scaling and any automated deployment pipeline.
The bootstrap package is a defined folder structure, /config, /content, /license, /software, /plugins: supplied at launch, in AWS typically as an S3 bucket referenced at instance creation.
The two configuration files, and how they combineinit-cfg.txt is mandatory: basic network settings for the management interface (static or DHCP), DNS, and optionally the Panorama IP, VM auth key, device group and template stack.
bootstrap.xml is optional: a full configuration, either written by hand or exported from an existing firewall's running configuration.
If both are present and their settings overlap, init-cfg.txt wins: the firewall merges the two but treats init-cfg.txt as authoritative on any conflict.
Auto registration with PanoramaA VM auth key generated on Panorama, included in init-cfg.txt, lets the newly booted firewall authenticate itself to Panorama on first contact. Supplying a device group and template stack in the same file means the firewall is fully onboarded and receiving policy within minutes of boot, with no manual step in Panorama at all.
The /license folder needs an auth code bundle, not individual auth codes, supplying individual codes means the firewall only retrieves the license tied to the first one it finds.
In practiceThis is what makes VM-Series genuinely cloud native rather than "a firewall someone happened to also put in the cloud": launch, bootstrap, and the instance is licensed, configured, and managed, the entire lifecycle scriptable and repeatable, which is the actual point of an autoscaling group.
Bootstrapping is what makes autoscaling possible: a cloud autoscaling group can launch a new VM-Series instance in response to load, and that instance bootstraps itself into a working, policy enforcing firewall with no human involved in the loop at all.
The patternA load based trigger (CPU, session count, throughput) causes the cloud provider's autoscaling group to launch a new instance from a template referencing the bootstrap package. The instance boots, reads its bootstrap configuration, registers with Panorama using the VM auth key, receives its device group and template stack assignment, and begins passing traffic, the whole sequence typically completing in minutes.
Scaling in is the less obvious half of the design: an instance being terminated needs its sessions drained gracefully rather than dropped mid flow, and needs to be cleanly removed from Panorama's managed devices list so it does not linger as a stale, unreachable entry.
Where Terraform fitsTerraform (covered later in this part) typically provisions the surrounding cloud infrastructure (the autoscaling group itself, load balancers, the bootstrap S3 bucket) while the bootstrap package handles what happens inside each instance once it launches. The two are complementary layers of the same automation, not competing tools.
This connects directly to the Multicloud Security Fabric covered later in this part: MSF's automated deployment and lifecycle management is, at its core, this same bootstrap and register pattern, extended and orchestrated across an entire multicloud estate rather than one autoscaling group.
In practiceThe design question worth asking in an interview: scaling out is the easy half; scaling in gracefully, with sessions drained and Panorama's inventory kept clean, is where automation projects actually fail.

✓ Check yourself cloud deployment models and bootstrapping

  1. What is the core difference between self managed VM-Series and Cloud NGFW?
    Who operates the infrastructure: self managed means you size/patch/scale it; Cloud NGFW means Palo Alto does, as a native marketplace service.
  2. Which bootstrap file is mandatory, and which is optional?
    init-cfg.txt is mandatory. bootstrap.xml is optional, for a full configuration.
  3. If init-cfg.txt and bootstrap.xml both set the same value differently, which wins?
    init-cfg.txt wins on any conflict.
  4. What does the VM auth key in init-cfg.txt enable?
    The newly booted firewall authenticates itself to Panorama on first contact, for automatic registration.
  5. Why must the license folder use an auth code bundle rather than individual codes?
    With individual codes, the firewall only retrieves the license tied to the first one found: a bundle lets it fetch all associated licenses.
  6. What is the harder half of autoscaling design: scaling out or scaling in?
    Scaling in: draining sessions gracefully and cleanly removing the instance from Panorama's inventory.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Self managed = you operate it. Cloud NGFW = Palo Alto operates it, native to AWS/Azure.
  • Bootstrap folders: /config, /content, /license, /software, /plugins.
  • init-cfg.txt mandatory and authoritative on conflict; bootstrap.xml optional.
  • VM auth key in init-cfg.txt enables Panorama auto registration.
  • License folder needs an auth code bundle, not individual codes.
  • Scaling in requires graceful session drain and Panorama cleanup.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Assuming Cloud NGFW means giving up policy control, only infrastructure ops changes.
  • Forgetting init-cfg.txt overrides bootstrap.xml on conflicting settings.
  • Supplying individual auth codes and getting only one license activated.
  • Designing autoscaling around scale out only, with no scale in plan.
  • Treating Terraform and bootstrapping as the same layer rather than complementary.
Self managed VM-SeriesCloud NGFW

ConsumptionMarketplace image / TerraformNative cloud provider service
Policy controlFull, via PanoramaFull, via console/Panorama/SCM

Part 17 · Cloud and Orchestration

NSX-T Service Insertion and CN-Series on Kubernetes

Two private cloud and container integrations that follow the same underlying pattern seen already in this guide: a distributed, per host option and a centralised, cluster option, trading operational simplicity against consolidated capacity.

Mode
NSX-T's own built in firewall is capable but lacks App-ID and the deeper inspection this guide has covered throughout. Service insertion is how a VM-Series firewall is attached into the NSX-T fabric to inspect traffic that NSX-T itself would otherwise just switch or route.
North south, attached to a Tier-0 gatewayThe Tier-0 (T0) gateway is NSX-T's edge, handling traffic leaving or entering the software defined data centre. Attaching a VM-Series service insertion here inspects north south traffic, internet bound or inter datacentre. Only one service insertion per gateway is supported, though the firewall itself can run in HA. The T0 gateway must be Active/Standby for this attachment; Active/Active is not a supported attachment point.
East west, attached to a Tier-1 gatewayA Tier-1 (T1) gateway sits at an application or tenant boundary, handling traffic between segments within the data centre. Attaching service insertion here inspects east west traffic, lateral movement between workloads, which is exactly the traffic a traditional perimeter only design misses entirely.
Two east west deployment modesDistributed (per host): a firewall instance runs on every ESXi host, inspecting that host's local traffic with no hop off the host at all. Centralised (service cluster): firewall instances run in a dedicated cluster, and traffic is redirected to it. Distributed minimises latency and blast radius per host; centralised is simpler to size and scale as one pool rather than per host capacity planning, the same distributed versus cluster trade off that reappears with CN-Series on Kubernetes in the next topic.
Keeping policy in sync with a dynamic environmentA Notify Group, configured in the service definition, tells NSX-T integrated firewalls about VM creation, deletion and movement in real time, which is what keeps Dynamic Address Groups from Part 6 accurate as workloads are created and destroyed, policy written against a DAG stays correct without anyone updating an address object by hand.
In practiceT0 for north south, T1 for east west is the fact to have instantly available. And the Notify Group to DAG connection is worth stating unprompted. It is what makes NSX-T integration genuinely dynamic rather than a one time insertion that immediately goes stale.

T0 versus T1, and the distributed/centralised pattern that keeps recurring the same trade off as HSF and CN-Series

CN-Series is the container native firewall, running as Kubernetes native pods rather than a virtual machine, purpose built to inspect traffic between pods that a VM based firewall sitting outside the cluster would never see.
The two podsCN-MGMT is the management plane, connecting to Panorama the same way any managed firewall does, licensing, device group and template stack assignment.
CN-NGFW is the dataplane, doing the actual App-ID, Content-ID and threat prevention work on pod to pod traffic.
PAN-CNI: how traffic actually reaches CN-NGFWA Container Network Interface (CNI) plugin configures networking for pods as they are created. PAN-CNI is inserted into the cluster's CNI plugin chain as a DaemonSet, and it reads an annotation on each application pod or namespace: paloaltonetworks.com/firewall: pan-fw, to decide whether that pod's traffic should be redirected to CN-NGFW for inspection as it enters and leaves the pod.
This is a genuinely different redirection mechanism from the VM-Series world: instead of a route or a security group steering traffic to a firewall, a Kubernetes annotation is what turns inspection on for a given namespace or workload.
Distributed versus cluster deployment, the same pattern againDistributed mode: CN-NGFW runs as a DaemonSet, one dataplane pod per node. Cluster (service) mode: CN-NGFW runs as a Kubernetes Service in dedicated security nodes, and is the recommended deployment mode. Same trade off as NSX-T's distributed versus centralised choice on the previous page, per node simplicity versus pooled, centrally sized capacity.
Deployment is via Helm charts, the standard Kubernetes package manager, and namespaces, Kubernetes' own tenant separation construct, are commonly used as the security boundary, letting policy be written per team or per application the same way zones separate traffic elsewhere in this guide.
In practiceThe detail worth having exact: an annotation, not a route, is what turns on inspection for a pod or namespace. That single fact answers most "how does traffic actually get to the CN-Series firewall" questions.

✓ Check yourself NSX-T and CN-Series

  1. Which gateway tier handles north south traffic, and which handles east west?
    Tier-0 = north south. Tier-1 = east west, at the application/tenant boundary.
  2. What HA mode must the T0 gateway be in for a service insertion attachment?
    Active/Standby: Active/Active is not a supported attachment point.
  3. What does a Notify Group do, and what does it keep accurate?
    Tells firewalls about VM creation/deletion/movement in real time, keeping Dynamic Address Groups accurate.
  4. Name the two CN-Series pods and their roles.
    CN-MGMT (management, connects to Panorama) and CN-NGFW (dataplane, App-ID/Content-ID/threat prevention).
  5. What actually decides whether a pod's traffic gets inspected by CN-NGFW?
    A Kubernetes annotation on the pod or namespace, read by PAN-CNI, not a route or security group.
  6. What is the recommended CN-Series deployment mode, and what pattern does it echo?
    Cluster (service) mode: the same distributed versus centralised trade off as NSX-T service insertion.

✓ Anchor facts

  • T0 = north south (SDDC edge). T1 = east west (app/tenant boundary).
  • T0 attachment requires Active/Standby; one insertion per gateway.
  • Notify Group keeps DAG membership accurate as VMs change.
  • CN-Series: CN-MGMT (control) + CN-NGFW (data), via PAN-CNI.
  • Traffic redirect is by pod/namespace annotation, not routing.
  • CN-Series cluster (service) mode is the recommended deployment.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Swapping T0 and T1, T0 is the edge, T1 is internal boundaries.
  • Assuming Active/Active works for a T0 service insertion attachment.
  • Forgetting the Notify Group is what keeps DAGs live, not a one time sync.
  • Expecting CN-Series traffic redirection to work like VM-Series routing.
  • Treating distributed and cluster/centralised modes as interchangeable with no trade off.
IntegrationDistributed / per nodeCentralised / clusterRecommended
NSX-T east westPer ESXi hostDedicated service clusterDepends on scale
CN-SeriesDaemonSet, per nodeKubernetes Service, dedicated nodesCluster mode

Part 17 · Cloud and Orchestration

The Two Orion Fabrics

Part 1 named these as Orion's two fabrics and deferred the detail here. Both are 12.1 Orion capabilities, both are genuinely new, and this guide states plainly where the material is recent enough that a live check is worth doing before an exam or an interview.

Mode
The Multicloud Security Fabric is Palo Alto's answer to a specific, named problem: security teams running fragmented visibility, inconsistent policies, and unsustainable manual effort across AWS, Azure, GCP and private data centres, each managed differently. MSF is described as four capabilities working together, not four separate products.
1 · Deep VisibilityAutomatic discovery of the entire multi cloud estate: VMs, Kubernetes clusters, AI applications and LLMs, plus the underlying network topology of VPCs/VNets, subnets and cloud native gateways. This builds a single topology map spanning clouds that would otherwise each need their own console.
2 · Intelligent AutomationAutomated deployment and lifecycle management for the software firewalls doing the inspection, plus automatic traffic steering, programming routes to redirect VM, container and AI workload traffic through the firewalls without manual route configuration. Palo Alto states this reduces what was previously 20 plus manual steps for firewall insertion down to an automated workflow.
3 · A Unified Data PlaneAn automated network mesh interconnecting application environments across clouds, providing consistent inspection for east west and cross cloud traffic. This introduces macro segments: a consistent Layer 3 segmentation strategy applied uniformly across the entire multicloud footprint, regardless of which cloud a given segment happens to sit in.
4 · Centralized ManagementThe whole fabric is governed from Strata Cloud Manager (SCM), defining and enforcing one uniform security policy across every cloud, with tools for cross cloud session insight and a drift detector to flag risky configuration changes.
This is the automated, multicloud scale version of the bootstrap and register pattern from earlier in this part, the same idea of a firewall configuring and registering itself, extended to an entire estate and orchestrated centrally rather than one instance at a time.
In practiceKnow the four pillars by function, not by name recall alone: discover everything, deploy and steer traffic automatically, inspect consistently across clouds, manage from one console. This is genuinely new material from the current PAN-OS 12.1 Orion release, worth a quick check against Palo Alto's own site for anything that has moved since this guide was written.

Multicloud Security Fabric: four pillars, one governing console discover, deploy, inspect, manage

Where MSF solves multicloud consistency, Hyperscale Security Fabric solves a narrower, sharper problem: capacity planning for a data centre where traffic can spike far above what any single firewall can absorb, without buying hardware sized for a theoretical peak that mostly sits idle.
HSF is a dynamic cluster of software firewalls with a built in load balancer, engineered for active active elasticity, scale, and resilience, without needing a third party external load balancer at all.
P-Nodes and S-NodesP-Nodes are fixed capacity NGFWs that do double duty: they inspect traffic and load balance to the layer behind them, exposed as a single IP using ECMP. S-Nodes sit behind the P-Nodes and auto scale based on session utilisation, absorbing the actual traffic volume as it fluctuates.
This is the same distributed versus centralised design language as NSX-T and CN-Series from the previous page, but resolved differently: rather than choosing one mode, HSF splits the two jobs across two node types: a fixed, predictable inspection and routing layer, and an elastic capacity layer behind it.
Scale and platform supportHSF scales from roughly 1 Gbps up to 200 Gbps. It is available on ESXi and KVM, with public cloud platform support described as coming; check current documentation before relying on a specific cloud target. A CN-Series equivalent exists for containerised, Kubernetes based deployments, aimed specifically at 5G and mobile service provider use cases.
Session resiliencyHSF provides session failover to healthy instances within the cluster and auto scaling driven by real time session load, so both a node failure and a genuine traffic spike are absorbed without manual intervention.
In practiceThe two node type architecture is the fact worth having exact: P-Nodes inspect and load balance; S-Nodes just scale. This, like MSF, is current release material, the platform support list in particular is worth a live check rather than treating this guide as the final word.

✓ Check yourself MSF and HSF

  1. Name the four MSF pillars.
    Deep Visibility, Intelligent Automation, a Unified Data Plane, Centralized Management (via Strata Cloud Manager).
  2. What does MSF's "macro segment" concept provide?
    A consistent Layer 3 segmentation strategy applied uniformly across the entire multicloud footprint.
  3. Roughly how many manual steps does MSF claim to reduce firewall insertion from?
    20 plus manual steps, down to an automated workflow.
  4. What does a P-Node do in HSF?
    Fixed capacity NGFW that inspects traffic and load balances to the S-Nodes behind it, exposed via ECMP.
  5. What does an S-Node do, and how does it differ from a P-Node?
    It auto scales based on session utilisation. It absorbs capacity, it does not load balance.
  6. What throughput range does HSF scale across?
    Roughly 1 Gbps to 200 Gbps.

✓ Anchor facts

  • MSF pillars: Deep Visibility, Intelligent Automation, Unified Data Plane, Centralized Management.
  • MSF is managed from Strata Cloud Manager, with a drift detector.
  • HSF: P-Nodes inspect + load balance (fixed); S-Nodes auto scale.
  • HSF exposes as one IP via ECMP, no third party load balancer needed.
  • HSF scale: roughly 1 to 200 Gbps, on ESXi and KVM today.
  • Both are current PAN-OS 12.1 Orion material, verify against live docs.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Confusing MSF (multicloud consistency) with HSF (single data centre elasticity).
  • Assuming P-Nodes auto scale. They are the fixed layer; S-Nodes scale.
  • Treating HSF as needing an external load balancer. It is built in.
  • Stating platform support (especially public cloud) as fixed rather than evolving.
  • Forgetting MSF is four pillars working together, not four separate purchases.
Multicloud Security FabricHyperscale Security Fabric
SolvesMulticloud consistency and visibilitySingle site elastic capacity
ScopeAWS, Azure, GCP, private DCOne data centre / cluster
Managed viaStrata Cloud ManagerPanorama / SCM, per platform
