Part 17 · Cloud and Orchestration

APIs, Terraform, and CLARA

The last piece: the two APIs everything in this part is actually built on, the Terraform provider that wraps them, and CLARA, the assessment that tells an organisation where to start.

Mode
Every automation covered in this part (bootstrapping, autoscaling, MSF's traffic steering, Terraform) ultimately calls one of two APIs on the firewall or Panorama. Knowing which is which, and that Terraform sits on top of them rather than being a third option, ties this whole part together.
The XML API, full control, older interfaceA low level API covering every feature the firewall has, usable directly against a firewall or Panorama. Its type parameter selects the operation: config for reading or writing configuration, op for operational commands, commit, and log for retrieving logs, the same log-query syntax covered in Part 18 works here too. Depth and completeness are its strength; verbosity is its cost.
The REST API, newer, resource shapedExposes policy objects as top level URIs (address objects, security rules, NAT rules) each independently addressable with standard HTTP verbs. Simpler to reason about for straightforward CRUD style automation, but it does not yet cover every corner the XML API does.
The Terraform provider, built on top of bothThe official panos Terraform provider is now on its 2.x generation, auto generated from the same normalised PAN-OS schemas that generate the Go SDK, rather than hand maintained resource by resource. Under the hood it drives the XML API, and a performance feature called multi-config batching combines up to 500 XML API calls into a single HTTP request by default, a direct answer to the "many small changes are slow" problem that plain scripted API calls run into at scale.
A separate official Terraform module (distinct from the provider) generates and uploads a complete bootstrap package to an AWS S3 bucket: the exact bootstrap mechanics from earlier in this part, expressed declaratively rather than assembled by hand.
Where Ansible and Python fit, cross referencing the interview materialAnsible's PAN-OS collection and the pan-os-python SDK (covered in the interview supplement's automation page) both also call these same two APIs underneath, the choice between Terraform, Ansible and raw Python is a question of workflow and team preference, not of which one has access to different underlying capability.
In practiceThe layering to state precisely: XML API is the full featured original; REST API is the newer, simpler, resource shaped alternative; Terraform, Ansible and pan-os-python all sit on top of one or both, adding declarative or scripted convenience rather than new capability.

Everything in this part calls one of two APIs Terraform, Ansible and Python are convenience layers on top, not separate capability

CLARA (Cloud Network & AI Risk Assessment) is a Palo Alto delivered assessment that scans an organisation's cloud and AI estate to produce a risk brief, explicitly positioned as having zero impact on production environments: it is a read only assessment, not a deployment.
The problem it targetsPalo Alto's own framing: as organisations shift from multicloud expansion into AI native workloads, shadow AI emerges, assets and integrations that exist without visibility or governance, hidden behind the operational noise of a large, fragmented estate. CLARA is pitched as the first, low friction step to find out where that exposure actually is before committing to a broader remediation project.
Where it fits relative to everything else in this partCLARA is the assessment; MSF's Deep Visibility pillar is the ongoing discovery capability that keeps working after the one time assessment. In practice, CLARA is a reasonable entry point for an organisation that has not yet deployed MSF at all, establishing the baseline that justifies the investment, rather than assuming the investment first and discovering the gaps later.
This mirrors a pattern already covered in the interview material: it is the same "assess before you commit" instinct behind Policy Optimizer's evidence based rule rewriting, or a staged rollout in the automation topic, look before acting, on a larger, organisation wide scale.
Sign up and delivery is through Palo Alto directly, producing a report intended for a leadership or board level audience as much as a technical one. It is explicitly framed as decision support, turning "we don't know our exposure" into a quantified starting point for budget and prioritisation conversations.
In practiceKnow the acronym expansion cold, Cloud Network and AI Risk Assessment, and the one line that captures its purpose: a zero impact scan that turns unknown multicloud and AI exposure into a quantified, prioritised starting point.

✓ Check yourself APIs, Terraform and CLARA

  1. What does the XML API's type parameter select between?
    config, op, commit, log: the operation being performed.
  2. How does the REST API differ structurally from the XML API?
    It exposes policy objects as top level URIs with standard HTTP verbs, rather than one general purpose XML endpoint.
  3. Which API does the Terraform provider actually drive underneath?
    The XML API: Terraform is a declarative layer on top, not a separate capability.
  4. What does multi-config batching do, and what's the default limit?
    Combines multiple XML API calls into one HTTP request; default up to 500 calls per batch.
  5. What does CLARA stand for?
    Cloud Network & AI Risk Assessment.
  6. What is CLARA's defining operational characteristic?
    Zero impact on production: a read only assessment, not a deployment.

✓ Anchor facts

  • XML API = full feature coverage, older, config/op/commit/log.
  • REST API = newer, resource shaped, not yet full parity.
  • Terraform panos provider is 2.x, auto generated, drives the XML API.
  • Multi config batching: up to 500 XML calls per HTTP request, default.
  • CLARA = Cloud Network & AI Risk Assessment, zero production impact.
  • CLARA targets shadow AI and fragmented multicloud exposure specifically.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Treating Terraform as a third API rather than a layer on the XML API.
  • Assuming the REST API already has full XML API feature parity.
  • Forgetting CLARA is an assessment, not an ongoing capability like MSF's discovery.
  • Missing that tool choice (Terraform/Ansible/Python) is workflow, not capability.
  • Not verifying REST API coverage before committing automation to it for an edge case feature.

Part 18 · Troubleshooting

Packet Flow, First Packet and the Session Table

Every troubleshooting question in this guide has assumed you know what happens to a packet between arrival and departure. This page makes that path explicit, because reading a capture or a counter means nothing without it.

Mode

Read the topic, recall what you can, then reveal. Mark what's solid to track what still needs work. Controls act across every page.

Single Pass Parallel Processing from Part 1 describes what the data plane does to a packet. This topic is when it does it, not every packet in a session gets the same treatment, and knowing which stage a packet is in explains most confusing behaviour.
First packet, the slow pathThe first packet of a new session triggers the expensive work: a route lookup, a zone lookup, a security policy match walking the rulebase top to bottom, NAT translation lookup, and App-ID's initial classification attempt. This is genuinely more CPU intensive than everything after it, because none of the answers exist yet. They all have to be computed.
Once that work completes, the result is written into the session table as a new entry, keyed on the session's 6 tuple (source/destination IP, source/destination port, protocol, and the security zone).
Every subsequent packet, the fast pathA packet belonging to an existing session is matched against the session table directly. If it matches, the firewall reuses the policy decision, zone, and NAT translation already computed on the first packet. It does not rewalk the security rulebase or rerun the route lookup. This is dramatically cheaper, which is why session setup rate and throughput are reported as two separate performance numbers on a datasheet.
Where App-ID complicates thisApp-ID is not always fully resolved on the first packet, some applications only reveal their identity after several packets of payload. The session is created on packet one with a provisional identification (often the app matching the port, or "incomplete"), and policy can reevaluate once App-ID reaches a final verdict, potentially changing which security profile or even which rule now applies to that already open session. This is the session end reason policy deny appearing mid session rather than at session start, and it is a frequent source of "why did this get blocked after it was already working" questions.
Verify session state and which path a flow is taking with show session all filter (narrow by source, destination, or application) and show session id <id> for the full detail on one session, including its current App-ID verdict.
In practiceThe one line version worth having ready: first packet is expensive and decides everything; every packet after it is cheap and just checks the session table: except when App-ID's identification changes mid flow and forces a reevaluation.

First packet versus every packet after it the session table is what makes the fast path fast

The session table is the data plane's live record of every active flow, the object the fast path checks against, and the single most useful place to look when a flow is behaving unexpectedly right now.
Each entry carries the 6 tuple key (source/destination IP, source/destination port, protocol, zone), plus the resolved application, NAT translation, security rule matched, and current state.
Session states worth recognisingINIT: the session is being set up, first packet processing in progress.
ACTIVE: established and passing traffic normally.
CLOSING / CLOSED: a graceful TCP teardown (FIN) in progress or complete.
DISCARD: the firewall has decided to drop this session's packets but is still tracking the session itself, typically to avoid reevaluating a denied flow on every subsequent packet, a discard entry existing is not evidence the firewall is passing that traffic.
Reading a specific sessionshow session id <id> gives the full picture: which rule matched, the current application verdict, byte and packet counters in each direction, the NAT translation applied, and which HA peer owns it if the platform is in active/active from Part 12.
Session timeouts are protocol- and application specific (a TCP session has a different idle timeout than UDP or ICMP, and some applications carry their own timeout override), configured at Objects › Applications or the session settings under Device › Setup › Session. A session sitting idle past its timeout is aged out and removed from the table, which is itself a common cause of an application that "randomly disconnects" after a period of inactivity.
In practiceIf traffic that should be denied is somehow still flowing, or traffic that should work is not, checking show session all filter for that specific flow and reading its state and matched rule directly is almost always faster than rereading the policy configuration from scratch.
Global counters are the data plane's internal instrumentation, every packet processed increments some set of counters as it moves through the pipeline, and reading them tells you where in the pipeline packets are actually being dropped, rather than just that they are being dropped.
Viewed with show counter global filter delta yes: the delta yes filter is the detail worth remembering, since it shows only counters that have changed since the last time you ran the command, turning an overwhelming full list into just the counters actively incrementing right now while you reproduce a problem.
Counter categories worth recognisingCounters are grouped by the stage of processing they represent: flow (session setup and packet forwarding decisions), tcp (TCP specific state handling), pktproc (packet processing pipeline stages), ssl (decryption related), and others matching the pipeline stages covered throughout this guide. A counter name like flow_policy_deny or flow_fwd_l3_noroute names the exact reason a packet was dropped, directly.
The workflowRun the filtered command, reproduce the problem (send the traffic that is failing), run the command again, and compare, whichever counters incremented during the reproduction window are the ones that actually fired for this specific problem, cutting through the hundreds of counters that exist for scenarios unrelated to what you are chasing.
This pairs directly with the session table from the previous topic: the session table tells you what happened to a specific flow; global counters tell you why, at the pipeline stage level, when the session table's own fields do not make the cause obvious.
In practiceThe counter workflow is the answer whenever a scenario describes drops with no clear cause in the Traffic log, a packet dropped before a session is even created (a malformed packet, a route lookup failure) may never generate a Traffic log entry at all, but it will always increment a global counter.

✓ Check yourself packet flow and the session table

  1. What work happens on the first packet of a session that does not happen again?
    Route lookup, zone lookup, the full security rulebase walk, NAT lookup, and initial App-ID classification.
  2. Why can a session be denied mid flow with no configuration change made?
    App-ID's identification changed once enough payload arrived, forcing policy reevaluation against the new verdict.
  3. Does a session in DISCARD state mean the firewall is passing that traffic?
    No: DISCARD means the firewall is still tracking the session while actively dropping its packets.
  4. What does the "delta yes" filter do on a global counter command?
    Shows only counters that have changed since the last run, isolating what fired during a specific reproduction.
  5. Why might a dropped packet never appear in the Traffic log?
    A packet dropped before a session is created (malformed packet, route failure) may generate no log entry, but always increments a global counter.
  6. What is the relationship between the session table and global counters when troubleshooting?
    The session table shows what happened to one flow; global counters show why, at the pipeline stage level.

✓ Anchor facts

  • First packet = full lookup and rulebase walk. Every packet after = session table match only.
  • App-ID can be provisional on packet one and revise later, forcing reevaluation.
  • Session states: INIT, ACTIVE, CLOSING/CLOSED, DISCARD.
  • show session id <id> gives rule, app, NAT, counters, HA owner.
  • show counter global filter delta yes isolates counters firing right now.
  • A dropped packet with no session yet may have no Traffic log entry at all.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Assuming all packets in a session get the same expensive processing as the first.
  • Treating a mid session deny as a bug rather than an App-ID reevaluation.
  • Reading DISCARD as "not being tracked" rather than "being tracked and dropped".
  • Running show counter global without the delta filter and drowning in noise.
  • Searching only the Traffic log for a drop that never created a session.
StateMeaning
INITFirst packet setup in progress
ACTIVEEstablished, passing traffic
CLOSING / CLOSEDGraceful TCP teardown in progress or done
DISCARDStill tracked, actively dropping packets
● NETWORKING● DEVICE SETTINGS● INTEGRATION● CONTEXT KNOWCONFIGUREVERIFY

Part 18 · Troubleshooting

Packet Capture and a Structured Approach

Packet capture was mentioned in passing across earlier parts, certificates, decryption, tunnels. This page is the dedicated treatment: the four stages, and how to sequence a troubleshooting session so capture is the last resort, not the first reflex.

Mode
PAN-OS packet capture is staged: a packet can be captured at up to four distinct points as it moves through the pipeline, and which stage it appears in tells you where it died before you have read a single byte of the capture itself. This is the detail that makes Palo Alto capture more diagnostically useful than a plain interface level tcpdump.
Receive (RX): the packet as it arrives on the ingress interface, before any firewall processing at all. If a packet does not appear here, it never reached the firewall, the problem is upstream, in cabling, switching, or routing before this box.
Firewall: the packet as it passes through the security processing engine itself: policy lookup, App-ID, Content-ID, decryption. A packet appearing at Receive but not Firewall means the packet was consumed by an earlier stage before reaching security processing, commonly a zone or interface configuration issue.
Drop: captures only packets the firewall has decided to discard, at whatever stage that decision was made. A packet appearing here, and here only, confirms the firewall itself made an active decision to drop it, check the matched rule and the session's discard reason from the previous page next.
Transmit (TX): the packet as it leaves the firewall on the egress interface, after all processing completed. A packet reaching Firewall but never appearing at Transmit, with no corresponding Drop entry either, points at something failing silently inside processing rather than a clean policy decision, worth escalating with a support case if global counters do not explain it either.
Reading the four stages togetherEnable all four simultaneously with a tight filter (specific source, destination, port) at Monitor › Packet Capture or the CLI equivalent, then reproduce the problem. Which stages the packet appears in, and which it does not, localises the failure without needing to read the packet contents at all, the stage pattern alone is often the entire diagnosis.
In practiceState the four stages in order if asked: Receive, Firewall, Drop, Transmit. A packet at Receive but nowhere else is a pre firewall problem. A packet at Receive and Drop is a firewall decision. A packet at all four except Transmit, correlated against global counters, is where deeper investigation actually needs to start.

The four capture stages, and what each pattern of presence/absence means diagnosis before you've read a single byte

Every tool covered in this part has a cost, and the discipline that runs through this whole guide applies here most directly: work from cheap and broad to expensive and narrow, gathering evidence before changing anything.
1 · Define the symptom preciselyExact source, destination, port, application, and time. "It's slow" or "it doesn't work" is not yet a troubleshooting problem, a specific flow that fails in a specific way is.
2 · Check the cheap sources firstThe Traffic and Threat logs from Part 14, was the session even seen, what rule matched, what was the session end reason. This alone resolves the majority of "why was this blocked" questions without touching anything else.
3 · Check the session tableFrom the previous page, is there an active session for this flow right now, what state is it in, which rule and application does the firewall currently believe apply.
4 · Check global countersIf the logs show nothing and no session exists, the packet may be failing before a session is ever created, global counters with the delta filter will show this even when nothing else does.
5 · Packet capture lastOnly once the above has narrowed the problem to a specific flow and a rough idea of where it fails. Capture confirms a hypothesis; it rarely generates one efficiently on its own, and an unfiltered capture on a busy interface costs real CPU.
The habits that cut across every part of this guideFilter before capturing. Check show system resources before assuming a data plane problem when the symptom might be management plane (Part 1). Check the obvious layer first (DNS, then reachability, then application) rather than reaching for the most sophisticated tool immediately.
In practiceThis five step order is the answer to almost any "how would you troubleshoot X" question in an interview: define the symptom, logs, session table, counters, capture: cheapest and broadest evidence first, expensive and narrow tools only once the problem is already mostly localised.
A quick reference set of patterns this guide has built toward, pulled together as the kind of pattern matching an experienced engineer does automatically.
Worked yesterday, fails today, no changes made: check the content update version from Part 16; a new App-ID reclassifying traffic is the leading cause with literally no configuration change involved.
One application fails, everything else on the same path is fine: almost always certificate pinning or mutual TLS breaking under decryption (Part 8), or an App-ID dependency issue (Part 7) where the parent application was denied.
Works from one site, fails from another, same rule and objects: check device group and template assignment in Panorama (Part 15) before assuming a policy problem; the site may not actually be receiving what you think it is.
VPN connects, but no traffic passes: check security policy for the tunnel zone (Part 10/11) before rechecking Phase 1 and Phase 2; a successful tunnel with no policy permitting it is the single most common GlobalProtect and site to site support call.
Slow GUI or commit, traffic flowing fine: management plane, not data plane (Part 1); check show system resources before considering a reboot.
HA failover happened with no obvious cause: check link and path monitoring thresholds (Part 12) and whether preemption is enabled and flapping against a marginal priority difference.
The thread running through all six: the actual root cause is almost never where the symptom first appears to point, which is exactly why the structured, cheap to expensive approach from the previous topic outperforms guessing based on the symptom alone.
In practiceThese six patterns cover a large share of real support calls on this platform. Recognising the shape of a problem, not the specific config, is what separates fast diagnosis from starting the five step process from zero every time.

✓ Check yourself capture stages and structured troubleshooting

  1. Name the four packet capture stages, in order.
    Receive, Firewall, Drop, Transmit.
  2. A packet appears at Receive only, nowhere else. What does that tell you?
    It was consumed before security processing: check zone or interface configuration, not policy.
  3. A packet appears at Receive and Drop. What's confirmed?
    The firewall made an active decision to discard it: check the matched rule and session discard reason next.
  4. State the five step troubleshooting order.
    Define the symptom, logs, session table, global counters, packet capture: cheap and broad before expensive and narrow.
  5. Why should packet capture be the last resort, not the first tool reached for?
    It confirms a hypothesis rather than efficiently generating one, and an unfiltered capture costs real CPU.
  6. Traffic that worked yesterday fails today with no config change. First thing to check?
    The content update version: a new App-ID may have reclassified the traffic.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Capture stages, in order: Receive → Firewall → Drop → Transmit.
  • The pattern of which stages a packet appears in is the diagnosis.
  • Troubleshooting order: symptom → logs → session table → counters → capture.
  • Always filter before capturing.
  • Rule out management plane before assuming a data plane fault.
  • "No changes made, but it broke" often means a content update did.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Reaching for packet capture before checking logs and the session table.
  • Running an unfiltered capture on a production interface.
  • Misreading a Drop stage capture as evidence of a network problem rather than a policy decision.
  • Assuming "no config change" rules out content updates as a cause.
  • Diagnosing a VPN traffic failure by rechecking IKE before checking the tunnel zone's security policy.
PatternPresent inAbsent fromMeaning
Never arrived, All fourProblem is upstream of the firewall
Pre processing dropReceiveFirewall, Drop, TransmitZone/interface issue, not policy
Policy decisionReceive, DropFirewall (partial), TransmitFirewall actively discarded it
Silent failureReceive, FirewallDrop, TransmitFailing inside processing, check counters

Part 18 · Troubleshooting

Actually Querying the Logs

Part 14 covered what each log type records, and the previous page assumed you'd "check the logs" as step two of any investigation. This page is the part that was missing: the actual filter syntax, in the GUI and the CLI, to pull the entry you need out of millions of others.

Mode
Every log view under Monitor › Logs › Traffic (or Threat, URL Filtering, Decryption, System, Configuration, and the rest from Part 14) shares the same filter language, entered in the search bar at the top of the log view. Learning it once covers every log type.
The basic shapeA filter is ( field operator value ), and filters chain together with and / or. The GUI's Add Filter builder generates this syntax for you by clicking on any value in an existing log row, clicking a source IP in a log entry automatically adds ( addr.src in x.x.x.x ) to the search bar, which is the fastest way to build a filter without memorising field names.
Common fields worth knowing directlyaddr.src / addr.dst: source or destination address, accepts a single IP, a subnet, or a range.
zone.src / zone.dst: source or destination zone.
port.src / port.dst: source or destination port.
app: the identified application.
action: allow, deny, drop, reset-client, reset-server, reset-both.
rule: the security rule name that matched.
receive_time: useful with geq / leq to bound a time window precisely, tighter than the GUI's preset time range dropdown.
Worked examples( addr.src in 10.1.1.5 ) and ( app eq ssl ): everything from one host using SSL/TLS.
( action eq deny ) and ( zone.src eq trust ): every denied session originating from the trust zone, the fastest way to spot a rule that's blocking more than intended.
( port.dst eq 443 ) and ( action neq allow ): anything to port 443 that was not allowed, useful when a specific service is intermittently failing.
The Threat log adds fields specific to it: severity (critical, high, medium, low, informational) and category (the threat type) are the two used most, e.g. ( severity geq high ) to cut straight to what actually matters during an active incident.
In practiceThe habit worth having: click before you type. Clicking a field value in any existing log row builds the correct filter syntax automatically, which is faster and less error prone than typing field names from memory, save the memorised syntax for when you're working from the CLI instead.

Building a filter without memorising syntax the GUI generates it; the CLI needs you to know it

( addr.src in 10.1.1.5 ) and ( app eq ssl ) works identically in both places learn it once from the GUI's click to build, and it transfers directly to the CLI when that's all you have
The CLI equivalent matters whenever you only have SSH access: a management interface that's up but with the GUI degraded, or simply working faster from a terminal already open for other commands.
Pulling historical entriesshow log traffic direction equal backward query "( addr.src in 10.1.1.5 )": the query argument takes exactly the same filter syntax as the GUI search bar, in quotes. Swap traffic for threat, url, system, or any other log type from Part 14 to query that log instead. direction equal backward returns the most recent entries first, which is what you want during an active investigation.
Watching logs live, the most useful command for active troubleshootingtail follow yes filter "( addr.src in 10.1.1.5 )", run against the log type in the current CLI context (less mp-log and tail mp-log also apply to specific management plane log files by name). This streams matching entries as they happen, which is the single most effective technique for an in progress problem: apply a tight filter, reproduce the issue on the affected host, and watch the exact log entry appear in real time rather than searching after the fact.
Live tailing pairs directly with the global counters workflow from the previous page, run the counter command and the tail in two terminal panes, reproduce the problem once, and you see both what the logs recorded and which counters incremented, from the same single reproduction.
On PanoramaThe same show log syntax works from the Panorama CLI, querying across managed firewalls' forwarded logs rather than one device's local log, which is the CLI equivalent of the estate wide Monitor view from Part 15.
In practiceFor an active, reproducible problem, tail follow yes with a tight filter is usually faster than anything else in this guide. You see the exact log entry the moment the problem happens, with no need to guess a time window or page back through history afterward.
Neither is strictly better. They suit different moments in an investigation, and knowing when to switch is itself part of working efficiently.
GUI strengthsClick to build filters mean no syntax to remember, columns can be added or removed to see exactly the fields relevant to this investigation, and a result set can be exported directly to CSV for sharing with someone who doesn't have firewall access. This is the right choice for exploratory investigation. You don't yet know exactly what you're looking for.
CLI strengthsWorks when the GUI itself is the problem (Part 1's management plane symptoms), scripts and repeats identically across many devices, and tail follow yes has no GUI equivalent at all, the GUI can auto refresh a search, but it does not stream matching entries the instant they occur the way the CLI tail does. This is the right choice once you know precisely what you're filtering for and need to watch it happen live.
The combined workflow that actually worksStart in the GUI, click through a few log entries to understand the shape of the problem and build the filter syntax by clicking rather than guessing. Once the filter is right, take that exact syntax to the CLI and run it as a live tail while reproducing the problem, getting the best of both: the GUI's ease of exploration, and the CLI's real time visibility that nothing else in this guide provides.
This mirrors the structured approach from the previous page exactly: broad and cheap first (GUI exploration), narrow and precise once you know what you're looking for (CLI live tail): the same cost discipline applied to log tooling rather than to the choice between logs and packet capture.
In practiceIf asked how to troubleshoot a live issue, this two step answer is stronger than naming either tool alone: build the filter visually in the GUI, then run it as a live CLI tail while reproducing the problem: it demonstrates both the syntax knowledge and the operational judgement of when each interface earns its place.

✓ Check yourself querying and tailing logs

  1. What's the fastest way to build a correct GUI log filter without memorising field names?
    Click a value in an existing log row: the GUI writes the filter syntax for you.
  2. Which field would you use to find every session denied from the trust zone?
    ( action eq deny ) and ( zone.src eq trust )
  3. What does the CLI's query argument accept?
    The exact same filter syntax as the GUI search bar, passed in quotes.
  4. What can tail follow yes do that the GUI cannot?
    Stream matching log entries the instant they occur, no GUI equivalent exists for true real time tailing.
  5. What's the two step workflow that combines GUI and CLI strengths?
    Build the filter visually in the GUI, then run it as a live CLI tail while reproducing the problem.
  6. Why is the CLI sometimes the only option, regardless of preference?
    When the GUI itself is degraded, a management plane symptom from Part 1, SSH and the CLI may be all that's available.

✓ Anchor facts

  • Filter shape: ( field operator value ), chained with and/or.
  • Key fields: addr.src/dst, zone.src/dst, port.src/dst, app, action, rule.
  • Click a log value to auto build the filter, fastest GUI method.
  • CLI: show log <type> query "( ... )" for history.
  • tail follow yes filter "( ... )" streams entries live, no GUI equivalent.
  • Workflow: build in GUI, tail in CLI.

💡 Easy to get wrong

  • Typing filter syntax from memory when clicking a log value is faster and error free.
  • Forgetting the CLI query syntax is identical to the GUI's, not a separate language.
  • Reaching for GUI auto refresh when a true live tail is what's actually needed.
  • Not knowing any CLI log syntax when the GUI is the thing that's down.
  • Skipping straight to CLI tailing before knowing what filter is actually worth watching for.

zone.src / zone.dstSource / destination zonezone.src eq trust
appIdentified applicationapp eq ssl

56

Fast Recall

Parts 1 to 9 · Everything in the Room, No Explanation

No questions, no reveal, no quiz, just the facts this guide already earned across 51 pages, compressed to what you'd want in front of you five minutes before you walk in. If a line doesn't make sense on sight, that topic needs another pass before today.

✓ 1 · Platform

  • Single pass = inspected once. Parallel = networking/security on dedicated hardware at once.
  • Mgmt plane vs data plane diagnosed separately, show system resources vs show running resource-monitor.
  • PA = hardware. VM = any hypervisor/IaaS. CN = Kubernetes pods.
  • Orion 12.1: Quantum Readiness Dashboard, cipher translation, 10M user contexts, passwordless/KCD.
  • HSF and MSF are Orion's two fabrics, single site elastic vs multicloud consistency.

✓ 2 · Prisma AIRS

  • 5 capabilities: Runtime Firewall, Runtime API, Model Security, Red Teaming, Posture Mgmt.
  • Network Intercept = inline traffic. API Intercept = direct model/app API calls.
  • AI threats: prompt injection, agentic identity impersonation, memory manipulation, tool misuse.
  • Arc: Discover → Protect → Defend.
  • AI Runtime Security is a 5th deployment option alongside PA/VM/CN/Cloud NGFW.

✓ 3 · Interfaces & Zones

  • Modes: tap (copy only), vwire (no IP, transparent), L2, L3, tunnel.
  • A zone needs a type match to its interface(s); traffic can't cross zones without policy.
  • Aggregate = bandwidth/redundancy bundle. Subinterface = 802.1Q tag, own zone possible.
  • Loopback = always up, used for GP gateway, router ID, management services.
  • Zone Protection = per zone floods/recon. DoS Protection = specific src/dst pairs. Packet Buffer Protection = global, per session.

✓ 4 · Routing

  • Legacy virtual router = one object, protocols configured inside it.
  • Advanced Routing Engine = logical router + reusable routing profiles (route maps, prefix lists).
  • AD: static 10 · OSPF int 110/ext 110 · iBGP 200 · eBGP 20 · RIP 120.
  • Path monitoring on a static route/PBF withdraws it if the next hop dies, same idea as HA path monitoring.
  • Redistribution profiles (legacy) vs route maps + prefix lists (ARE) filter what crosses protocols.

✓ 5 · NAT

  • Golden rule: pre NAT address, post NAT zone in security policy.
  • Static 1:1 · dynamic IP · dynamic IP and port (PAT) · U turn for internal to public IP internal.
  • NAT rule evaluation is top down, first match, separate from security rule order.
  • PBF overrides the routing table for policy based path selection; still needs its own health checks.

✓ 6 · Policy & Objects

  • Rule order top down, first match wins. App-ID often needs several packets to finalise.
  • application-default lets App-ID's real ports through; "any" service is a common over permission.
  • Policy Optimizer: rewrite port based rules from observed traffic, not guesswork.
  • EDL = external list, auto refreshed. DAG = tag based, membership changes need no commit.
  • Tag action in a Log Forwarding Profile + DAG + deny rule = auto quarantine in seconds.

✓ 7 · App-ID & Content-ID

  • App-ID sequence: signatures → decryption if needed → decoders → heuristics.
  • Log app values: incomplete, insufficient data, not applicable, unknown-tcp/udp.
  • 7 security profiles: AV, Anti-Spyware, Vulnerability Protection, URL Filtering, File Blocking, WildFire, Data Filtering.
  • Security Profile Group bundles them; applied per rule, not globally.
  • Content-ID = single pass scanning stream, not stored then scanned.

✓ 8 · Certificates & Decryption

  • Forward trust CA on client trust stores; forward untrust CA is never distributed.
  • Subordinate CA from enterprise PKI is the recommended forward proxy pattern, avoids a brand new root.
  • SSH proxy inspects SSH without decrypting content, detects tunnelling/port forward abuse.
  • Decryption Exclusion list for pinning/mutual TLS; Decryption log has the error reason.
  • Decryption broker/mirroring sends cleartext to external tools, a real security control on its own.

✓ 9 · Auth & Identity

  • Stack: server profile → auth profile → auth sequence. Layer order matters.
  • LDAP 389/636 · RADIUS 1812/1813 · TACACS+ 49 (full payload encrypted, device admin).
  • KCD = passwordless Kerberos constrained delegation.
  • User-ID methods: security log monitoring, GlobalProtect, syslog listener, agent/agentless.
  • Redistribution carries IP-User, IP-Tag, User-Tag mappings between firewalls, segments define the propagation path.
  • Cloud Identity Engine centralises identity for both User-ID and GlobalProtect SAML.

57

Fast Recall

Parts 10 to 18 · Everything in the Room, No Explanation

Second half. Same rule: if a line doesn't land instantly, that's the topic to reopen before you close this guide for the last time today.

✓ 10 · VPN & Tunnels

  • IKE Gateway = Phase 1. IPSec Tunnel = Phase 2, runs inside Phase 1's channel.
  • PAN-OS is route based; proxy IDs bridge to a policy based peer's fixed encryption domain.
  • DPD = is the peer alive (IKE). Tunnel monitoring = is traffic actually passing (ICMP, data plane).
  • GRE = encapsulation only, no encryption, pair with IPSec for confidentiality, needed for multicast over a VPN.
  • RFC 8784 = symmetric PQ preshared key. RFC 9370 = extra key exchange (e.g. ML-KEM). Both additive to IKEv2.

✓ 11 · GlobalProtect

  • Portal = hands out config. Gateway = terminates the tunnel and enforces policy.
  • Build order: certs → interfaces → gateway before portal → security policy → client settings/HIP → pilot.
  • Connect: User-Logon (always on) · Pre-Logon (machine cert, before login) · On Demand.
  • Split tunnel by route/domain/app; empty Include list = full tunnel by default, not split.
  • HIP object = one fact checked. HIP profile = objects combined with AND/OR/NOT, used in policy.
  • Clientless VPN: browser only, published web apps, no HIP data at all.

✓ 12 · High Availability

  • HA1 = heartbeats + config sync. HA2 = session state. HA3 = A/A packet forwarding only.
  • HA1 down + HA2 up = split brain precursor. Back up HA1.
  • Election: lower priority wins. Preemption off = no automatic failback (most designs).
  • Link monitoring = is the interface up. Path monitoring = can I reach beyond it (ICMP).
  • A/A: session owner (processes, fixed) vs session setup (first packet, may differ), mismatch forwards via HA3.
  • Upgrade order: suspend passive → upgrade → verify, rejoin → fail over → stabilise → upgrade the other.

✓ 13 · Virtual Systems

  • VSYS = logical firewall on shared hardware, the ASA contexts equivalent.
  • Interfaces belong to one VSYS; zones cannot cross VSYS boundaries.
  • Each VSYS gets its own virtual/logical router, independent, addressing can overlap.
  • External zone = one VSYS to a specific other VSYS. Shared gateway = many VSYS to one common exit.
  • VSYS admins see only their assigned VSYS; resource limits cap sessions/rules/zones per VSYS.

✓ 14 · Web Proxy & Logging

  • Explicit proxy = client aware, PAC file, supports direct proxy auth. Transparent = unaware, relies on User-ID.
  • 10 log types, each independently forwardable via its own match list.
  • Log Forwarding Profile does nothing until attached to a rule/log setting.
  • Tag built in action + waiting DAG + deny rule = the auto quarantine chain, no commit needed.
  • Strata Logging Service = cloud, unsized, subscription. Panorama collectors = you own and size it.

✓ 15 · Panorama & ACC

  • Device group = policy/objects, hierarchical. Template stack = device/network config, higher wins conflicts.
  • Eval order: Pre Rules (unbypassable) → local rules → Post Rules (safety net) → implicit rules.
  • Commit = real on Panorama only. Push = sent to firewalls. Commit without push is the #1 mistake.
  • Access Domains scope an admin to specific device groups/templates, the Panorama scale VSYS admin.
  • ACC = Aggregated Monitoring on Panorama; blank widgets usually mean broken log forwarding, not a bug.
  • 3 root causes behind most Panorama issues: missing assignment, forgotten push, resource constraint.

✓ 16 · Software Updates

  • Content updates (App-ID/Threat/AV/WildFire/URL) are frequent, no reboot, biggest risk is a new App-ID reclassifying live traffic.
  • Mitigate with threshold based deployment + reading release notes first.
  • PAN-OS upgrades cannot skip major versions, follow the published path. Preferred Release ≠ latest.
  • Export a named config snapshot off device before any risky change, per commit history is not a backup strategy.

✓ 17 · Cloud & Orchestration

  • Self managed VM-Series = you operate it. Cloud NGFW = Palo Alto operates it, native marketplace service.
  • Bootstrap: init-cfg.txt mandatory (wins conflicts), bootstrap.xml optional. VM auth key = Panorama auto register.
  • NSX-T: T0 = north south (Active/Standby only). T1 = east west. Notify Group keeps DAGs live.
  • CN-Series: CN-MGMT + CN-NGFW pods, PAN-CNI. A pod annotation turns on inspection, not a route.
  • MSF = 4 pillars (Visibility, Automation, Unified Data Plane, Central Mgmt via SCM). HSF = P-Nodes (fixed, inspect+LB) + S-Nodes (auto scale).
  • XML API = full feature depth. REST API = simpler, resource shaped. Terraform/Ansible sit on top of both.

✓ 18 · Troubleshooting

  • First packet = full rulebase walk + lookups. Every packet after = session table match only.
  • App-ID can revise its verdict mid session, forcing a policy reevaluation, not a bug.
  • Session states: INIT, ACTIVE, CLOSING/CLOSED, DISCARD (still tracked, actively dropping).
  • Capture stages in order: Receive → Firewall → Drop → Transmit. The pattern is the diagnosis.
  • Order: symptom → logs → session table → global counters (delta yes) → capture, always filtered.
  • GUI: click a log value to build the filter. CLI: tail follow yes filter "( ... )" for live.

💡 The six things worth saying without being asked

  • Pre NAT address, post NAT zone: the single most tested NAT/policy detail in the platform.
  • Gateway before portal in every GlobalProtect build, the portal references objects the gateway must already have.
  • Commit ≠ push on Panorama, a committed, unpushed change has changed nothing on any firewall.
  • Tag action + DAG + deny rule is the whole auto quarantine story, and it needs no commit to take effect.
  • Capture stage pattern is the diagnosis: Receive only is upstream, Receive+Drop is a policy decision, all four is clean.
  • A new content update, not a config change, is the first thing to check when something "just stopped working."
